<meta>This page begins the preface of the work, establishing the historical context of previous attempts to edit Greek musical treatises.</meta>

# PREFACE.

Although many have long argued that the Greek doctrines of the musical art <note>original: "musicae artis"</note>, which Meibomius had published in the year 1652, ought to be revised according to the reliability of better manuscripts <note>term: codices (handwritten ancient books)</note>, it has happened through most unfavorable circumstances that they have lain neglected until this day. Johannes Franz indeed, who earned great merit for his editions of Greek inscriptions, had prepared many materials for restoring the musical writers; while staying in Rome from 1833 to 1839, he had either examined or transcribed many manuscripts, undertaken the establishment of the texts of these short books, and completed large portions of the work. However—whether he himself abandoned the undertaking, or whether booksellers were lacking—beyond a small specimen of his labor, a commentary on the Greek musicians (Berlin 1840), he produced nothing from his desks. Yet the care of this man is not to be despised, inasmuch as he transcribed many things word-for-word from the manuscripts and compared those parts of a certain manuscript which, though neglected by others, are of the greatest value for establishing the true readings <margin>W</margin>. After his death (1851), Poetko of Grimma is said to have purchased the gathered materials and intended to publish the doctrines; he even copied out several passages from Fabricius's library, but he published nothing for the public. Now, at the urging of Ritschl, Fran— <note>The text cuts off mid-name, likely referring to the scholar Karl von Jan or a collaborator continuing Franz's work.</note> prepared himself to publish the musicians.

<summary>The author discusses the failed historical attempts by scholars like Johannes Franz and Poetko to produce a modern, accurate edition of ancient Greek musical treatises to replace Meibomius's 1652 version.</summary>
<keywords>Greek music theory, Marcus Meibomius, Johannes Franz, manuscript tradition, Friedrich Ritschl, philology, musicology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, detailing the collaborative efforts and setbacks of various scholars in editing ancient Greek musical treatises.</meta>

# Preface

->VI<-

Franz Buecheler <note>original: "ciscus Buecheler"; a prominent 19th-century German philologist</note> had collected various readings from German manuscripts or ordered them to be collected, and had begun to restore the *Harmonic Art* of Aristoxenus <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum, a pupil of Aristotle and the most important ancient writer on music theory</note>, until, weary of the dryness of the material, he abandoned it. When I learned of this situation from Froehner <note>Wilhelm Fröhner, a scholar and archaeologist</note> some time ago in Paris <note>original: "Lutetiae"</note>, I—being no less a student of the art of music than a friend of Greek literature—requested and obtained from Buecheler that he yield to me whatever had been prepared for publishing these books. With the greatest industry, I then read and re-read these precepts of the art; I was not called away from this task by the terrors of undergoing examinations nor by the duties of holding classes. I was already transcribing the text and readings of Aristoxenus when I heard that the work had been completed by Marquard of Berlin. Upon receiving this news, I left Aristoxenus aside, yet I did not desert the musical writers; rather, I entered into a partnership of labor with men thinking along similar lines. For Hermann Deiters was intending to edit Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of a major treatise on music</note>; Paul Marquard, having finished Aristoxenus (which was published in the year 1868), was to take care of Theon and Nicomachus; and I dedicated myself to the *Introduction* which they used to say was by Euclid <note>The "Introduction to Harmonics" formerly attributed to the famous geometer Euclid</note>. Once that was finished, I intended to take up those remaining doctrines. A most powerful supporter of our society was Wilhelm Studemund, who, just as he had assisted Marquard in editing Aristoxenus, promised that he would assist us with both an abundance of manuscript collations and the authority of his counsel. But it happened to none of us that we could immediately complete what we hoped. For Marquard, troubled by a very serious illness and having set out for warmer climates, met his final day at Messina; Deiters was distracted both by the business of managing a school and by writing the life of Beethoven; I proposed my finished *Introduction* by Cleonides <note>The author to whom the "Euclidian" musical treatise is now attributed</note>, as I call it, to Studemund for his judgment; and when he...

<summary>The author describes the academic "relay race" to edit ancient Greek musical texts, involving a circle of German scholars including Buecheler, Deiters, and Marquard, and the eventual attribution of a musical treatise to Cleonides rather than Euclid.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, music theory, classical philology, Paul Marquard, Hermann Deiters, Wilhelm Studemund, Cleonides, Euclid, history of scholarship</keywords>

# Preface

Page VII

Since he preferred to read printed proofs rather than the drafts I had composed with a running pen <note>original: "currente penna"; meaning written quickly or informally</note>, he hesitated to respond, so that I turned myself to other studies. Theon’s exposition was published in 1878 by Hiller; the art of Aristides appeared in public in 1882 through the work of A. Jahn—but no one has revised the musical doctrines of Nicomachus and Euclid. Consequently, those who wished to purchase these small sections were like people seeking the Sibylline books <note>A reference to the legendary Roman prophetic books; the author implies the texts were rare, expensive, and only available in fragments</note>: they had to pay the price of the whole set just to obtain these meager remains for themselves.

In the year 1880, however, Studemund entrusted certain divisions of the canon <note>The "canon" refers to the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate mathematical musical intervals</note> discovered in Florence to a certain student to be edited, and he permitted me to examine them. When I proposed a new way of interpreting them, he understood that this method should be approved. From that point on, that most discerning man became the promoter and most tireless director of my plans. For whatever readings either he himself or Marquard had noted down from Italy or other libraries, he provided to me with the greatest generosity; he spared neither time nor labor in instructing me with his own skill in restoring the texts. I lament with a pious heart that this excellent man has been snatched from us by death and cannot rejoice with me now that the work is finished. 

And indeed, I was aided by the kindness of other men as well in completing this work. For because the readings of the Marcian "father" and the Vatican "son" <note>The author is using familial metaphors to describe the relationship between manuscript copies (codices)</note> were frequently in disagreement, it seemed necessary to collate the manuscript Marcianus VI 3 once again. However, those who were in charge of the Kingdom of Italy and the libraries, when publicly petitioned, refused to send the manuscript to Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentoratum"</note>. I was released from these difficulties by the kindness of Albrecht; this most renowned man sought a grant from the governor of Alsace, by which I was supported to personally examine the Italian manuscripts. Thus, through the generosity of my superiors, it happened for me that Marquard’s...

<summary>The editor describes the scarcity of previous editions of Euclid and Nicomachus and acknowledges the vital support of scholars like Studemund and Albrecht in accessing Italian manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript collation, history of scholarship, musical theory, Euclid, Nicomachus, Florence, Strasbourg, Vatican manuscripts</keywords>

# Preface

<page-num>VIII</page-num>

->Preface<-

I sought to exhaustively examine the manuscript, to find the archetype of the hymns (designated as V), to identify the source of the common readings (designated as U), and to investigate whether anything useful for my plans existed in Milan, Bologna, or Modena. Then, after I handled the Mersenne manuscript in Paris because of that reference to "Theodorus" <note>original: "Theodoro"; likely a reference to a specific scribe or dedication in the manuscript of Bacchius</note> (Bacchius 101, 8) and looked around for a few days during Pentecost to see if there were other books useful for musicians, I published a precursor to this edition: the *Art of Bacchius* in the 1890 annual program of the Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentinensis"</note> Lyceum. However, so that the Neapolitan manuscripts—from which Bellermann and Studemund had produced quite impressive passages—would not be neglected, I decided that this library also had to be visited. Aided once again by the kindness and generosity of those same superiors mentioned before, I departed for Italy. In Naples, I investigated how much the Bourbon manuscripts were worth for the editing of these musical writers and Ptolemy; I found the Italian companion to the Parisian rhythmic fragment; I also surveyed the Barberini manuscripts in Rome; and from the Vatican, I obtained what was necessary through the mediation of Petersen, who arranged for Mau to examine the material (in the year 1891). Here, my pious thanks must be extended to all those in Italy, France, and Germany who examined parts or passages of manuscripts for me, and to those library directors who assisted me with their work and generosity—especially to Count Soranzo of Venice, a most courteous man, who very quickly dispatched whatever I desired and saw to it that Plate II was produced by a photographer's labor. Nor shall I ever forget that Deiters, with the greatest liberality, lent me what he had transcribed from the Munich manuscripts, while Ruellius, by examining the Parisian manuscript 2532, confirmed and verified what I had determined regarding the manuscripts of Bacchius.

Now that the material of manuscripts and readings has been gathered, the texts of the musical writers themselves had to be edited. But regarding the writers...

<summary>The author details his research journey through major European libraries (Paris, Naples, Rome, Venice) to compile manuscript sources for ancient Greek musical texts, acknowledging the scholars and librarians who assisted him.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript research, Bacchius, Ptolemy, Strasbourg, Naples, Vatican Library, Count Soranzo, musical theory, philology</keywords>

# Preface

The work was delayed by the sheer multitude and abundance of manuscripts, and I was unable to reach the conclusion with a swift pace. For I had to undertake studies in the theory of meter <note>The study of poetic rhythm and structure</note> in order to correctly interpret Bacchius <note>Bacchius Geron, a 4th-century author of a musical catechism</note>; I had to handle astronomy, lest I be deceived by those Neapolitan documents. For a long time, the difficult problems of the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy</note> delayed the work, which must by no means be neglected when dealing with the sources of Greek music. In investigating these, I was led to Aristotle himself, and I do not think you will take it poorly that I undertook to collect what he had thought regarding that art. 

However, if you disapprove of the length of the introductions, I ask you to blame the bookseller; in order to spare your eyes, he kindly expanded into this large format what he could have compressed into a few pages with smaller type. Furthermore, as for my attempt to enumerate all the manuscripts of this art, this was a great undertaking to manage, and I did not hope that I would encompass them all; but for the benefit of posterity, it seemed that some foundations for a widespread work had to be laid, and certain "pigeon-holes" <note>original: "loculi" - categories or filing compartments</note> established, so that whatever neglected material anyone found anywhere, they might contribute it here. (In doing this, I request that whoever describes a manuscript of Ptolemy or Porphyry <note>Claudius Ptolemy and Porphyry of Tyre, key theorists of ancient harmonics</note> should not fail to note the ending.) And in this part of the work, I gratefully declare that I was exceptionally aided by the knowledge and kindness of Bruno Keil <note>A prominent German classical philologist (1859–1916)</note>.

Now let these long-forgotten musicians go forth into the public, and let learned men see whether my labor has any value. Let them judge whether I acted rightly in thinking that the authority of the manuscripts should be protected where I was able, but where I was not—as in the 18th proposition of Euclid—I should rely on my own judgment. In recording the variant readings, I have most scrupulously rendered the account of the words, but in the musical signs or notes—

<summary>The editor explains the interdisciplinary research in astronomy, metrics, and Aristotelian philosophy required to complete this volume, while acknowledging the help of Bruno Keil.</summary>
<keywords>Bacchius, astronomy, Peripatetics, Greek music, Aristotle, Ptolemy, Porphyry, Bruno Keil, Euclid, manuscripts, philology</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion of the preface, following the author's technical explanation of his methodology. The tone shifts from scholarly defense to a personal reflection on mortality and the urgency of publication.</meta>

# Preface

Since the variations in these characters could not always be accurately expressed using lead type <note>original: "plumbeis formis"; referring to the limitations of metal movable type in printing complex scripts</note>, I have refrained from sharing every minute detail once the more significant matters had been compared and collated. Indeed, my opinion on many subjects shifted at various times, and I fear that when you compare certain passages, you may find that not all parts are sufficiently consistent with one another. 

But old age is here; my eyesight is already being clouded by disease, and the end of my labors is imminent. Lest that which befell Franz Poetko and Marquard Studemund <note>Poetko and Studemund were renowned 19th-century classical scholars who died before completing their major works</note> in the undertaking of this work should also happen to me, these affairs must now be brought to a close. This work, prepared by the toil of so many men and overseen by me for so many years, must finally be sent forth. May you receive it with a kindly spirit!

<summary>The author concludes his preface by admitting the limitations of printing technology and his own evolving views, ultimately rushing the work to publication due to his failing health and the fear of leaving it unfinished like his predecessors.</summary>
<keywords>preface, printing technology, scholarly consistency, old age, Franz Poetko, Marquard Studemund, legacy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page begins the discussion of manuscript sources for the musical treatises, establishing the hierarchy of the most important codices found in Venice and the Vatican.</meta>

## Concerning the manuscript codices.

---

Out of the great number of manuscript codices containing musical matters, since two from Venice and one from the Vatican stand out far above the rest in importance—while another certain Vatican manuscript is of the greatest value for establishing the text of Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a 2nd-century mathematician and music theorist</note>—I shall deal with these books in the first part of this dissertation; then, following the alphabetical order of cities, I shall enumerate the remaining ones.

Among the Venetian books, it is helpful to first examine **V**, the one whose likeness you have reproduced in Plate II. Friedrich Bellermann <note>A 19th-century German scholar known for his work on ancient Greek music</note>, when he was editing the hymns of Mesomedes <note>A Greek lyric poet and composer of the 2nd century AD</note>, did not actually see this specific book (1), but through his sharp intellect, he imagined and reconstructed its existence. For he concluded from the order of the verses—which is disturbed in most codices—that there once existed a book in which fifty-two lines of the songs were arranged in two <term>columns</term> <note>original: "selidiis", referring to the vertical columns of text on a page</note> exactly as he described on page 26 of his edition and as you see drawn in this plate.^1 This manuscript, which I have designated as **V** in the sections on Bacchius and Mesomedes (where no Vatican manuscript intervenes), is:

The Venice Marcian Library manuscript, appendix class VI, number 10; made of parchment, written most elegantly in the 12th or 13th century^2 by one and the same hand in such a way that, with the lines always halved, two <term>columns</term> appear on each page. It contains:

---

1 I have demonstrated that the order of the codex differs a little in the title of the second hymn in Fleckeisen’s *Yearbooks for Philology* CXLI (1890) 684.
2 Mittarelli (p. 973, concerning whom see below p. XIV) and Max. Treu (Volkmann’s miscellaneous observations on Plutarch in the acts of the Juraviensis gymnasium annals, 1873, p. 4) attributed it to the 13th century. Studemund, who formerly thought of the 11th century, now says it was written in the latter part of the 12th century. *Breslau Philological Dissertations* I **3** p. 155. (Amsel on the power of rhythms.)

<summary>The author identifies the most significant Greek musical manuscripts, focusing on a specific 12th-13th century Venetian codex (Venetus Marcianus) previously hypothesized by the scholar Friedrich Bellermann.</summary>
<keywords>manuscripts, codicology, Nicomachus, Mesomedes, Friedrich Bellermann, Venice Marcian Library, Greek music theory, paleography</keywords>

# XII
-> On manuscript codices. <-

<margin>V.
(1)</margin> Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*,
Pseudo-Plutarch’s small book *On Music*,
Porphyry’s commentary on Ptolemy’s *Harmonics* (Book I, chapters 1—4),
Aristides Quintilianus’s books *On Music*,
Anonymous works edited by Bellermann in 1841,
Bacchius the Elder,
Dionysius’s *Art* (edited by Bellermann and erroneously attributed to Bacchius),
Trimeter verses (transcribed below on page 284),
Three hymns.

It is not sufficiently clear in what age this book was completed. For the notes regarding the <term>indictions</term> <note>A 15-year cycle used in Roman and Byzantine dating</note> written on the first cover have completely faded away; nor are the words remaining on folio 1 after the title of Ptolemy any clearer: <note>original Greek: οἰκονομίας νϛ || ἰουνίῳ . . . ϛ . . . γύρες ξ.</note> "...of the administration 56 || in June ... 6 ... 60." We know with more certainty who the owners of the book were. For from the last leaf, which contains these words: "To the most devout and all-holy, our master <note>original: αὐθέντῃ, equivalent to the Turkish 'effendi'</note> and father, Lord Seraphim of Phanar," the scholar Sathas determined that the codex once belonged to a certain bishop of the Thessalians. In Italy, it seems first to have been at Mantua in the possession of Victorinus of Feltre <note>Vittorino da Feltre, a famous Renaissance humanist educator</note>. Indeed, Ambrogio Traversari,<sup>1</sup> leader of the Camaldolese monks, relates that when he was traveling to Basel for the church council in 1433, he saw "volumes on music by Claudius Ptolemy and a certain well-learned Quintilianus, as I noticed from the style, and of Bacchius the Elder in the same volume." Mittarelli, in a passage to be discussed later, referred these words to the codex which is now preserved at Venice; even if you do not hold this as certain, you will grant it is at least probable. But before we come to that library in which Mittarelli saw this book, other things must be said which are entirely certain. For on the back of the last leaf, it is written: "In this codex are contained those books which belong to Francesco Barbaro, formerly Lord of Can—

---

<note>1 Letters VIII 50, p. 419. In another letter (51) he writes to Nicolaus that he had obtained permission for that Quintilianus and Bacchius to be transcribed in his (Nicolaus's) name. (ibid. 419.)</note>

<summary>This page describes a specific Greek manuscript containing ancient musical treatises, tracing its physical condition and its ownership from a Greek bishop to Italian Renaissance humanists like Vittorino da Feltre and Francesco Barbaro.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Aristides Quintilianus, Ambrogio Traversari, Francesco Barbaro, Greek music theory, manuscript provenance, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

# Page XIII
## (1) Venice, Library of St. Mark VI 10.

...diani, whom he bought from Giorgio Trevisan." I do not wish to be **v.** <note>abbreviation for "valde" or "videtur," likely meaning "too long-winded"</note> long, nor to speak further of the Trevisans, who collected handwritten books (1) in Padua¹, nor of those Trevisans with whom Barbaro was on intimate terms.² Giorgio Trevisan is said to have lived in Padua and to have been a painter³ around the year 1437. Therefore, the codex <note>a "codex" is a handwritten book in leaf form, rather than a scroll</note> was transferred from Padua to Venice, to a man most fond of all books—especially Greek ones—from the most noble Barbaro family. And that Francis⁴, son of Candiano, was often appointed to great honors for delivering orations, governing cities, and even repelling enemies⁵; finally, he was named Procurator of St. Mark <note>one of the highest administrative positions in the Republic of Venice</note>. This is the family tree:

# XIV

->On manuscript codices.<-

See here Francesco <note>Francesco Barbaro (1390–1454), a Venetian humanist and diplomat</note>, who dedicated a book on marriage <note>original: "de re uxoria"</note> to Lorenzo de' Medici¹ and wrote many letters which have been published in print², marked this codex in his usual manner.³ Three centuries later, that book was in the monastery of St. Michael near Murano. For Mittarelli <note>Giovanni Benedetto Mittarelli (1707–1777), abbot and historian of the Camaldolese order</note>, who in the year 1779 described the codices of this monastery with the greatest care and diligence, says that in entry no. 83 (p. 973) there are contained Ptolemy, Porphyry, Aristides Quintilianus <note>abbreviated as AQ.</note>, "Plutarch on the music of Ptolemy," and Bacchius. Since he adds the notes that Barbaro wrote at the end of the codex, he indicates most clearly that he is speaking of this very same codex. Indeed, seeking by what path the book is thought to have come to Murano, someone might perhaps remember the passage where Giovanni Agostino (p. 41) proclaims that Francesco lent books to the Camaldolese monks with the greatest liberality, from which it happened that many of his books were later found in the Murano monastery—however, beware of applying this to this Ptolemy, which Mittarelli says he himself brought into the monastery library after it was purchased (p. XVII). Moreover, since it was said that books were poorly preserved on that island, the Venetian Council of Ten <note>original: "decemviri Veneti"</note> ordered them to be transferred in the year 1789 to the Marciana Library (Soranzo communicated this to me).

Just as it appears from Bellermann's and my own⁴ labors that this manuscript **V** is the archetype <note>the original source from which others are copied</note> for many books in the hymns of Mesomedes—as you can see clearly from our Plate II—so too in reviewing the text of Bacchius, I have seen that it is the father of many manuscripts. For compare, besides the forms of the musical notes⁵, the words...

## (1) Venetian Manuscript Marcianus VI 10.

<note>The author is discussing specific Greek terms for musical positions</note> *last* and *from the middle* <note>original: "ἔσχατον et ἀπόμεσον"</note> section 71, omits "of which the second" <note>original: "οὗ δεύτερος"</note> 76, has "is woven together" <note>original: "συμπλέκεται"</note> 94, "utterance" <note>original: "ἐκφώνησιν"</note> 98, "one and one-third ratio" <note>original: "ἐπίτριτον"</note> 101, 2 "I have lamented" <note>original: "τεθρήκω"</note> 101, 8. In the diagrams of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, a 3rd-century music theorist</note>, since the nature of the manuscripts appears most clearly, I mention that this one in the first diagram (page 15 of Meibom's edition) omits *alpha* [α] and the symbol ⁜. Since I have discussed the most ancient harmonies² in the *Yearbooks* 1890 p. 683, I pass on to Ptolemy. And the beginning of this in folio 1 has this painted in red lead: † **The first book of the Harmonics of Music by Claudius Ptolemy.** <note>original: "βιβλίον πρῶτον τῶν ἁρμονικῶν τῆς μουσικῆς κλαυδίου πτολεμαίου"</note> Then there were writings concerning the time and origin which cannot be read. Then: † **Table of the Harmonics of the first book of Music.** <note>original: "πίναξ τῶν ἁρμονικῶν τοῦ πρώτου βιβλίου τῆς μουσικῆς"</note> And always with red numbers and beginnings: **1. Concerning the criteria in harmonics.** <note>original: "α. περὶ τῶν ἐν ἁρμονικῆ κριτηρίων"</note> In the arguments of chapters 5 and 6 one reads "Pythagoreans" and "Pythagorean," <note>original: "Πυθαγορίοις et Πυθαγόριος"</note> just as in chapter 9 "Aristoxenians." <note>Followers of Aristoxenus, who favored the ear over mathematical ratios</note> In 10 it has "of a half," in 11 "through eight" <note>the octave</note>, in 14 "nor the other." Following the table of titles is inscribed in red lead: † **Claudius Ptolemy concerning the criteria in harmonics, Book 1.** † p. 1, c (I take the letters from Wallis's edition in quarto, 1682) it omits the word "indeed" <note>original: "μὲν"</note> after "is."

---

With the books faithfully represented, errors easily arose (note 8 in V +'), the form of the 11th note there is round. Although all these things are seen more clearly by the eyes than they are demonstrated by words.

**1** In Aristides Quintilianus Book I, chapter 7, the Venetian manuscript is unique (along with the Mutinensis), in that it displays B before number 26. They write the 24th note as *lambda* [λ] here and in Vatican 192 and the Mutinensis. These have the sign for 30a as ∈ (so also Naples 4), those as ∈, and 30b is written in the same way. In signs 12a, 11b, 12b, the Venetian has right angles E; likewise in 5b Π, while others like U provide rounded forms. Sign 19 in the Venetian is ⊣, in the Vatican and Naples manuscripts it is Y, similarly in the Mutinensis, while in others it is either V or 𝜏.

**2** The signs in V are true: Dorian 7a and Lydian 7b are ⊏, Dorian 8a is ⊐, Dorian 5b, Phrygian 5b, and Ionian 6b are < (this last is ∈ in U), Phrygian 6a and 9a are painted with right angles. Phrygian 3a is a large P. (I use the numbers as Bellermann p. 67).

# XVI

### Concerning the manuscript codices.

<margin>v.</margin> has the symbol ÷ to draw attention to a defined term. <note>original: "ut animos advertat ad rem definitam"</note> *e* has "the" <note>original Greek: τὸ</note>
<margin>(1)</margin> omitted || *g* has "it is that of the one hand." <note>original Greek: ἐστὶ τὸ τοῦ μὲν</note> 2 *k* "of the other" <note>original Greek: τοῦ δὲ</note> | omits the figure. | "proper to perception, while those of reason." <note>original Greek: αἰσθήσεως οἰκεία, τὰ δὲ λόγου</note> after "differences" <note>original Greek: διαφορὰς</note> it has "upon," <note>original Greek: ἐπί</note> but another hand wrote "from" <note>original Greek: ἀπό</note> above it, and in "of those things understood through perception" <note>original Greek: τῶν δι’ αἰσθήσεως νοητῶν</note> it likewise inscribed "the... thing understood." <note>original Greek: τὸ . . νοητὸν</note> On page 39 at "so that" <note>original Greek: ὥς τε</note> *e* is written over an erasure; perhaps it was "the." <note>original Greek: τό</note> It has "neither that of the same" <note>original Greek: μήτε τὸν τῶν αὐτ.</note> in the text, but "the" <note>original Greek: τὴν</note> is written above. Finally, I advise that on page 22 the figure is omitted and the words ($x$) "so that the AG becomes twice through all [octaves]" <note>original Greek: ὥστε τὸ αγ γίνεσθαι δὶς διὰ πασῶν; "through all" is the Greek term for an octave</note> are omitted, from which it appears that Wallis’s Savilian codices (he marks them A and B, I mark them 80 and 81) approach this one most closely. Moreover, the Munich manuscript 215 <note>Monacensis 215</note>, as in the works of Bacchius and Mesomedes, so too in Ptolemy, repeats this model most accurately; they agree in both the inclusion and omission of the iota subscript, in accents (such as "not even"), <note>original: μὴ δέ</note> and in the ÷ signs by which the Venetian manuscript <note>V</note> either indicates definitions (as in line 1) or fills empty spaces (as on page 13, 7 of Wallis's minor edition after "extending through"). <note>original Greek: διήκουσα</note> However, regarding those things we see corrected in V, since the Munich manuscript recognizes part of them (such as "and" <note>καὶ</note> at 8, 21 after "appearing"; <note>φαινόμενον</note> "of those through which" <note>τῶν δι’ ὧν</note> instead of "of that through which" <note>τοῦ δι’ οὗ</note> at 9, 21), but is ignorant of others (such as those inscribed in red lead at 13, 8: "either exceeding in magnitude or in breadth"), <note>original Greek: ἢ μεγέθους ἢ πλάτους ὑπερέχον</note> we are made more certain of the differing ages of the corrections. — It ends after "of those becoming" <note>γινομένων</note> in Book III, chapter 14.

Since I have gathered much concerning the "sons and grandsons" <note>the descendant copies</note> of this manuscript in the edition of Bacchius (Argos 1890, pp. 27–31) and in the *Yearbooks* <note>Jahrbücher</note> cited above, and shall demonstrate other points below when I come to the individual books, I consider it sufficient for now to provide this family tree <term>stemma: a diagram showing the relationship between surviving manuscripts</term>:

<margin>Hand 1.</margin>
<margin>(2)</margin>
Finally, a third hand, whose ink has faded (I believe from the 15th century), added a portion of the second treatise of Bellermann’s Anonymous (§ 29—66), specifically sections 29—57.

On folio 16 of this volume, since it was written by a very ancient hand: <note>original Greek: Ζώσιμος διώρθου ἐν Κωνσταντινουπόλει εὐτυχῶς</note> "Zosimus corrected this in Constantinople, with good fortune," there have been those who thought the codex was actually penned by the hand of Zosimus.¹ But the word "corrected" <note>original Greek: διώρθου</note> by no means indicates that the book was written by him, but rather that it was corrected and brought into a better order. Just as in a certain scholion <note>a marginal explanatory note</note> in the Vatican Codex 176, added after the argument of Book I of Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*, Nicephorus Gregoras is said to have revised <note>original Greek: διωρθώσατο</note> these books. Furthermore, just as the Venice Marciana 322, Ambrosiana E 19 inf., Bologna 2048, and other codices repeat this subscription by Zosimus from the old Marcian manuscript, so too could this manuscript very easily have repeated it from some older book.

In the first gathering <term>quaternion: a group of four folded sheets making eight leaves</term> there are 27—28 lines; in the remaining ones, there are 20. The same scribe wrote with a firm hand, using elegant letters that are easy to read, so that wherever the words are preserved, what he intended can be understood without difficulty. However, part of the parchment has been damaged by moisture (whence folios 2, 6, 10, 11, and 16 appear seized by wrinkles)² and—what is much more serious—the old context of the words has been changed and torn apart by the corrections of many hands. These...

# XVIII
-> Concerning the manuscript codices. <-

<margin>Hand 1. (2)</margin> The "abusers" of the codex <note>The author refers to early scholars or collectors who damaged the manuscript while trying to study it.</note>, since they neither restrained their knives nor abstained from using caustic fluids¹, have destroyed no small portion of the ancient and true lettering. Furthermore, because the inks have faded in many places, and in other places—perhaps due to the pouring of liquid or damage to the surface of the parchment—have turned a dark and almost black color, it is rarely possible to determine anything certain from the distinction of colors². On the contrary, it is more easily demonstrated that corrections of the same color originated from different men³. You might therefore think the matter is better resolved by the specific characteristics of the hands and the style of writing. However, he who undertakes this finds himself falling out of Scylla and into Charybdis <note>A classical idiom meaning to escape one danger only to fall into a worse one.</note>. Indeed, the traces of a certain specific scribe are detected in several places. For sometimes, with a very sharp pen, certain thin little letters seem to be beautifully and neatly drawn (such as *in order* <note>original Greek: ἐφεξῆς</note> 151, 10; 157, 12 and *double* <note>original Greek: διπλάσιος</note> 164, 3, also *sesquitone* <note>original Greek: τριημιτόνιον; a musical interval</note> 187, 8). In other places, letters appear made with a broader pen, but drawn with the same elegance which the primary scribe had used, so that you might say his forms were expressed by this second hand not without skill.

---

1 On leaf 11 (p. 152, 8) after the words *the [letter] theta* <note>original: θ τὸν</note> it is apparent that what followed was deleted by some liquid, and the color of the recent letters was thereby made dark and almost black. Similar things appear on p. 191, where after *interval* <note>original: διάστημα</note> almost two lines are missing. On p. 198, 17 after *but where* <note>original: δὲ οὗ</note> the letters have been wiped away. Also in Nicomachus <note>A mathematician and music theorist, c. 60–120 AD.</note> 252, 20 the last letter of the word *syllable* <note>original: συλλαβάν</note> was deleted by some liquid, and in Bacchius the first sign of the *proslambanomenos* <note>The lowest note in the Greek musical system</note>, which was initially a Z, was changed by the same method. On p. 295, 14 before *seventh* <note>original: ἕβδομον</note> four letters were thus deliberately erased.
2 On leaf 12 of the codex (Euclid 155), the pale color of a recent diagram is easily distinguished from those black letters *k* and *l* <note>original: κ and λ</note>, which the most recent corrector inscribed.
3 For the color has faded both for the one who corrected Euclid's diagrams (Hand 2) and for the one who added the lemmas on p. 186 and wrote *parts of the harmony* <note>original: μερῶν τῆς ἁρμ.</note> etc. on p. 206, 20; no less than the ink of the one who inserted *speed* <note>original: τάχει</note> and a little later *and* <note>original: καὶ</note> in Nicomachus 241, 13, and likewise the one who inscribed the Anonymous precepts in the margins. Conversely, of those things which were added in Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and music theorist, 4th century BC.</note> p. 74, 32 (leaf 51 verso), part (at the edge of the margin) now appears yellow, while part (placed closer to the text) appears black. And of the marginal note <gloss>glossema</gloss> on p. 190, 1, part (*enharmonic* <note>original: ἐναρμ.</note>) is black in color, and part (*genus* <note>original: γένους</note>) is yellow.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion regarding the physical characteristics and scribal hands of the manuscript Venice Marcianus VI 3, specifically addressing the difficulty of distinguishing between different correctors.</meta>

# (2) Venice Marcianus VI 3

...and you may complain that the scripts are distinguished with less ease$^1$ (the same complaint that Marquard already made on pages 13 and 14). However, it is not possible to attribute all corrections to the hands of these two specific scribes; several scripts intervene, and the same scribe is not always consistent in his letterforms. On the contrary, depending on the available space, they sometimes compressed the letters and sometimes spread them out; in other places, having deleted the old text, they added little or nothing, so that you may see nothing characteristic of any specific <term>calligrapher</term> <note>A professional scribe or master of fine handwriting</note>. Marquard, indeed, seemed to himself to have recognized a certain specific hand in many places, but once the <term>apographs</term> <note>Copies made directly from the original manuscript</note> were compared, his judgment was rejected and refuted.$^2$ And although he distinguished six hands, it was even necessary to use a seventh designation, M$^x$, and he confesses that he frequently doubted whether M$^1$ or M$^3$ or another hand had written a passage.$^3$ In order to judge these matters most effectively, it seemed to me that the codex itself had to be consulted; when I inspected and examined it, I too despaired of my own visual judgment amidst such a great variety of hands. But we are rescued from this disaster by the fact that we possess various copies transcribed from M at different times. For around the year 1300, the books attributed to Euclid, as well as Aristoxenus and Alypius, were drawn from here into the manuscript Vatican 191 (from folio 292). Since this copy repeats the words of the Marcian manuscript most faithfully, we have a most reliable witness of what was in that book in the 13th century. And since other manuscripts appear to have been transcribed from this one in the 15th century, we can also clearly see what the condition was in later centuries. Since things were so, I found the doubtful and deceptive nature of the hands and scripts...

---

1 He uses the same form for the letters alpha, eta, nu, and tau (= 7) as the one who first wrote the text. On page 152, line 5, he added <note>original Greek: μέσοι οὔτε</note> "neither the middle ones," at 181, 4 <note>original Greek: καὶ ὀξύτης</note> "and sharpness" and what follows; at 184 he supplied the omitted sounds. At 196, 6 he has <note>original Greek: ἔχει</note> "has." 17 <note>original Greek: δέ</note> "but/and." 198, 9 <note>original Greek: ἐπί</note> "upon." 22 <note>original Greek: πέμπτον</note> "fifth."

2 Marquard attributed <note>original Greek: Μὲν οὖν ἐστι</note> "It is therefore" (181, 4) and <note>original Greek: βαρύτης δὲ τὸ δ. ἀ.</note> "and depth is the..." to the second (old) calligrapher, but several things on pages 184, 185, 186 and others are absent from the Vatican manuscript. However, he attributed <note>original Greek: τριημιτόνιον</note> "trihemitone" (187, 17) to the first scribe. Those letters kappa and lambda supplied in the Euclid proposition 6 he thought were by the second hand, but they are recent.

3 Marquard placed question marks at the variant readings at 156, 23 ff.; 184, 1; 186, 3; 188, 6 and others.

<summary>The author critiques previous attempts by the scholar Marquard to identify specific scribal hands in the Marcian manuscript, arguing that the scripts are too inconsistent for certain attribution. He suggests using later copies (apographs) like Vatican 191 to reconstruct the manuscript's historical state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of manuscript transmission, specifically focusing on the corrections and physical alterations found in the Marcian and Vatican codices.</meta>

# On manuscript codices.

<margin>First Hand (2)</margin> I have set aside the authority <note>of the printed editions</note> and fled toward the more certain testimony of the transcripts <note>apographa: copies made directly from an original manuscript</note>.

I am convinced, moreover, that these changes to the Marcian manuscript were made during various periods.

<margin>Second Hand.</margin> The first scribe corrected very few things himself.<sup>1</sup> However, since he had admitted things foreign to the genius of the authors (as on pages 177 and 190), another scribe later erased part of these—for example, that scholarly note <note>scholium</note> written next to Euclid at 155, 1. Furthermore, in place of the ancient figures which were very poorly drawn in the Marcian archetype itself,<sup>2</sup> I believe a second scribe sought out those figures from another codex<sup>3</sup> which now appear pale and yellow in the Marcian manuscript.<sup>4</sup> The headings <note>lemmata</note> which appear written in a very pale color at the *Introduction* <note>Isagoge</note> 186, and also the scholarly note I recorded on page 197, were perhaps added by the same hand. Yet another scribe, whose ink even now is seen to be black,<sup>5</sup> changed the accent of the word <term>hypatōn</term> <note>original: ὑπάτων to ὑπατῶν; a technical term in Greek music theory regarding the "highest" strings or notes</note>. Indeed, it is clear that the strange variety of tones which strikes the eyes of those reading the Vatican manuscript arose from the fact that this idle harasser of the Marcian manuscript changed the acute accents on the penultimate syllable <note>paroxytona</note> into circumflexes on the last syllable <note>perispomena</note>.<sup>6</sup> Because he intruded nothing more serious here, it was not necessary to distinguish the various hands of the correctors prior to the time of the Vatican transcript; I considered it sufficient to mark them as "Second Hand."

***

1 To this I attribute that <term>r</term> in <term>chrōmati</term> <note>color/chromatic</note> 186, 18; <term>ta</term> 199, 9; <term>hyperiastios</term> 203, 9; <term>melop.</term> <note>melopoeia/composition</note> 205, 2. The word <term>panta</term> <note>all</note> 187, 16 can also be attributed here. Marquard established something similar in section XIII.

Because the scribes struggled miserably over this matter—a third scribe would restore the old accents, and much error of this kind occurred even after the margins were filled—it can now rarely be explained which was the first and true accent. ->(2) Venice Marcianus VI 3.<-

Even in the 14th century, not many things were corrected or changed in the older parts of the manuscript. <margin>Scribe M in the margin</margin> On the contrary, while new doctrines were being inscribed in the margins, <margin>(2)</margin> these spaces were mostly empty and not obstructed by scholarly notes or additions. However, on folio 33 verso, when an excerpt from Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a mathematician and music theorist</note> was being written in, the scribe withdrew somewhat because notes had already been added to the 55th principle of Aristoxenus (page 25 in Marquard's edition), which you may read in Marquard on page 134. These, moreover, were written before the Vatican copy was made. Furthermore, on folio 5, when that phrase "the connected [system]" <note>original: τὸ δὲ συνημμένον</note> (which is absent from the Vatican manuscript up to the word "until") had occupied part of the margin, written in a thin script, the person who was excerpting the *Art* of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of a famous treatise on music</note> kept himself within a narrower space. Although correctors added many things to the <term>isagoge</term> <note>An introductory manual or "Introduction to Music"</note> of Pseudo-Euclid, they never used the space of the margins, because they found it already occupied by the precepts of Aristides.

For in the 14th century, there came a man most stingy with parchment, who inscribed the musical doctrines of three authors in the margins of the manuscript. Since he wrote in an inelegant and uneven hand and used many abbreviations, the parts written by him are not easily read.

In the 15th century, he was followed by those <margin>Scribes M 3-5</margin> who erased the old texts and forced in many new things. To be sure, they added some things that anyone might easily find by their own wit—such as changing the order of parts at the beginning of the <term>isagoge</term>, supplying omitted notes (page 182 and following), or striking out diatonic notes from the <term>oxypycnis</term> <note>A term for the "high-pitched" notes within a dense musical interval</note> (186); nevertheless, because what they add fits perfectly with other manuscripts, it must be believed that they sought those additions from elsewhere.

***

1. In those parts excerpted from Aristides, note that "I said" <note>original: εἶπον</note> is read instead of "sound" <note>original: ἦχον</note> on page 7, 11 M (4, 26 J.).
2. Note 187, 15: "the diatonic does not participate in the <term>pycnum</term>." <note>A <term>pycnum</term> is a "compression" of three notes in ancient Greek music theory.</note> Similar is the judgment of the one who, in Bacchius § 25, denies that a <term>pycnum</term> is admitted in the diatonic genus. However, it was more convenient for the masters of the art to transfer the name of <term>pycnum</term> even into the diatonic genus, and these notes appeared in the old text of the <term>isagoge</term>; compare Bacchius 20, 33.
3. No one would have added those numbers "12, 13" on page 152, 8 after the word "measure" from their own mind; the things thrust in at 190, 9 agree with Bryennius <note>Manuel Bryennius, a Byzantine music theorist</note>, and at 191, 8 with...

# XXII
## Concerning the manuscript codices.

<margin>M³.</margin>
However, regarding the fact that I have marked with the <term>siglum</term> <note>A shorthand symbol used by scholars to identify a specific manuscript.</note> **M³** whatever is repeated in the manuscript **Naples III C 2**, there will perhaps be those who find fault. For although this manuscript is similar and closely related to the **Marcian** codex, there remains some doubt about its origin; and it might perhaps have been better to use the mark **M⁴** to designate things foreign to the **Vatican** manuscript, except for one correction on page 194, line 16, which should be attributed to an earlier period. But having judged the Naples manuscript to be a "son" <note>A direct copy or descendant in the manuscript lineage.</note> of the **Marcian**, and having seen that everything agreeing with **N** reappeared in the codices of **Rhosos** <note>Johannes Rhosos, a famous 15th-century Greek scribe.</note> (manuscript **U** and its descendants), I distinguished, while compiling the variant readings, which appeared to be earlier and which later. Nor do I think it is a bad thing that whatever corrections in **M** match **N** are briefly indicated as **M³**. Furthermore, the greater part of these was written with that sharp-pointed stylus I mentioned above.

<margin>M⁴.</margin>
Moreover, I have marked with the fourth number whatever is found not in **N**, but in **U**, the **Leipzig** manuscript, and **Munich 104**; I shall discuss the relationship of this family below. For when **Bessarion** <note>Cardinal Bessarion (1403–1472), a key figure in bringing Greek manuscripts to Italy.</note> had seen to it that the section of **Euclid**, **Nicomachus**, and **Alypius** were transcribed from **M** into **U**, and all the things I had labeled **M³** were present in this codex, I determined that these—the greater part of which are written in almost the same manner as the most ancient words of the codex (see above, page XVIII)—were inscribed shortly before **Bessarion** and **Rhosos** read them. You can also conclude a great deal from those things which are *not* read in **U**. For example, in a shared erasure on page 152, line 1, we see from the **Vatican** manuscript (see note to 151, 3) that **M** once had the numbers 6, 12, 24. Since, however, they are absent from **U**, it appears they were erased before **Rhosos** performed his transcription. Other things, which now exist in **M** but are absent from **U**, I have marked **M⁵**. And I estimate that during that age (the first or middle part of the 15th century, before **Rhosos** arrived), several scribes were busy altering this manuscript **M**. For I see that the teachings not only of **Euclid**, but also of **Nicomachus** and **Bacchius**, were corrected by several hands. In the section on **Nicomachus** especially, it is apparent that the colors of the corrections are diverse and the letterforms varied; for there are passages in a certain leaden color

---

the codices of **Pappus** (**B**). Therefore, **Marquard** rightly says in chapter XIII that those things were added from other books.

# (2) Venice, Marciana Library VI 3.

The letters were corrected with thinner forms¹, and have been altered with a stronger stroke of the hand in black ink by the fourth scribe (M⁴), so that they approach the hand of the principal copyist.² Just as those scribes had contended—before the Vatican manuscript was completed—concerning the true accent of words (especially <term>enclitics</term> <note>words that "lean on" the preceding word for their accent</note>), so they fought again in the 15th century. Now, because Cardinal Bessarion <note>Basilios Bessarion (1403–1472), a famous Greek scholar and Roman Catholic cardinal who donated his massive library to Venice</note> proclaimed this manuscript (U) to be the best and rarest, some people suspected, not without reason, that those many corrections (M⁴) were inscribed by this most famous man himself. However, after comparing his handwriting in the Marciana manuscript 319, I saw that this must be denied. But where manuscript U had not been examined with sufficient accuracy, I substituted the Leipzig manuscript, which I had seen agreed in most matters with the first hand of the Venetian manuscript. Since that fourth hand also corrected the *Introduction* of Cleonides <note>An ancient Greek author of a treatise on harmonic theory</note>—which they also attribute to Pappus—according to the reliability of a similar book as it did with Euclid, and since the same readings in the Venetian manuscript came from there, you very often see the Venetian or Leipzig manuscripts in agreement with the fourth hand (M⁴) in this little book.

A few things which I saw were foreign to the manuscript of Rhosus³ <note>Joannes Rhosos, a prolific 15th-century Greek scribe</note>, I have marked as M⁵. To these later times, it seems to me, a part of those corrections should also be referred which Marquard <note>Paul Marquard, a 19th-century scholar of Greek music</note> thought originated from the second hand (M²). For there are places where words on the intact <term>parchment</term> <note>original: "membrana"</note> are not corrected by deleting the old writing and adding new, but rather by restoring and strengthening what was already there with a certain black ink. There are very many examples already on the first leaf of the manuscript (such as *haste* 180, 3; *system* 4; *indeed* 13; *in* 18). I do not think these were restored in an ancient age, but rather in a recent one,

2 Page 243, 23 *striking* and 246 *diagonal*, where Marquard thought of the second hand (M²). Compare the additions in Bacchius p. 295, 299, and others. It is clear from Bacchius 295 that the corrector knew the Lydian mode, but was ignorant of the <term>disjunct system</term> <note>original: "synnemmenon systema," a specific arrangement of scales in Greek music theory</note>.

However, since a similar <term>gloss</term> <note>original: "glossema," an explanatory note</note> *in order* at 151, 157, 12 had been inscribed before the time of Rhosus, I do not know whether that *in order* at 151, written in the <margin>margin</margin>, escaped the eyes of the calligrapher and is likewise ancient. From Nicomachus 240, 24 the word *of the voice* should be recalled here, and from 249, 11 *concord*.

# Concerning the Manuscript Codices

<margin>Manuscript M, 5th hand</margin> ...when the letters had faded due to water being poured over them or through old age. For where <margin>(2)</margin> some stain was sprinkled and the color of the parchment became dark, there these very black letters meet the reader. However, since it was clear that these did not differ from the ancient forms, I did not clutter the critical apparatus <note>The "apparatus" is the list of variant readings at the bottom of a scholarly text.</note> with such trivialities.

Finally, the anonymous precepts inscribed in the margin have not been corrected by anyone.

<margin>Manuscript V</margin> **V** and **W** refer to the Vatican Codex 191. It is a cotton-paper manuscript <term>bombycinus: a type of paper made from silk or cotton, common in the later Byzantine period</term> in folio size, from the 13th <margin>(3) or</margin> 14th century. After the writings of mathematicians and others, it contains:

->Gaudentius's *Introduction*, folio 287<-
->Euclid (or Cleonides), folio 292<-

->The names of those who brought the Laws of the Twelve Tables<-
->On the Origin of Constantinople<-

Regarding Gaudentius, see the following page. Marquard has already demonstrated in his work on Aristoxenus (pages 15–23) that the Aristoxenus text in this codex was derived from the Marcian Codex VI, 3. Anyone who compares the *Introduction*—which we attribute to Cleonides—will judge no differently. For the order of the parts is the same there (p. 179, 6); it agrees in the use of "and" <note>original: "τε"</note> in lines 3 and 5; it omits "is" <note>original: "ἐστιν"</note> in line 4; they both have the phrase "composed of things having a certain order" <note>original: "ποίαν τάξιν ἐχόντων συγκείμενον"</note> in line 5, and "concerning pitch" <note>original: "περὶ τόνου"</note> in line 8. It agrees in the word "imperceptibly" <note>original: "ἀφανῶς"</note> at 180, 15; "placing" <note>original: "τιθεῖσα"</note> at line 19; and "the modulations... intervals" <note>original: "τὰς δὲ μεταβάσεις . . . διαστήματα"</note> at line 20. It has "sharpness" <note>original: "ὀξύτης δὲ"</note> at 181, 4, but omits "heaviness [of pitch] is that which results from relaxation" <note>original: "βαρύτης δὲ τὸ δι' ἀνέσεως"</note>. They both have "to be stretched" <note>original: "τετᾶσθαι"</note> in line 6, and "of those touched" <note>original: "καθαπτῶν"</note> in line 8. It omits "but" <note>original: "δὲ"</note> at 182, 4; omits the same sounds at 184, 15 and 185, 8; and they both enumerate the "diatonic notes" <note>original: "diatonos"</note> at 186, 22. Nor do they agree any less in Euclid’s *Division of the Canon*; indeed, the numbers of the propositions and the asterisks which Manuscript M placed, these reappear in V. And regarding the diagrams, V chose those which a second hand had drawn in M; transcribing these with the greatest diligence, he omitted them only where he had recognized that a diagram...

# XXV
-> (2. 3) Mark VI 3. Vatican 191. <-

<margin>V.</margin>
...the earlier sections. However, since remnants of ancient diagrams <note>original: "diagrammatum"</note> had existed in M that were not clearly legible—the purpose of which the scribe was ignorant—these crept into V. To this I refer the numbers 6, 12, and 24 <note>original: "ϛ ιβ κδ"</note> which are detected in V and W, whereas in M they have now been erased (see note to 151, 3). The relationship of the codices also shines forth clearly enough when you have compared the tables of Alypius <note>Alypius was a 4th-century Greek writer on music whose tables provide the key to ancient notation.</note>; for they omit the same sounds (such as the *nete synemmenon* of the hyperphrygian tone) and express very similar forms of signs. Examples include the *psi* and *hemialpha* (371, 13), *phi* and *eta* (19), *hemialpha* (373, 32), and *digamma* (374, 5). However, from the hypoaeolian tone onwards in the enharmonic genus, V omits the final signs and brings only the context of the words to the end of the Marcian manuscript.

Furthermore, the fact that you may read *hypatōn* <note>original: "ὑπάτων" - a musical term referring to the "highest" strings in pitch, though "lowest" in physical position on the lyre.</note> in V in one place (as in 182, 8; 183, 23 f.), and *hypatôn* <note>original: "ὑπατῶν" - the same word with a different accentuation.</note> in another (as in 182, 6 f.; 183, 3), and in a certain place even *hypátôn* <note>original: "ὑπάτῶν" - featuring two accents.</note> (184, 16), you might greatly wonder at this and find no cause, unless you had inspected Codex M. For in that manuscript, since it had been a paroxytone <note>A word with an acute accent on the second-to-last syllable.</note> from the beginning, but was later changed into a perispomenon <note>A word with a circumflex accent on the last syllable.</note> wherever it seemed appropriate, V faithfully expressed this, and once even received two accents. From this, it is clearly demonstrated not only from where V originated, but it is also plainly declared with what great religious devotion the scribe transcribed whatever he had found. Behold a witness of Codex M, than which a richer or firmer one could not be imagined! And since one part of the words corrected in M is recognized by V while the other is ignored, it is clear and evident that those corrections are old (M²), while these others are recent (M³, M⁴). Moreover, in Gaudentius <note>A Greek music theorist, likely 2nd or 3rd century AD.</note>, since the accent of the word *hypatōn* is no more consistent than in the treatises of Euclid (on page 332, 14. 15 Studemund noted that *hypatōn* is written and often repeated, but on page 349, 18 and 352, 6 and 353, 8 he notes the different accent *hypatôn*), I would judge that the scribe sought this part also from that Marcian manuscript, which seems formerly to have been more complete.

<margin>W.</margin>
However, those treatises attributed to Euclid (the *Introduction* and the *Canon*) return in the same codex from folio 397 after the teaching of Diophantus, though drawn from another source. Studemund did not examine this part, so that unless I had used and diligently examined the papers of Franz, these readings would still lie neglected...

# Page XXVI
## Concerning the manuscript codices.

<margin>w. [the best]</margin> Anyone who has accurately inspected the readings of Euclid in manuscript **W** will see that they are very close to the most ancient form of the **Marcianus** manuscript. For whatever remains of the ancient diagrams in **M** <note>The Marcianus manuscript, a primary source</note> reappears in **W**; in Proposition 2, you find the same line directed toward the horizon and inscribed between the same words of the text. Nor do the remains of the diagrams drawn in Proposition 3 differ; and as we see happened in **M**, where the ancient remains were not erased by any force, but rather the small lines by which a large line was divided turned into the letter *iota* [ι] and are read as numbers completely foreign to mathematical logic (14, 17, 19), this appears even more often in **W** (see Propositions 5 and 9). Since in the 4th Proposition both omit the phrase "not a multiple" <note>original: "μὴ πολλαπλάσιον"</note>, and in demonstrating Proposition 6 they omit the same letters and have the same gloss <note>An explanatory note that crept into the main text</note>, and they agree in the readings "the half" <note>original: "τὸν ἥμισυν"</note> (155, 15), "the next-to-middle string" <note>original: "παράμεσον"</note> (164, 16) and others, and in the form of the number 90 <note>The archaic Greek numeral koppa [Ϙ]</note> which turned into a *psi* [ψ] (158, 4), and they both omit "equal to one of them" <note>original: "ἑνὶ αὐτῶν ἴσον"</note> (165, 11), no one can doubt that **W** and **M¹** flowed from one and the same source. Now, since the context of the ancient **Marcianus** could in no way have been expressed according to the model of the lesser **Vaticanus**, the question arises: was this text drawn from that one, or is the origin of both to be traced back to some third source? However, there are not a few things in which they differ, so that the **Vaticanus** cannot be thought of as the "son" of the **Marcianus**. As at the beginning of the book:

| Reading in M¹ | Reference | Reading in W |
|:--- |:---:|:--- |
| "if there were silence" | | "silence being present," |
| "so that since" | 148, 6, | "those at least," |
| "in such" | 149, 14, | "in such words," |
| "I learned" | 151, 9, | "we learned," |
| "means . . . will fall in" | 152, 2, | "a mean . . . will fall in," |
| "but as many as into" | 153, 1, | "but as many as (gap) into," |
| "of the sesquialter and from the sesquitertian" <note>Ratios of 3:2 and 4:3</note> | 154, 16, | "of the sesquitertian part and the sesquialter." |

But if on page 149, line 1, you see an entire sentence omitted in **M¹** but written in **W**, you will easily concede that either **W** itself or its archetype <note>The original lost manuscript from which copies are made</note> proceeded not from **M** itself, but from some relative of it. Since these things are so, although **W** was written quite carelessly and suffers from not a few errors, its authority is nevertheless of the greatest importance; and wherever the ancient [readings] from **M**...

# XXVII
-> (3) Vat. 191. <-

reading is removed, the correction must be sought from the authority of this manuscript. **W.**
For instance, on page 152, line 9, where all the remaining books, by inserting the word *consequently* <note>original: "ἄρα"</note>, made Euclid conclude exactly what he had placed in the hypothesis (that line *δζ* is a superparticular <term>superparticular: a ratio where the numerator is one greater than the denominator, like 3:2</term> of line *θ*), **W** alone says *since* <note>original: "ἐπεὶ"</note>, so that Euclid may argue correctly.

Since its authority in restoring the words of Euclid is so great, I was unwilling to rest content with Franz’s collation. Because I myself was unable to visit the Vatican library in the month of August, Petersen, the director of the German Institute, persuaded Mau—a most learned man and very skillful in this work—to compare the section of Euclid with this codex <term>codex: an ancient manuscript in book form</term> once again. One thing is to be regretted in this matter: that **W** omits propositions <note>original: "protases"</note> 11–18.

In the introduction <note>original: "isagoga"</note>, however, which was usually transcribed together with the *Division of the Canon* <note>Euclid's "Sectio Canonis," a work on the mathematics of music</note>, I often saw this **W** agreeing with Meibom’s common manuscripts<gloss>1</gloss>, so that from the beginning I did not value it so highly. Nevertheless, there were places where it agreed<gloss>2</gloss> with **M** <note>The Marcian manuscript</note>. Finally, when I saw this manuscript departing from **M** to offer us the best readings, and even in its errors being recommended by the dignity of its related sources, I understood that even in the introduction these readings must be valued very highly. For compare that word *both* <note>original: "ἀμφοῖν"</note>, which is read in all books at 181, 6; in **W** alone it is correctly referred back to *tension* <note>original: "τάσιν"</note> and *pitch* <note>original: "φθόγγον"</note>, and *where it is first placed* <note>original: "οὗ πρῶτον .. κεῖται"</note> at 197, 1; and see this source agreeing with **N B** at 190, 9; 203, 11; 206, 20. Since these things are so, we shall say that this **W** once flowed from the same archetype <term>archetype: the lost common ancestor of a group of manuscripts</term> as the rest of the sources, but stands out for the dignity and excellence of its readings. Therefore, everyone will surely praise the fact that I have always regarded the readings of **M** and **W**, where they agreed, if not as the true ones, then at least as the most ancient and most approved by the authority of the codices; there will perhaps be those who would attribute even more authority to this Vatican manuscript than to the Marcian—

## Concerning the manuscript books.

<margin>w.</margin>
<margin>(3)</margin> ...while they contend. Just as manuscript M struggled with the <term>scholion</term><note>A marginal note or explanatory comment made by an ancient scholar</note>, so too is manuscript W not entirely free from error. $^1$ We shall say that Rhosus <note>Joannes Rhosus, a famous 15th-century Greek scribe</note> drew that <term>isagoge</term><note>original: "isagogen" - an introductory treatise or manual</note> from a codex which most closely approached this W $^2$; however, we will deny that he used W itself, because he includes passages (194, 7–9) which W omits.

The following scholars examined manuscript V (folios 287–96, 309–313): Studemund; (folios 292–96, 314–19, 397 ff.): Franz; (400 ff.): Mau.

<margin>R.</margin>
<margin>(4)</margin> **R** = Vatican Manuscript 198. Paper, in quarto. Early 15th or late 14th century. It contains works by Ptolemy, Porphyry (<gloss>Commentary on books 1 and 2 of Ptolemy's Harmonics</gloss>), Nicomachus, and Bryennius. — Although this manuscript appears younger in age than the Marcian manuscript, it is superior in its readings. Compare 237, 8 <gloss>that which concerns the ones</gloss>, 12 <gloss>with thought</gloss>, 241, 9 <gloss>volumes</gloss>, 248, 17 <gloss>unchanging</gloss>, 258, 12 <gloss>successively from above</gloss>, which it alone displays; and 242, 11 <gloss>concerning</gloss>, 253, 18 <gloss>long ago</gloss>, 256, 20 <gloss>schema</gloss> where it agrees with manuscript B. Also 249, 11, where others denied that the phrase <gloss>the... system</gloss> could be supported, this manuscript maintains the true reading, aided by Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher whose citations often help verify corrupt musical texts</note> who frequently agrees with it (247, 12; 248, 14, etc.). Nevertheless, since it also suffers from errors (236, 17; 238, 12, 19; 239, 21, etc.), I could not trace everything back to the authority of this one manuscript alone. A second scribe (R$^2$) corrected it at 246, 1; 247, 10 (<gloss>in double — weight</gloss>); 252, 2; 256; 259, 5, 9. To the same family belong Naples III C 3 and Paris 2531. Franz and Studemund examined this.

Although we know little of the remaining manuscripts—especially those preserved in Britain—I shall nevertheless gather here whatever I have found concerning them. I have arranged the order according to the names of the cities.

Those which had been at **Augsburg** <note>original: "Augustae Vindelicorum"</note> have been transferred to Munich. (See below, 68, 69.)

# XXIX
### (5—6) Arg. — Bern.

In the academy at Basel. F V 39. Paper. 16th Century. Ptolemy. (5^b) (ending at Book III, chapter 14 <note>original: "διὰ τῶν γενομένων" (through those things which have happened)</note> as in the 1st Venice manuscript). Formerly belonging to Faesch. Omont, *Swiss Libraries*, p. 28.

**B** = Bernensis 639. Paper in 16mo <note>A small book size where the sheet is folded into 16 leaves.</note>. 16th Century. Contains Books I and II of Nicomachus, and as can be gathered from this matter (Book II), it is very closely related to **M**, with which it shared a certain common archetype <note>The original source manuscript from which others are copied.</note> (see pages 236, 237 line 12, 238 ff.), but from the third chapter of the excerpts they disagree, and **B** seems to be the better version. It offers good readings at 237, 11; 239, 1; 240, 21 (and the word "lowest" <note>original: "κατωτάτω"</note> at 241, 21 is not to be despised), especially at 265, 4, and it agrees with Iamblichus at 248, 9 and 13; 249, 11. Although occasionally the scribe seems to have made corrections by his own wit (249, 11 "the" [neuter] instead of "the" [feminine] <note>original: "τὸ pro ἡ"</note>; 250, 10). A second hand corrected 241, 9; 243, 12; 251, 10, 15 and others. This manuscript **B** seems fuller than the rest because it includes diagrams; for it has a table of the planets in chapter 3, the forms of the triangle and the monochord <note>A single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals.</note> in chapter 4, the scale of the eight-stringed lyre in chapter 5, and the "unchanging system" <note>The standard Greek musical scale structure.</note> in chapter 11. However, since I had seen the tones of the Christian Church enumerated in these diagrams and had read a rule added to the monochord stating: "this is called a *tambouras* among the Turks" <note>original: "τοῦτο παρὰ τοῖς Τούρκοις καλεῖται ταμπουρᾶς"</note>, I decided not to share them, thinking they contributed nothing to the art of the ancients. Indeed, almost all those things reappear in the teaching of Pachymeres¹, from where the scribe seems to have inserted them here. — Collated by Studemund.

---

1 You have those diagrams printed in Vincent, *Notes and Extracts of Manuscripts* XVI 2. Paris 1847. p. 408, 410, 416. The first table shows "the daily movements and the daily assumptions of the seven planets" <note>original: "τὰ ἡμερήσια κινήματα καὶ τὰς ἡμερησίας ὑπολήψεις τῶν ἑπτὰ πλανωμένων"</note> = Vincent 408 above (omitting "the reverse" <note>original: "τὸ ἀνάπαλιν"</note>. — cf. Bryennius 411). The third diagram = Vincent 410. But it omits "of the thrice-greatest" <note>original: "τριςμεγίστου"</note>, falsely repeats "of the highest strings" <note>original: "ὑπατῶν"</note> instead of "of the lowest strings" <note>original: "νητῶν"</note>, omits above "which is also called the index finger string" <note>original: "ἡ καὶ λιχανὸς λέγεται"</note>, and encompasses the fifth and the fourth <note>The musical intervals of a perfect fifth and perfect fourth.</note> in one arc which he labeled "through all" <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" (the octave)</note>. Below it has the intervals: tone, half-tone, tone, tone, half-tone, tone, tone. and adds at the bottom to the line for the *parypates* "the hyperparypate and lichanos," to the *mese* line "the paramese, the one inserted between," then with a semitone placed between he writes at the *paramese* line "the third, formerly the paramese." Moreover, I see this order of intervals, which we compare to the scale **a — a'** or *la — la'*, returning in the work of Nicephorus Gregoras (Vincent 284 f.). — You have the fourth diagram designated in Vincent's *Notes* p. 416. The "unchanging system" is described there.

# On the manuscript codices.

(7) &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Berlin Phillippicus 1552.** Paper, small folio, 16th century. Contains Aristotle's *Problems* XIX 1–10, Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, *The Art of Harmonics* <note>original: "Arx." - likely an abbreviation for 'Ars Harmonica' or 'Harmonica Elementa'</note><sup>1</sup>, Nicomachus books II, Bryennius, (Euclid).<sup>2</sup> I inspected this in 1892 and found it very similar to codex **U**; in the Pseudo-Euclid section, it comes very close to **Paris 2456**. These Phillippicus codices, collected by Guillaume Pellicier, Bishop of Montpellier, brought fame to the library of Claude Naulot of Burgundy, then to the Jesuit College in Paris, then to Meerman of Holland, and finally to Thomas Phillipps of Britain; they have now found a permanent home in Berlin. See Studemund-Cohn, *Meerman Codices* (Berlin 1890), preface and p. 65.

(8) &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Berlin Phillippicus 1553.** Paper, 16th century. Ptolemy, Porphyry, and others.

(9) &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ,, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ,, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **1554.** &nbsp; ,, &nbsp; ,, &nbsp; ,, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Ptolemy, Porphyry, Aristides Quintilianus.

(10<sup>a</sup>) &nbsp;&nbsp; ,, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ,, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **1555.** &nbsp; ,, &nbsp; ,, &nbsp; ,, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Aristides Quintilianus, Anonymous [writer] 2, Bacchius, Pseudo-Bacchius (see p. 454), hymns, and others.

In the table of Aristides I 7 and those which follow there, this manuscript agrees with **V**. In Bacchius, it omits the same parts as **Munich 215**: the letters CC in § 16, three lines in 47, the same number in 70 ff., and the word "what" <note>original: "τί"</note> in 97. However, when you compare it with **Paris 2532**, you will see it differs in many respects. For **P** <note>The Paris manuscript</note> omits "what are these" in § 13, "semitone" in 23, "which of these" in 44; **P** has "tone" instead of "ditone" in 22 and "melody" instead of "end" in 61, whereas **B** <note>The Berlin manuscript</note> disagrees. From this, I conclude it was transcribed from the Munich manuscript, and certainly not from the Paris one. The same is apparent in the hymns. For it clearly harmonizes with the **Munich** manuscript<sup>3</sup> both in the forms of the words (such as *d' hēlie*, *rhodoessan*, *apeirēton*, *phomēdi* <note>Greek poetic forms: "O Sun," "rosy," "unbounded," etc.</note> I 8. II 8. 11. 20) and in the order of the verses (which follow on the left column I 1. 3. 5. 7). Yet from **Paris 2532** it is far removed.

---

...which corresponds to our sounds ***A*** — ***a'*** (***La***<sub>1</sub> — ***la***<sub>3</sub>). From this are cut the eight scales of the harmonies <note>The Greek "tonoi" or modes</note> from the Hypodorian to the Hypermixolydian, of which the Dorian is ***d*** — ***d'*** or ***re*** — ***re***, the Phrygian ***e*** — ***e'*** or ***mi*** — ***mi***, the Lydian ***f*** — ***f'*** or ***fa*** — ***fa***. Behold the church tones! However, the errors in **B** are very numerous. I consider it sufficient to note that, because the Hypophrygian, Dorian, and Hypermixolydian harmonies were omitted, not eight octaves <note>original: "diapason"</note> are numbered, but five.

# (7—11) Berlin — Bologna

it departs, having a worse order of verses (so that I 1. (10ᵃ) follows 5. 9) and a smaller number of musical notes (five) in I 5 (B has six signs). Since, however, in other verses P has more musical signs (it has eleven in II 9, where B has three; thirteen in II 11, where B has eleven), you can see that neither depends on the other. Nevertheless, B is closely related to the Paris manuscript 2458; it has five musical sounds inscribed in I 5, two in II 13, and a few in II 9 and 11. They share the forms <term>bacchius</term> <note>original: βακχαῖος; a metrical foot</note>, <term>rosy</term> <note>original: ῥοδόεσαν</note>, <term>of times</term> <note>original: χρόνων</note> II 22, <term>rejoices</term> <note>original: γάννυται</note>, <term>having</term> <note>original: ἔχουσα</note> III 5, and <term>measures</term> <note>original: μέτρα</note> (instead of <term>you measure</term> <note>original: μετρεῖς</note> III 11). But in three verses, the Berlin manuscript exhibits more notes (II 7 <note>the letter rho</note> ρ, II 9 <note>the letter c</note> c, II 11 two <note>the letter iota</note> ι). You see, therefore, that this Paris manuscript is thinner, so that the Berlin manuscript could not have originated from it. However, since the latter is fuller, yet equal to the Paris manuscript in many errors, I think that P was transcribed from B. I inspected these in 1890. Concerning all these, compare Studemund-Cohn p. 65.
**Berlin Phillips 1578.** Paper, 16th Century. Ptolemy, Bryennius, Porphyry complete. (10ᵇ) — Studemund-Cohn 77.

**Bologna 2048**, five paper volumes, (11) 16th Century. It formerly belonged to Joannes Cingarus. Amsel and Studemund report on this in the *Vratislav Acts* I 3, p. 124. Volume 1 contains Bryennius, Plutarch, Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, and Book I of Aristoxenus. The order of the books is the same as in U <note>referring to the Marcianus manuscript</note>, and when I compared the *Division of the Canon* <note>a mathematical music treatise</note> with the Bologna manuscript in 1889, I found everything to correspond accurately to it, even the signature of Zosimus on p. 166; from this, you may easily conclude that it was copied from U. On page 164, when Rhosus <note>Joannes Rhosus, a famous 15th-century scribe</note> had wandered from the words <term>again since</term> <note>original: πάλιν ἐπεὶ</note> in line 3 into the same words in line 4 and had corrected the error in the margin, this scribe had already begun to write what follows, <term>multiple...</term> <note>original: πολλαπλά</note>, when, having found the error, he supplied lines 3—4. However, regarding the things corrected or added in U, B did not see all of them as corrected (it recognizes emendations 1 3 5 7, but ignores 2 4); therefore, you might say it was finished after L., but before Mon. — Volume II contains Aristoxenus II and III, Alypius, Gaudentius, Nicomachus, and Aristides Quintilianus. And in Gaudentius it omits <term>and</term> <note>original: καί</note> 327, 10; it has headings painted in red lead, <term>but according to</term> <note>original: κατὰ δὲ</note> 329, 14, <term>parts</term> <note>original: μέρη</note> 15, <term>indeed</term> <note>original: μὲν δὴ</note> 331, 24, <term>of the highest strings</term> <note>original: ὑπάτων</note> 332, 14, <term>it has departed</term> <note>original: ἀπεῖκεν</note> 333, 17, all in agreement with U. But where U corrected something, here B recognizes it; it was therefore written after the Ambrosian manuscript, which ignores the corrections. Nicomachus...
