# Page XXXII
## On the manuscript codices.

-> <note>original: "De codicibus manuscriptis"</note> <-

I have seen that the first pages, when compared, were transcribed from **U** <note>The translator refers to a specific manuscript labeled 'U' in his classification system</note>; for the Bologna manuscript contains two books: *Pythagorean [matters]*, and a *Manual of Harmonics*. It shares specific readings with **U** at page 236, line 7 [of the reference edition], such as "planets"; it includes "the" at line 10 but omits "the" at line 11. It has "were found" at line 12, and "and" at line 19. At page 237, line 8, it reads "in the commentary among those"; at line 12 "burden" (where **U** has a small 'n' written above the word); at line 14 "undistracted"; and at page 238, line 19 "of the teacher." At page 239, line 1, it reads "divided and standing apart." It shares many features with **M** and **U**, but specifically matches **U** in the title *Manual*, the word "found," and the spelling of "burden."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since Studemund <note>Wilhelm Studemund (1843–1889), a noted German philologist</note> compared the section on Aristides [Quintilianus], and various readings exist in Amsel’s *On the Force of Rhythms* (Breslau 1887, page 128, marked **Z**), I see that this manuscript agrees with the Leipzig manuscript in these terms: "philosophers," "learning," "precious," "nearly," "and that of the art," "of painting," "establishing," "proceeding," "wise," "parts of the panacea," "alone," and "these things." Thus, it is clear from this point that they are very closely related and similar. Furthermore, what I determined regarding this manuscript's version of Euclid, Gaudentius, and Nicomachus, I have no doubt in determining for Aristides as well; indeed, I am persuaded that this is a "son" <note>A direct copy or descendant in the manuscript family tree</note> of the Venice manuscript 322, based also on the diagrams of Aristides which I have noted down. They agree in the order of the scales on page 15 of Meibom’s edition, and no less in the harmonies of the most ancient authors on page 22. For the arrangement of the lines is the same, the form of the musical signs is the same, and they show and omit the same things—except that sign 10 in the Bologna manuscript seems to come a little closer to the letter *iota*, sign 15 to the number 6, and sign 22 is a *Y*, but 23 is a *C* as in **U**. Sign 29 is a *C*, and the Bologna manuscript omits sign 33. In the fourth line, the Bologna manuscript does not have everything that **U** has, but moved some things to the 5th line; nevertheless, they join the same elements and separate the same with spaces. The third sign before the end in the Bologna manuscript gives a 7; the rest correspond accurately. — Volume V contains Ptolemy and Porphyry. I compared the parts from Amsel-Studemund 124 in the year 1889.

(12) &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Bologna 2432**, paper, folio. Bruno Keil discovered this and described it to me. However, because I hope that Italian scholars will also describe it in the coming days, I am deferring it to the supplements.
(13) &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In **Cambridge**, at Emmanuel College, a manuscript is said to exist according to Fabricius’s *Library*, volume III, page 635. Jahn <note>Otto Jahn, a 19th-century archaeologist and philologist</note> is silent on this.
(14) &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At **Coventry**, there was a certain "ancient" codex which Langbaine examined so that Meibom could use it for publishing Euclid. The text is not far removed from **U**; the gaps are the same as in Pena’s edition of Euclid, page 158 line 17 and page 160 line 7.

# XXXIII
### (11—16) Bologna — Escorial
<sig>c</sig>

**Escorialensis R I 17** (Miller Catalog 17) paper, folio (15<sup>a</sup>) small. 16th Century. Contains **Aristoxenus** <note>A Greek philosopher and the most important ancient writer on music theory</note>. Ruelle in *Archives of the Missions* III, II 517.

**Escorial Σ I 16** (Miller 72). paper, folio. 16th Century. AQ., (15<sup>b</sup>) (in another hand) **Bryennius** <note>Manuel Bryennius, a late Byzantine music theorist</note>.

**Escorial Υ I 9** (Miller 245). paper, folio. 16th Century. Various **Physics** (15<sup>c</sup>) <note>Natural philosophy</note>. Letters of **Psellus** <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine polymath</note> (cf. below chapter 19 and Ruelle *Archives* 558).

**Escorial Υ I 13** (Miller 249. Ruelle *Archives* 530). paper (16<sup>a</sup>) folio. 16th Century. Contains the **Hormasia** <note>A technical term for a musical arrangement or "setting in order"</note>, parts of **Theon** and **Bacchius**, Pseudo-Psellus, and other fragments.

You will find the **Hormasia** below on page 421. There follows the rule of the **Hypolydian mode**, regarding which see the Munich manuscript (Mon. 104). From Theon and Bacchius, this manuscript contains the same material as Munich 104 and Vatican 192. The errors of the Munich manuscript also reappear in the Escorial manuscript (such as "to all" <note>original: πᾶσιν</note> 87, 17, Hiller; "divided from both" <note>original: ἐξ ἑκατέρων διαιρουμένη</note> 89, 12; "for" <note>original: γὰρ</note> 18; "upon" <note>original: ἐπὶ</note> 91, 1; "being" <note>original: οὔσης</note> 92, 24). The Escorial manuscript also omits what the Munich manuscript omitted. However, the Escorial manuscript wanders further from the truth in 92, 27 with "it might become" <note>original: γένοιτο; Munich reads: γένοι τὸ</note> and it omits a portion of the fragments of Bacchius (concerning the **Hypophrygian** and **Hypodorian** modes, for it strayed from the "four-tone" <note>original: τετρατόνῳ</note>). It contains two lines, whereas the Munich manuscript has five (regarding the sounds and signs which end the symphonies <note>consonances</note> § 6 Ruelle § 11 Bacchius). All these things from the *Hormasia* onward seem to me to be taken from Munich 104, which itself drew from Vatican 192. To these, the Escorial manuscript adds things sought from elsewhere. For there follows the Anonymous author § 103 (Ruelle 543) inscribed **"How one must understand the orders of differences,"** then this diagram, which brings to our mind the Neapolitan excerpts § 19:

->**Tone**<-
->**Semitone** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Semitone**<-
->**Quarter-tone** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Quarter-tone** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Quarter-tone** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Quarter-tone**<-
<note>The original Greek terms are: τόνος (tonos), ἡμιτόνιον (hemitonion), and δίεσις (diesis)</note>
->64 &nbsp; 65 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 66 &nbsp; 67 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 68 &nbsp; 69 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 70 &nbsp; 71 &nbsp; 72<-
<note>These are Greek alphabetic numerals: ξδ ξε ξς ξζ ξη ξθ ο οα οβ</note>

There follows **"the linear exposition of the entire system of the 18 notes"** (Ruelle 544 cf. below p. 423) *and* **"on the eight-stringed lyre the first and lowest note is called the Hypate, and the last and highest is the Nete."** The procession is closed by a diagram of the genera <note>the types of scales: diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic</note>, which you have in Theon p. 56 H., and a definition of sound (Ruelle 545).

# XXXIV
-> Concerning manuscript codices. <-

<margin>(16ᵃ)</margin> The scribe also included in his work the so-called *quadrivium* <note>The four liberal arts: arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</note> of Psellus. It contains the section on the art of arithmetic on folio 205, geometry on 113, music on 213, and the doctrine of the stars <note>astronomy</note> on 128. <margin>(17)</margin> Escorial Φ II 5 (Miller 199). Paper, folio, 16th century. Contains Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, Nicomachus, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius. Ruelle, in *Archives* 519, considers it closely related to the codices of Meibomius.

<margin>(18)</margin> Escorial Φ II 21 (Miller 215. Ruelle 522). Paper, folio, 16th century. Written by Jacob Diassorinus. Contains Aristoxenus.

<margin>(19)</margin> Escorial Φ III 1 (Miller 217. Ruelle 546). Paper, quarto, 16th century. Contains a letter of Psellus addressed to the Emperor (titled <note>original: "Πότερον ὡς αὐλητής"</note> "Whether as a flute-player," edited by Ruelle, page 612), the generation of the soul and whatever Linder published at Uppsala in 1854; see also the additions by Ruelle, page 548; the letter <note>original: "Ἐπίλυσις πῶς ἐστι τὸ διὰ πέντε ἡμιόλιον"</note> "Explanation of how the fifth is a sesquialter ratio" (Ruelle edition, 614), and <note>original: "Προβαλλόμενα εἰς τὴν ῥυθμικὴν ἐπιστήμην"</note> "Problems concerning the science of rhythm" (edited by Caesar in *Rhenish Museum* new series I, 1842, page 621, from which Westphal took material for *Fragments and Principles* 74 and *Metrics*² volume I appendix 3). See below, manuscripts 179 and 64.

<margin>(20)</margin> Escorial Χ I 12 (Miller 351. Ruelle 523). Paper, folio, 16th century. Aristides Quintilianus, Bryennius, Pseudo-Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius, Nicomachus, Ptolemy, Porphyry in books I and II. The readings cited by Ruelle are not far removed from Meibomius's Oxford manuscripts. Ruelle considers this very close to the Barocci manuscript, but he saw a fuller version in the introduction of Alypius 368, 9. The commentary on Ptolemy book I, chapter 5 has the title: "Also by the same author on the fifth chapter." At the end, it displays a diagram of the <note>original: "τοῦ ἐναρμονίου γένους μελῴδησις"</note> "melody of the enharmonic genus." It formerly belonged to Mendoza.

<margin>(21)</margin> Escorial Ω IV 4 (Miller 552. Ruelle 511). Paper, quarto, 16th century (partly by Darmarius). Theon of Alexandria (or Porphyry?) on Ptolemy I, 5–15. Theon of Smyrna’s exposition on arithmetic and music¹, and Theon of Alexandria on the Ptolemaic canons. Regarding the author of the first work, see below page 116 and Ruelle 512; he observed here (515) that the readings closely approach Wallis's manuscript E.

1 By "exposition on arithmetic" I mean that which Hiller presents on pages 1–46, 19; the musical portion you will find in the same work, pages 46–119.

## (16—26) Escorial — Florence

<margin>(22)</margin> In Florence, there are preserved in the Laurentian Library:
Laurentian shelf 28, manuscript 11. Parchment <note>original: "membr." for membranaceus</note>, folio size, 15th century. Contains Bryennius <note>Manuel Bryennius, a Byzantine music theorist</note>, Psellus *On the Generation of the Soul* (beginning: "That which is said is...") <note>original Greek: τὸ μὲν λεγόμενόν ἐστι</note> edited by Vincent, Note 316. Linder 1. — Bandini, *Catalog of Greek Manuscripts in the Laurentian Library*, volume II, pages 20 and 472.

<margin>(23)</margin> Laurentian shelf 28, manuscript 12. Cotton paper <note>original: "bomb." for bombycinus</note>, small quarto, 14th century. Theo of Smyrna *On Mathematics*. — Bandini, same work, page 21.

<margin>(24)</margin> Laurentian shelf 56, manuscript 1. Cotton paper, folio size, 13th century. Excerpts of Menander the Rhetorician. *The Divisions of the Canon* <note>original Greek: Κατατομαὶ κανόνος</note>, book 3, edited by Ad. Stamm in Studemund and Schöll’s *Various Anecdotes* (Berlin, 1886). — Bandini, same work, page 289.

<margin>(25ᵃ)</margin> Laurentian shelf 57, manuscript 34. Paper, small quarto, 15th–16th century. Contains short works by Libanius, Planudes, Choeroboscus, and others; on folio 190, *An Accurate Synopsis of Music*, beginning: "Music is the science concerning quantity..." <note>original Greek: Μουσική ἐστιν ἐπιστήμη περὶ ποσόν</note> and ending: "...the myriad and diverse melodies are constructed." There are four synopses of the mathematical arts <term>Quadrivium: the four subjects of higher education—arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</term> attributed to no author in the manuscript. Bandini says they are by Psellus, and this is the conclusion of that short work which Xylander and others¹ edited as if it were by Psellus. However, the beginning of that work is: "The ancients said that music holds things together."² They differ in the beginning and end regarding geometry, but agree regarding astronomy. Compare these with numbers 16 and 135. — Bandini, page 390.

Regarding 25ᵇᶜ, see the additions below.

<margin>(26)</margin> Laurentian shelf 58, manuscript 29. Paper, octavo, 15th century. Contains Planudes *On the Construction of Verbs*, adages, Psellus *On the Generation of the Soul* (see above, no. 19), Bryennius, Psellus’s commentary *In part you have asked me* <note>original Greek: Ἐκ μέρους ἠρώτηκάς με</note> (edited by Linder, Uppsala 1854, p. 50), another commentary *You love the Platonic spring insatiably* <note>original Greek: Ἐρᾶς τῆς Πλατωνικῆς ἀκορέστως πηγῆς</note> (Linder 54), and a fuller commentary on the "remainder" <note>a musical interval</term>: *What exactly is the remainder?* <note>original Greek: Τί ποτέ ἐστι λεῖμμα</note> (below, no. 179). — Ptolemy up to book III, chapter 9 (ending: "and for the rest...")

# (26—41) Florence — Hamburg XXXVII

... **Florence, Riccardianus 41 (K II 2).** Paper, 16th century. Designated as **R**. (36)
It contains Theophrastus’s *Characters*, Rhetorical Problems, and a poem by Planudes; then it contains whatever is found in the Naples manuscript III C 2, from Hippocrates to Barlaam’s *Logistics* <note>A work on arithmetic and calculation</note>; there follows Aristides Quintilianus <note>Abbreviated as AQ. in the text</note>, an anonymous author (titled Aristides), and the allegories of Heraclides Ponticus. The titles, along with the initial letters, were omitted by the first scribe and added by a later hand. In the middle section, which I believe was copied from the Naples manuscript III C 2, there exists a portion of Theon’s *Exposition of Plato*. It begins with the first chapter, which concerns music (page 46, line 20 in Hiller's edition) and ends with the word "breath" <note>original: πνεύματος</note> in chapter 12 (page 57, line 6). Moreover, from this same section of Theon, twelve manuscripts which I have numbered (12, 42, 76, 118, 159, 173) transition into Pseudo-Euclid under the name of Pappus; see whether the parent of these is manuscript 151 or 187. From this manuscript, Herwerden—complying with the requests of Marquard—extracted the works of Aristoxenus and Cleonides. Marquard demonstrates in *Arx.* XXIV that this manuscript closely approaches the third hand of the ancient Marcian manuscript; furthermore, the Barberini manuscript II 86 is derived from the same source, which source seems to me to have been Valla’s manuscript, Naples III C 2. However, I do not see why Jahn, in his edition of Aristides Quintilianus (page L), denies that this same manuscript contains both Aristides and Aristoxenus.
Vitelli, *Italian Studies in Philology* II 498.

**Magliabechianus 1 (II. III. 428, formerly XI 8).** Paper, octavo. (37)
16th century. Contains *Arx.* <note>likely referring to a musical treatise</note>, Ptolemy with a scholium <note>marginal commentary</note> on Book II, chapter 9, abbreviated excerpts of Porphyry, Damianus’s *Optics*, various works by Barlaam, and Hippocrates. —
Vitelli, as cited above, II 543.

**Wolfenbüttel 701.** Paper, folio. 15th century (Gud. gr. 3). (38)
Porphyry. Ebert, *Manuscript Studies* (Leipzig 1827) II 136.

**Wolfenbüttel 732.** Paper, folio. 15th century. (Gud. gr. 9). Ptolemy, Books I—III (39)
with scholia. Ebert, 140.

**Wolfenbüttel 740.** Paper, folio. 15th century. (Gud. 2.) Aristides Quintilianus. Jahn (40)
page LIII. Caesar, *Principles of Rhetoric* (Marburg 1861) 37.
Bellermann, in *Musical Scales* plate 5, printed the table of musical signs (page 15 in Meibom's edition) from here, where I see it agrees with the Venice manuscript 322. Ebert, 141.

**Hamburg, in the City Library¹ no. 103.** Paper. (41ᵃ)
16th century. Quarto. Bryennius, Aristides Quintilianus. Albert Jahn used this for his edition.

# Page XXXVIII
## Concerning manuscript codices.

(41ᵃ) **Aristides** <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of an important ancient Greek treatise on music</note>, see page LIV and plates I, II, from which I conclude that it agrees with the Venice manuscript 322. It formerly belonged to **Samuel Petit** <note>1594–1643, a French scholar</note>, and later to **Wolf** (according to Jahn). See **Omont**, *Catalogue of the libraries of the Hanseatic cities* (*Centralblatt für Bibliothekswesen* 1890) 368, no. 59.

(41ᵇ) **Hamburg Mathematics**, folio III, paper, 16th century. **Porphyry** <note>Porphyry of Tyre, who wrote a commentary on Ptolemy's Harmonics</note>, books I and II. Omont, 365, no. 46.

(41ᶜᵈ) In the same place, Mathematics, quarto, 17th century. **Hero**, *Pneumatics*. Omont 367, no. 51, 52.

(41ᵉ) In the same place, Mathematics, quarto, 17th century. **Theon**. Omont, same page, 54.

(42) In the same library at **Hamburg**, there were formerly six parts of a codex, which they now number 109–111, formerly 36a 1–6. Paper, small quarto, 16th–17th century. I do not know what the first and fifth parts¹ contained, as they are missing today. In part 2 are contained **Bacchius** and some verses of the first hymn of **Mesomedes**; in part 3, **Aristides**; in part 4, **Gaudentius**; in part 6, **Theon** and **Pappus** on music were once read. For **Buecheler** transcribed the text of Bacchius and the verses of Mesomedes from there, and most diligently examined Aristides, Gaudentius, Theon, and Pappus (meaning **Cleonides**). He noted the same regarding Gaudentius: "collated with the Hamburg manuscript 36a 4, which **Lucas Holstenius** <note>1596–1661, a noted German humanist and geographer</note> had prepared." Furthermore, all parts are written by the same hand. However, **Eyssenhardt**, the director of the library, who wrote to me in 1889 when asked about part II, remained silent about the author of the codex; and **Aemilius Geyso**, who surveyed the surviving parts in 1894, denies having found the name of Holstenius or any other.

Part II, since it has many headings in Bacchius (such as *definition of a musical sound* <note>original: "ὅρος φθόγγου"</note>) written in red ink, is very similar to the **Parisian manuscript 2532**, and considering individual details², you will very easily be persuaded that the Bacchius was transcribed from this Parisian source. I have persuaded myself of the same regarding the hymn of Mesomedes, after I discovered in Paris the traces of a certain letter not seen by **Bellermann**³.

---

1 It seems **Bryennius** was there in part V. For **Petersen**, *History of the Hamburg City Library* (1838), lists him on page 205 (Jahn on Aristides, LIII). In part I, there was perhaps **Aristoxenus**.
2 Manuscript H omits not only what the Munich manuscript O omitted (Bacchius, *Arguments* 1890, p. 27ᵇ), but also omits the words "the ones" <note>original: "αἳ"</note> before "heavy" <note>original: "βαρ."</note> 292, 9; it has "from melodies" <note>original: "ἐκ μελῶν"</note> 14; it has one note for the *mese* <note>the central note of the Greek scale</note> Ψ 294, 4 and ZZ OH 9 (omitting "and" <note>original: "καί"</note>); it omits "which are these?" <note>original: "τίνα ταῦτα"</note> 295, 2. All these agree with that Parisian manuscript which I designated as **Q**.
3 See *Yearbooks for Philology and Pedagogy* 141 (1890), 681.

# (41—42) The Hamburg Manuscripts. XXXIX

In this section, manuscript **P** is also very similar to **H** regarding the author Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, a writer on music theory likely from the 3rd century AD</note>. For in Book I, chapter 7, both contain that form of the diagram in the first position which we recognize from the Venetian manuscript **VI 10**; later, they follow the form of the Venetian **322**, which **H** indeed does not complete. Even the third diagram (in I 11) is very similar to that of **V**, yet it did not originate from there. However, Aristides' book *On Music*, Book I, chapter 21, already ends at page 45, line 14 in Meibom's edition (29, 28 in Jahn's), and neither Holstenius <note>Lucas Holstenius (1596–1661), a noted German humanist and geographer who studied these manuscripts</note> nor whoever compiled the archetype found any more. On page 3, line 13 of Meibom (2, 17 Jahn), manuscripts **H** and **Bb. II 86** have a marginal note: <margin>these things shall be said later</margin> <note>original Greek: "ταῦτα μὲν καὶ ὕστερον εἰπεῖν ἔσται"</note>.

In the fourth part, **H** presents the text of Gaudentius <note>Gaudentius the Philosopher, author of the 'Harmonic Introduction'</note> drawn from several sources. For various readings marked with "al." <note>Latin abbreviation for 'aliter' or 'alia lectio', meaning 'otherwise' or 'another reading'</note> have been added to the words of the primary text. Indeed, the primary words were sought from a manuscript that is not far removed from **Vat. 191**, nor does it come closer to any other source. ¹ For one seeking the origin of the corrections, they are directed to manuscripts **N** and **B** by the diagrams ² inscribed there, where the same three diagrams are found. However, between **N** and **H**, too many words or forms differ; the number of corrections that agree with **B** is quite large. ³ That some corrections do not correspond to the Barberino source is to be attributed to the fact that Holstenius, or whoever compiled those variant readings, used a third archetype; this is evident from the corrected passage at 339, 27. ⁴

---

¹ **H¹** <note>The first hand or original scribe of manuscript H</note> agrees with **V** at page 327, 10 <gloss>accurately and distantly</gloss> <note>Greek: ἀκριβῶς καὶ διαστ.</note>, 329, 12 omits <gloss>is</gloss>, 13 <gloss>so that indeed</gloss>, 14 <gloss>for according to the</gloss>, 15 <gloss>songs</gloss>, 330, 23 <gloss>not even</gloss>, 25 <gloss>and it is</gloss>, and so on frequently. **H** differs at 327, 3 <gloss>I shall sing</gloss> <note>Greek: ἀείσω; this good reading is approved by Stobaeus</note>, 4 <gloss>touching</gloss>, 328, 1 heading <gloss>on voices</gloss>, 19 <gloss>the</gloss> (omitting <gloss>again</gloss>), 22 <gloss>of the deeper</gloss>, 329, 5 <gloss>having</gloss>, 8 <gloss>tension</gloss>, 13 <gloss>shorter</gloss>, 18 <gloss>the one indeed voices</gloss>, 330, 1 <gloss>of the appearing</gloss>, 2 <gloss>deep the note</gloss>, 3 <gloss>he calls an interval</gloss>, 11 <gloss>concerning intervals</gloss> correctly, 17 <gloss>dissonant</gloss>, 332, 12 <gloss>standing over the interval and all things</gloss>, etc.

² You have the diagrams of manuscripts **N**, **B**, and **H** on pages 334, 337, and 340. However, the third one, which refers to chapter 10, has been shifted to a lower position than is proper in **B** and **H**.

³ **H²** <note>The second hand or corrector of manuscript H</note> = **Barb.** <note>The Barberini manuscript</note>: 327, 3 <gloss>I sing</gloss>, 11 omits <gloss>both and dissonant</gloss>, 330, 11 <gloss>concerning the system</gloss>, 332, 15 supplies omissions, 333, 25 <gloss>nete</gloss> <note>the highest note of a Greek tetrachord</note> instead of <gloss>paranete</gloss>, etc. See also 337, 1 <gloss>parypate</gloss> instead of <gloss>lichanos</gloss>, 339, 12 <gloss>half</gloss> and the spelling <gloss>lemma</gloss>, and the gap in the Barberini at 348, 1. Thus these agree in 23 places.

⁴ Page 339, 27: from <gloss>it has the 24</gloss> **H¹** wandered to 340, 2 <gloss>to the 8</gloss>. In the margin is added: <margin>otherwise 9 and again the (one) indeed</margin> <note>Greek: "al. θ καὶ πάλιν την (al. της) μὲν"</note>.

# Page 40
## Concerning the Manuscript Codices

<margin>(42)</margin> In the sixth part, manuscript **H** <note>likely the Hamburgensis manuscript</note> presents a portion of Theon <note>Theon of Smyrna, a 2nd-century mathematician and music theorist</note> and—as we have seen regarding the transition in the Riccardian manuscript—at the word *spirit* <note>original Greek: πνεύματος (pneumatos)</note> on page 57, line 6, it transitions to the introduction of the Aristoxenian doctrine. While other manuscripts of this type title this section *From the Works of Pappus*, **H** transmits it without a name or an index of parts. Indeed, leaving a space of two finger-widths, it begins: *A sound, therefore, is...* <note>original Greek: Φθόγγος μὲν οὖν ἐστι (Phthongos men oun esti)</note>. In the context, however, just as all those who claim this doctrine for Pappus usually agree, so too does **H** agree, such that it is manifest that all have flowed from a common source. This source can be neither Parisinus 2460 nor Barberinianus; for on page 193, **H** says: *this place was left for a diagram*; however, in **P** and **B**, six diagrams are present. It was perhaps Neapolitanus 2, in which those diagrams were inscribed later and not at the beginning, or Vaticanus 671, or Venetus 512. **H** erred in 180, 1 *division* <note>original: διέραισις (dieraisis)</note>, 8 *melody-making*, 9 *study*, 16 *of silence*, and omitted three sounds at 183, 9. However, it seems better than **B** and **P** at 181, 2 *but the result* <note>original: ἀποτέλεσμα δὲ (apotelesma de)</note>.

<margin>(43)</margin> In Leipzig, the following are preserved in the city library:
->**Leipzig 24.** 15th Century. Nicomachus, Aristoxenus, Alypius chapter 1. Bellermann examined this,<-
and the readings do not differ greatly from the other Leipzig manuscript.
<margin>L.</margin> **Leipzig 25.** 15th Century. Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius,
<margin>(44)</margin> Nicomachus I and II, Aristides Quintilianus, Bryennius.

Here, in the section on Euclid, it agrees with **U**, for it has *to be called* <note>149, 9</note>, *dissonant* <note>18 and 20</note>, *the d* <note>150, 6</note>, *multiple* <note>7</note>, *the ηζ*, *the remainder is a unit* <note>152, 8</note>, *the d ... to be multiple or superparticular* <note>154, 3</note>. The same necessity of the manuscripts is clear from the subscription of Zosimus and from the form of the diagrams. For each possesses figures transcribed from **M** by both the first and second hand; it has the broken figure 4 and other similar things. Because **L** is ignorant of the things corrected in **U** in either red or black ink, I conclude that this was written at a sufficiently ancient time, before anything in **U** was corrected. However, **L** corrected passages 189, 19; 190, 14; 195, 20 according to **W**. Marquard (p. XXXII) thinks the text of Aristoxenus was drawn from **U**.

---

*the octave plus the fifth (so B) triple, which 24 has to...*
<note>original Greek: διὰ πασῶν διὰ πέντε ... τριπλ. ὃν ἔχει ὁ κδ πρὸς τὸν</note>
from which you see that discrepancies from two manuscripts are being compared. To this third source, I trace eight corrections that are foreign to **B**.

# Page 41
## (42—45) Hamburg — London

In the works of Alypius <note>A 4th-century Greek writer on music theory</note>, there is a very great similarity between [Manuscript] M and [Manuscript] L. They both omit the word *character* <note>original: "τρόπου"</note> at 370, 1; they both have the word *having* <note>original: "ἔχοντα"</note> at line 3; they omit *drawn down* <note>original: "καθελκυσμένον"</note> at 32. (44) They omit *nodding downward* <note>original: "κάτω νεῦον"</note> at 371, 13; they have *greater* <note>original: "μείζων"</note> at 372, 10, and *somewhat variegated* <note>original: "ὑποαιόλου"</note> at 373, 1. They omit *third* <note>original: "τρίτην"</note> and insert it at the end of the tone; they omit *next-to-middle* <note>original: "παράμεσον"</note> at 375, 22, and have *left* <note>original: "ἀριστερόν"</note> at 31.

The same similarity of forms exists in the musical signs; for the minuscule form of the letter *mu* <note>μ</note> matches in the 7th and 17th positions; the forms 8b and 9b match as I provided on page 369, as do forms 15 (Theta-Eta) and 16b.¹ These same forms appear in the <term>parypata hypaton</term> <note>the second lowest note of the ancient Greek scale</note> of the Hypolydian mode V, in the <term>trita diezeugmenon</term> <note>the third note of the "disjunct" tetrachord</note> Xi, in the <term>trita hyperbolaion</term> <note>the third note of the highest tetrachord</note> Epsilon, in the <term>lichanos hyperbolaion</term> <note>the "forefinger" note of the highest tetrachord</note> Zeta, and so on. Anyone who compares those forms with their own eyes will most certainly be persuaded that L and M are closely related; and since Manuscript U is called the "son" of the Marcianus, while the Leipzig manuscript is called the "father" by other indicators, no one will hesitate to relate this to the tables of Alypius along with us.

In Gaudentius <note>A Greek music theorist, likely 2nd or 3rd century AD</note>, it has *this same* <note>original: "αὗ τὸ"</note> at 328, 19, *indeed* <note>original: "δὴ"</note> at 329, 13, *limma* <note>original: "λίμμα", a small musical interval</note> at 342, 9, and *great* <note>original: "μεγάλου"</note> instead of *fourth* <note>original: "τετάρτου"</note> at 351, 16—features shared with U and many other things of this kind. In Nicomacho <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a mathematician and music theorist</note> it agrees in the words *Pythagorean*, *suggested from nearby*, *chapters of those*, 236, 8 *those*, 237, 8 *beside*, 12 *burdens*, 14 *undistracted*, 238, 8 *introduction*, 19 *of the teacher*, 239, 1 *standing apart in divisions*, 3 *appearing to the sciences*, 243, 11 *water-organs* <note>original: "ὑδραύλους"</note> with an *alpha* written above it; add to this the title of Book II and many other things. Likewise, in the tables of Aristides [Quintilianus], I see them harmonizing so well that the internal necessity of their connection should be doubted in no place.

Since these things were so, and since L—where it had been examined—claimed to have originated from U itself, and since whatever I had seen in the introduction of Pseudo-Euclid in U clearly agreed with L: because I had not diligently noted everything from U (having been overcome by the boredom of common readings), I deemed it useful to put the Leipzig readings in place of U in the list of variants for this booklet. — Naumann, *Catalogue of Manuscript Books*, p. 9.

### In London, preserved in the British Museum: (45)
**London Harleian 5691.** Paper, 15th century. Contains Bryennius, Aristides Quintilianus, and others. Formerly of the College of Agen. *Catalogue of Harleian Manuscripts* III (1808) 288. Jahn, *Aristides Quintilianus* LI.

# On Manuscript Codices

(46) London, Burney 101. Paper, quarto. 16th Century. Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, author of the *Harmonics*</note>, Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre, who wrote a commentary on Ptolemy</note> (the preface is missing). Catalog *New series* (1840) II 43.

(47) London, Burney 102. Paper, folio. 16th Century. Ptolemy with marginal and interlinear scholia <note>Scholia are ancient or medieval explanatory notes</note> (compare Barberini II 86). Ibid. 44.

(48) London, Burney 103. Paper, folio. 16th Century. Porphyry (the title was added by a second hand, that of Bryennius <note>Manuel Bryennius, a late Byzantine music theorist</note>). Ibid. 44.

(49) British Museum, Additional 19,353. Paper, folio. 16th Century. Aristoxenus, Porphyry on Books I and II, Aristides Quintilianus, Alypius. Catalog *of additions* IV (1868) p. 225.^1^

(50) Leiden (Netherlands), no. 47 Scaliger <note>From the collection of Joseph Justus Scaliger</note>, paper, small quarto. 16th Century, written by various hands. It contains: Alypius, Nicomachus in two books, Aristoxenus, Aristides Quintilianus, Bacchius, Dionysius (Pseudo-Bacchius, edited by Bellermann), Anonymous, and hymns. — Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibom, 17th-century editor of *Antiquae Musicae Auctores Septem*</note> used this manuscript when reviewing the text of the musical writers. In Alypius, it differs hardly at all from manuscript M <note>likely Marcianus 322</note>; it seems he also sought Nicomachus (there are two books!) from the M family, for it contains <note>original: "Πυθαγορικοῦ, ἁρμονικῆς"</note> *Pythagorean, of harmonics*, chapter numbers in the index, 236, 8 <note>original: "ἐκείνων"</note> *of those*, 12 <note>original: "ηὑρέθησαν"</note> *were found*, 237, 8 <note>original: "παρὰ"</note> *beside*, 12 <note>original: "φορτίον"</note> *burden*, and many other things which agree with M. However, because it shows in the title <note>original: "κεφάλαια ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ βιβλίῳ"</note> *chapters in the first book* (which M omits), it seems to have originated from manuscript U. — According to Meibomius, those parts written by a different scribe begin with Aristides.

Furthermore, I am persuaded that Bacchius was sought from Paris 2532 (see Bacchius, edited by Argentoratensis 1890, 28), and Bellermann found that the same was done for the hymns (Hymns 18). In emending Aristides, Meibomius—who used two Parisian manuscripts in addition to this Scaliger manuscript—denies on page 224 that he found a better text there. He seems to have used Paris 2532, for that manuscript shows two forms of the diagram in Book I, chapter 7, which Meibomius indicates. But if indeed there is not much difference even in the words and forms of Aristides between this Leiden manuscript and that Parisian one, I suspect the scribe of the Scaliger manuscript sought all these parts, from Aristides through to the hymns, from Paris 2532.

# Page XLIV
## Concerning Manuscript Codices

(56) In addition to the arithmetic arts of Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a 2nd-century mathematician</note> and Diophantus, there are included the *Introduction* of Zosimus (Cleonides) and the *Section of the Canon* by Euclid. It is easily apparent that the name of Zosimus was placed in the title in what seems to be an ancient hand, based on that subscription of Marcianus: *Zosimus corrected this in Constantinople, with good fortune* <note>original: "Ζώσιμος διώρθου ἐν Κωνσταντινουπόλει εὐτυχῶς"</note> (a more recent hand, perhaps Constantine Lascaris, adds: *concerning harmonics* <note>original: "περὶ ἁρμονικῆς"</note>). Therefore, the title of the booklet seems to have been missing from the archetype <note>The original source manuscript from which copies were made</note>. In the *Introduction* itself on page 179, line 5, since it contains the phrase *having the order of things composed* <note>original: "τάξιν τῶν συγκειμένων ἔχον"</note>, I see that it agrees with manuscript W. It ends the *Introduction* with the word *voice* <note>original: "φωνῆς"</note> on page 207, line 7, just as Vatican manuscript 1341 is said to do, and as the Marcian manuscript formerly did. There was added later a description of what a "diagram" and "power" <term>dynamis: the functional role of a musical note</term> are, with the inserted title *Euclid's Introduction to Harmonics* <note>original: "εἰσαγωγῆς ἁρμονικὴ εὐκλείδου"</note>, similar to what we see written in the Marcian. However, Ruelle says the Madrid codex differs from the Marcian in this respect: that this one contains much more (about 50 lines, such as are found in Meibomius). Finally, the Madrid manuscript also ends the *Introduction* with the words *this is the limit of...* <note>original: "οὗτος ὁ ὅρος τῆς . ."</note> as the remaining books end. See Iriarte, Catalogue page 136; Ruelle, *Archives* III, II 575.

(57) Madrid Manuscript N 62. Quarto. 15th Century (written by Constantine Lascaris, 1460, in Milan). Plutarch’s *Sayings of Kings and Commanders* and other Latin works are joined by an anonymous author’s writings on music, which Ruelle published on page 610. *The double-fourth in music, that is, through eight notes* <note>original: "Δὶς διὰ τεσσάρων μουσικῶν τουτέστι δι’ ὀκτὼ φθόγγων"</note> continuing to page 612 *and four from there*. I, Galland, also transcribed these in 1883: *Either the [double] fourth consists of 4 notes and three intervals. The note corresponds to the term of a proposition, and the interval to the proposition itself; for this reason, the 4 notes have three intervals, just as 4 terms have three propositions. After the fourth is the fifth: consisting of 5 notes and 4 intervals. The octave consists of eight notes and 7 intervals.* <note>This passage explains the mathematical relationship between musical notes (phthongoi) and the spaces between them (diastemata) using logic terminology.</note> — Iriarte no. 62. Ruelle, as cited above, 569.

(58) Madrid Manuscript O 35. Paper folio. 16th Century (written by Cornelius Murmuris of Naupactus, Venice, 1554). Ptolemy (text similar to A and B in Wallis); Porphyry books I and II; Aristides Quintilianus; Aristoxenus; Nicomachus; Bryennius; and again Ptolemy (up to book III, chapter 14), the text of which seems to Ruelle to diverge from the wording of the other codices.

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Greek musical manuscripts, specifically focusing on the relationship between the manuscripts of Aristides Quintilianus (AQ) and the "U" codex (Codex Bessarionis). It transitions from discussing notation diagrams to describing specific holdings in the Ambrosian Library in Milan.</meta>

XLV

(56—59) Madrid — Milan

<margin>(58)</margin>The diagrams of musical notation <note>original: "Notarum diagrammata"</note> and the scales of harmonies from Aristides Quintilianus were published by Ruelle (page 608), from which it appears that this recension <note>A specific version of a manuscript text</note> is connected not with manuscript V, but with manuscript U. Furthermore, the table of harmonies from Aristides Quintilianus (Book I, chapter 9, page 22) is to be arranged such that out of the 24 Dorian sounds included in line 2, 9 are referred back to the Dorian harmony, then 6 (omitted above) to the Lydian, and 9 to the Dorian. From the Phrygian harmony, the first 7 sounds (written in the upper line in manuscript U) have perished; the last ones, $\Delta$ $U$, remain. The six signs that follow belong to the Ionian harmony; then follows the lower order (the *krousis* <note>instrumental striking or accompaniment</note>) of the Phrygian (7 signs) and 2 Ionian notes, $\Gamma$ and $<$. In the 4th line, you have the remaining Ionian notes (of which 2 have perished; the last ones, $c \pi \epsilon$, are easily recognized and found in Bellermann’s table 5) and 5 Mixolydian notes (4 to 8). However, the scribe strayed from $\rho \pi \zeta$ to similar notes that had preceded, $c \pi \epsilon$; he repeated $\epsilon$ and all the Mixolydian notes (8 to $\pi \zeta$), and to these he added 6 Syntonolydian signs (compare with manuscript U). In the last line, he has 6 Mixolydian notes and 5 Syntonolydian (compare with U). Thus, everything could easily have originated from manuscript U. See Ruelle 566, 608.

<margin>(59)</margin>In Milan, in the Ambrosian Library, the following are preserved:
Milan, Ambrosian Library, E 19, lower order, paper, folio, 15th–16th century. This contains elegantly written short books by Aristides Quintilianus, Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, Porphyry’s commentary on two books of Ptolemy, Alypius, Gaudentius, and two books of Nicomachus. Another hand added Bacchius on folio 253 and Dionysius (who is falsely called Bacchius) on folio 254; the same hand also corrected several things in Ptolemy.

I first inspected this manuscript in 1889 upon entering Italy, and what one might suspect from the number of Nicomachean books, I have increasingly proven to myself: that it originated from the Bessarion codex, U. The books of Ptolemy are adorned with the same figures and diagrams as the Venetian manuscript. In Gaudentius, it has <note>original Greek: "αὖ τὸ"</note> *the same* (328, 19), <note>original Greek: "μέρη"</note> *parts* instead of <note>original Greek: "μέλη"</note> *melodies* (329, 15), <note>original Greek: "μὲν ἡ"</note> *on the one hand* (331, 24), <note>original Greek: "λίμμα"</note> *limma* <note>a small musical interval</note> (342, 9 ff.), <note>original Greek: "αὐτοί"</note> *themselves* (345, 4), <note>original Greek: "μεγάλου"</note> *of the great* (351, 16), <note>original Greek: "ντίνν"</note> (352, 3), <note>original Greek: "ὑπάτων"</note> *of the lowest strings* (332, 14, but elsewhere often <note>original Greek: "ὑπατῶν"</note> with different accentuation), all exactly as in U. But where U was corrected, this manuscript A preserves the ancient readings <note>original Greek: "τρίτη"</note> *third* (333, 22) and <image-desc>A Greek musical notation sign resembling a square bracket or an antisigma</image-desc> (352, 22), showing itself to be older than the Bologna manuscript.

In Nicomachus, you have the title <note>original Greek: "Πυθαγορικοῦ, ἁρμονικῆς"</note> *Of the Pythagorean, [Manual] of Harmonics*.

# Page XLVI
## Concerning the manuscript books.

(59) In the *Handbook* <note>original: ἐγχειρίδιον (encheiridion); likely referring to a manual of music or harmonics</note>, you find "planets" <note>original: πλανήτας</note> at 236, 7; "to the" <note>τῇ</note> at 10; the word "the" <note>τὴν</note> is omitted at 11; "they were found" <note>ηὑρέθησαν</note> at 12; "in the commentary among the harmonicists" <note>ὑπομνήματι... παρὰ τοῖς ἁρμονικοῖς</note> at 237, 8; "of burdens" <note>φορτίων</note> at 12; "of the teacher" <note>διδασκάλου</note> at 238, 19; "in divided and standing apart" <note>διῃρημένοις καὶ διεστώς</note> at 239, 1; "appearing to the sciences" <note>ἐμφαῖνον τοῖς ἐπιστήμασι</note> at 5; "individually" <note>ἰδίᾳ</note> at 244, 118; "according to connection" <note>κατὰ συναφὴ</note> at 14; "of the diagonal" <note>διαγωνίου</note> at 246, 22; "as it will be at some time" <note>ὡς ἐγ’ ἔσται ποτὲ</note>; "it will show to you" <note>δείξει σοι</note> at 249, 6; "a diphthong in the tongue" <note>γλώσσῃ δίφθογγος</note> at 255, 14. And in the manuscript **U**, since it appeared from the beginning at 249, 11: "the system becomes a fifth" <note>original: τὸ διὰ πέντε γίνεται σύστημα</note>; then "the" <note>ἡ</note> was added after "the" <note>τὸ</note> in the text and "concord" <note>συμφωνία</note> in the margin, the Ambrosian scribe transcribed it as: "the concord, the fifth, becomes a system." See Amsel, *Studemund Studies*, page 127.

(61) ->The Ambrosian manuscript **J 89 lower order**, paper, folio, 16th century, contains Aristides Quintilianus, Aristoxenus, and Ptolemy. (65) ->Munich manuscript **104**, paper, folio, 16th century, written in Venice in 1552 by Franciscus Cladius. It contains Aristides Quintilianus, Bryennius, Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius, and Nicomachus books I and II. In the verses written at the end of this part, since the liberality of Cardinal Bessarion is praised, you might easily believe the resources of this manuscript were drawn from book **U**, which he calls "the best and hard to find" <note>original: ἄριστον καὶ δυσεύρετον</note>. The order of the teachings also agrees (except that **U** adds three small books) and the readings agree. Specifically, the title of the "many-named introduction" <note>isagoges polyonymae</note> is the same, and the readings "having the arrangement of things composed" <note>τάξιν τῶν συγκειμένων ἔχον</note> and the order of parts on page 179, "it is placed" <note>τιθεῖται</note>...

# XLVII
## (59—65) Medici — Munich

180, *against them* <note>original: "καθ' αὐτῶν"</note> (so in the Munich manuscript) 181 and so forth (compare the Paris manuscript, which I have numbered 114 in section 65). The readings also agree in the section on Euclid and the appended text of Zosimus; moreover, those things which were corrected in the Venice manuscript (U) were also found corrected by Claudius. Moving on to Gaudentius, I can repeat what I reported regarding the passages where the Bologna and Ambrosian manuscripts agree with U; likewise, the notes taken from Nicomachus fit this Munich manuscript exactly (add *water organs* <note>original: "ὑδραύλους"</note> 243, corrected to *water organ* <note>original: "ὕδραυλα"</note>). Therefore, this manuscript is also a "son" <note>A philological term meaning a direct copy</note> of the Rhosos codex. In smaller matters, this Munich manuscript (M), which seems to be the youngest of the "sons" (see the corrected passages in Euclid), sometimes departs from the Venice, Ambrosian, and Bologna manuscripts, such as in the words *burden* (237), *divided and set apart* (239, 1), *two* (18), *they thought* (19), *of that which is moved* (241, 22 and similar 242, 3).

After Nicomachus, someone added: *It was equated with its own prototype as far as was possible.* <note>original: "ἐξισώθη τῷ ἑαυτοῦ πρωτοτύπῳ ὡς ἦν δυνατόν"</note> The hand seems to be the same one that corrected many things in Aristides in the margin, marking them with the symbol for "equal" <note>original: "ἴσ."</note>. — On folio 239, however, you find these words added by the man who wrote the book itself: *It was transcribed in Venice by the hand of Franciscus Claudius the Cretan; in the year from the birth of God 1552, on the eighth day of the waning of November. The writer [copied it] as if from the book itself.*

> If you hold me, a book full of Greek graces,
> You might know the intense harmony of the Muses;
> Alas, which the great Lord Bessarion—you know whom I mean—
> Left for me, bestowing grace upon all servants of the Muses.
> But receive me gladly, friend of the Muses; for by always
> Tasting the delight of this in your heart, you shall carry it away.

Written in another hand in the second part of the codex on folio 289 is that *ormasia* <note>A technical term for a specific musical scale or arrangement</note> (1), which you have below on page 421, and the Hypolydian canon comprising 25 sounds. I would have repeated this here, though it has been printed three times already (2), had I not [found] better things from the Vatican—

1 This order of sounds was first edited by Zarlino in *Musical Supplements* (Venice 1588) from the codex of Michael Sophianos of Chios (died 1565). Cf. Fétis, *Memoir on Simultaneous Harmony* 1858. Ruelle, *Archives* III, II 530.
2 Vincent, *Notices* XVI, 2 p. 258. Tzetzes, *On Ancient Greek Music in the Christian Church*. Munich 1874. p. 101. Ruelle, *Archives* III, II 607.

# XLVIII
-> Concerning manuscript codices. <-

<margin>(65)</margin> ...I hoped that assistance would be forthcoming. For now, I say this: that the symbol *chi* <note>original: "χ"</note> signifies five chromatic sounds interspersed within the diatonic genus; the symbol *phi* <note>original: "φ"</note> seems, following Tzetzes (whom I cited in the notes) p. 102, to refer to the *phthorai* <note>original: "φθόροι" - these are "modulating" or "corrupting" signs in Byzantine music that change the interval structure</note>. Whether Ruelle (p. 535) is correct in referring these five sounds—which were treated with no critical skill by Vincent—to Phrygian sounds higher than the rest of the order, will be better judged by one who has consulted Vatican manuscript 192. There follow in the Munich manuscript <note>Codex Monacensis</note> on folio 290 those items which the scribe of Vatican 192 excerpted from Theon and Bacchius. For it teaches what Thrasyllus prescribed concerning the division of the canon (chapters 35, 36) and concerning analogies (I mean arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic) and concerning the *limma* <note>original: "limmate" - a small musical interval, the "remainder" in Pythagorean tuning</note> (Theon chapters 35–36, 33–34); there follow the maxims of Bacchius §§ 44–49, 11–12, which Ruelle published on p. 609 from the Escorial codex (16). And it is easily perceived that this scribe sought his material from Vatican codex 192. For he makes the same errors and omissions as that one in Theon 88, 21; 89, 19; 90, 2; 85, 20, 24; 86, 9. And if anything was poorly written there, he himself displays a corrupt reading, such as "to all" <note>original: "πᾶσιν"</note> at 87, 17, the "diatessaric limma" <note>original: "διαισιαῖον λῆμμα"</note>, the numbers at 86, 8, 15, 16, and "towards" <note>original: "πρὸς"</note> instead of "thrice" <note>original: "τρὶς"</note> at 86, 17. You can see that the doctrine of Bacchius also flowed from there by that phrase "by how much the Lydian" <note>original: "λύδιος πόσῳ"</note> (Ruelle note *f.*) and from the very order of the maxims. However, the one who transcribed these made errors in "tension" <note>original: "τάσις"</note> (Ruelle note *b*) and "trihemitone" <note>original: "τριημιτόνῳ"</note> (*g*). Nevertheless, the Munich manuscript is fuller than the Escorial one, as it provides what the Escorial omits regarding the Hypodorian tone; the same is true in § 6 (excerpted from Bacchius 11), for it displays the signs of the five symphonies <note>concordant intervals</note> just as the Vatican does (see below), though it omits the diagram of the tones. — Finally, the same person who had approved the earlier part of the codex seems to have added here as well: "It was equated with its own prototype" <note>original: "ἐξισώθη τῷ ἑαυτοῦ πρωτοτύπῳ" - a common scribal formula indicating the copy was checked against the original</note>. There follows the handbook of Hephaestion written by a third scribe. — cf. codices 16, 36, 149ª. The Catalog of Hardt Vol. I p. 551 and Jahn in *Auctores Classici* <note>original: "AQ."</note> LII examined this most diligently, and H. Deiters generously lent me his notes.

<margin>(66)</margin> Munich Manuscript 159. Silk paper, folio, 14th century. Diogenes Laertius, prooemium. Other works by Sextus Empiricus, *Against the Mathematicians*. Hardt Vol. II 186.

<margin>(67)</margin> Munich Manuscript 193. Paper, folio, 16th century. By A. Darmarius with marginal commentary on Ptolemy’s *Harmonics* up to Book III, chapter 16. — Hardt Vol. II 263.

# XLIX
### (65—72) Munich — Mutina
**d**
<meta>Musical writers edited by Jan.</meta>

**Munich 200.** Paper, folio. 15th–16th Century. Aristotle’s *Physics* (68)
and other lectures. Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*—Book III, chapter 13, with interlinear scholia <note>Scholia are ancient or medieval explanatory notes written in the margins or between lines of a text.</note> on the term *hexis* <note>original: "ἕξις"</note> (the title of the chapter regarding the criteria in harmony): "not coldness or heat, but a vocal striking." Compare Barberini II 86.
Marginal scholia are also present: *Definition of Harmonics. Definition of Sound. Hearing is the matter, reason is the form and cause.* All of these recur in Barberini H. II 333.

**Munich 215.** Paper, quarto. 15th Century. (Designated as **O**). The first part (69)
elegantly written by Michael Lygizos <note>A famous 15th-century Greek scribe active in Crete and Italy.</note> contains Plotinus’s *Enneads*. The second part, written with a thick reed pen and in a different, less skillful hand, repeats the short books of Venice VI 10, arranged in the same order. I have demonstrated that these agree with one another in Aristides’ diagrams and text in Fleckeisen’s *Yearbooks* 1890, page 684. I also showed that everything matches in Bacchius (the terms *lowest*, *middle*, *interwoven*, *utterance*, *one-and-a-third ratio*, *I have mourned*, <note>original: "ἀπέσχατον, ἀπόμεσον, συμπλέκεται, ἐκφώνησιν, ἐπίτριτον, τεθρήκω"</note> even the spelling of *nothing* <note>original: "οὐθέν"</note> and the iota subscripts) in my edition of *Bacchius the Elder* published in 1890, page 27. However, the scribe omitted the sign ⊢ at 293, 18; he wrote — υ for the signs of the *neta* <note>The highest string/note in the ancient Greek lyre system.</note> at 294, 6; he skipped from the word *Mixolydian* at 303, 11 to the same word at line 14, and from *middle* at 307, 16 to the same at line 19; he omitted the signs **CC** at 297, 14. From these errors, one can easily recognize the "children" (such as Paris 2532) and "grandchildren" <note>Jan uses genealogical terms to describe how later manuscripts were copied from this specific source.</note> of this manuscript. If you wish, you may compare the hymns yourself as they are drawn here in Table II and in Bellermann’s edition, Table I. (I consider Paris 2532, 2458, and Berlin 1555 to be "children"; Paris 2460 belongs to the same family). — I personally examined this in Strasbourg, 1890. — H. II 418. Bellermann, *Hymns* 9, 18. Caesar, *Principles of Rhythmics* 37. Jahn, *Aristides Quintilianus* LIII.

**Munich 301.** Paper, quarto. 16th Century (by Andreas Darmarios). Nicomachus, Book II. (70)
H. III 234.

**Munich 361** (formerly of Augsburg). Paper, folio. 13th Century. (Various works of Euclid.) Aristotle’s *Problems* XIX 1 ff. Theodorus Sophianus’s *Astrology and Music*, section 2. This is actually taken from Ptolemy’s *Art of Harmonics* II 1 and 2. Ptolemy, Book III. H. IV 45.

<margin>(71ᵇ)</margin>
**Munich (formerly Augsburg) 403.** Paper, folio. 15th Century. Theon’s *Arithmetic and Music*. Psellus’s *On the Generation of the Soul*. H. IV 246.

# On manuscript codices <note>Latin: De codicibus manuscriptis</note>

<margin>(73)</margin> **Modena II F 8.** Paper, 15th century. Contains Bryennius, Aristides Quintilianus, Anonymous authors, Bacchius, Pseudo-Bacchius, and hymns. Following the end of Aristides, the subsequent parts on folio 87 were written by a different hand. From the first diagram of Aristides, it is clear¹ that this belongs to family V <note>A specific grouping or "family" of related manuscripts</note>, for it omits the letter alpha ($\alpha$) and that cross; it has $\pi$ at $5^b$, $\sqsupset$ at $11^b$, $\Lambda$ at $19^a$, (omits $24^a$), $\lambda$ at $24^b$, the number $\beta$ before 26, <note>original: a musical sign resembling a diagonal arrow with a vertical bar</note> at $30^b$ and not far from $30^a$, and ends the line with the sign at $34^a$. The same is made manifest from the second diagram (page 22). For the Phrygian mode it has $5^b$ <, and the Mixolydian $2^b$ < is present (which is omitted in the Vatican manuscript U). Furthermore, anyone who compares the portions of the text mixed into those harmonies² with closely related codices (such as (75) **Naples 1**), will quickly find the original source <term>archetype|The original manuscript from which others were copied</term> and demonstrate it clearly. I believe (79) **Naples 5** is a "son" <note>a direct copy</note> of this one. In the hymns of Mesomedes, the arrangement of the verses is such that I am persuaded this one originated from Naples 1 (Yearbooks 1890, 681). This codex formerly belonged to Giorgio Valla and Alberto Pio, as did $13^b$, $74^{a, \ b}$, and 76. I inspected it myself in January 1889.

<margin>(74ᵃ)</margin> **Modena . . . 85.** 15th century. [Psellus] *Compendium of the Four Sciences*.
<margin>(74ᵇ)</margin> **Modena . . . 149.** Porphyry. These also belonged to Giorgio Valla. See *Historical Notes on the Estense Library* (Modena 1873). Heiberg in *Philologus* XLII 434.

<margin>(75)</margin> **Naples III C 1** (formerly 259). Paper, with parchment leaves inserted, small folio size, 15th century. Written by "the humble Peter," <note>original: ταπεινὸς Πέτρος</note> for at the end is read: "the gift and labor of Peter." <note>original: δῶρον καὶ πόνος τ. Πέτρου</note> Contains Nicomachus's *Art of Arithmetic* with the scholia <note>marginal explanations</note> of Philoponus, Plutarch's *On Music*, Porphyry's commentary, Aristides, Anonymous authors, Bacchius, Dionysius Bell., and hymns. Indeed, the beginning of Ptolemy—"Harmonics is the power..." <note>original: Ἁρμονική ἐστιν ÷ δύναμις</note>—is similar to Venice VI 10. Compare also "and the low [pitch] is a property" <note>original: καὶ βαρὺ (omitting τὸ) ἴδιόν ἐστι</note>, the word "proper" <note>original: οἰκεία</note>, and the lack of diagrams.

1 That it was not transcribed from Vatican 192 is demonstrated by the second order of the Phrygian mode, where the Vatican manuscript omits two signs but the Modena manuscript has them. They agree with Naples III C 5: Lydian $8^b$ N, Ionian $3^a$ and the synthesis $3^a$ $\sqsupset$, Phrygian $6^b$ C, synthesis $1^a$ $\supset$.

2 The Modena manuscript has, after the first order of the Dorians: "of the characters of the notes," <note>original: τῶν ἠθῶν τὰς τῶν φθόγγων</note> after the second order: "qualities of harmony," <note>original: ποιότητας ἅρμο</note> after the Phrygian and Ionian titles: "concerning those things," <note>original: ττόμενοι περὶ</note> after the first Ionian: "on the one hand therefore of these things I shall speak," <note>original: μὲν οὖν τούτων υ. ἀ. ἐροῦ</note> and after the synthesis title: "on the one hand now, on the other..." <note>original: μὲν τανῦν δὲ</note>.

# (73—76) Modena — Naples

The teaching of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, 2nd-century astronomer and music theorist</note> and Porphyry <note>Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a commentary on Ptolemy's Harmonics</note> leads to the same conclusion (75), and in the Venetian manuscript of Aristides, the musical signs agree perfectly between them. Both omit two notes, ⊔ and Ε, in the last line of the exposition according to the second tone (page 27, line 9 in Meibom's edition). It also appears that he sought the *Introduction* of Bacchius from the Venetian manuscript (V), yet not from the Munich manuscript (Mon.) (Bacchius, Strasbourg edition, 31); therefore, you might call it a "son" <note>a direct copy or descendant</note> of the Venetian. But in the form of the hymns, which Bellermann took care to print in Table IV, the order of the verses is such (since sometimes one and sometimes three verses are omitted, and the cause of this arrangement is not understood from the Naples manuscript itself, but is more easily traced back to a certain archetype) that I think some book intervened between V and N (Bacchius, ibid., and *Yearbooks* 1890, 686). I found a "son" <note>copy</note> in Modena. I inspected it in 1891. — Cyril's Catalogue II, page 339. Bellermann in *Anonymous* 1. Hymns 18. Studemund-Amsel 154.

**Naples III C 2 (formerly 260).** Paper, quarto. 15th Century. In the first part of the Naples manuscript are contained Hippocrates *On Urines*, Gaudentius, Theo with (76) Pappus’s *Introduction*, Aristoxenus, the Naples excerpts, hypotheses on optics, and Damianus’s *Optics*. In the second part, which is very well drafted with a reed pen, you have Cleonides, Euclid, Barlaam’s *Logistics* and *Arithmetic*, and *On the Eclipse of the Sun*.

The text of Gaudentius is adorned with three diagrams (as in the Barberini and Hamburg manuscripts, no. 36), yet the wording does not differ far from the Vatican or Venetian manuscripts. The codex begins the exposition of Theo with music (page 46, Hiller edition) and presents it up to chapter 12, "the relaxation of the breath" <note>original Greek: ἀνέσεως τοῦ πνεύματος</note> (page 57, 6). From here follows the inscription "From the works of Pappus," <note>original Greek: ἐκ τῶν τοῦ Πάππου</note> an introduction to Euclid in the form that Cramer edited in *Anecdota Parisina* I, 47. (I have counted related manuscripts as 12, 36, 42, 118, 151, 159, 173, 187.) However, since these readings agree in almost all books, I have refrained from noting them down at Naples. — In the titles of Aristoxenus, it agrees with the Marcian manuscript VI, 3. For it has "Aristoxenus: Before the Harmonic Elements," "Aristoxenus: Harmonic Elements, Book II," and "Aristoxenus: Elements of Harmonics, Book III" <note>original Greek: 'A. πρὸ τῶν ἁρμκ. στοιχείων. 'A. ἁρμκ. στοιχείων Β, 'A. στοιχείων ἁρμκ. Γ</note> (Marquard 360). At the end, however, it has additions which a third hand wrote into the Marcian manuscript. Marquard, having compared some part of it, saw that it was similar to the Riccardian codex, but contained fewer errors. — There follow the *Musical Works* of Ptolemy, which I have seen to the printing of under number IX. In this part, this manuscript is more complete than the Paris Supplement 449 in section 18.

# On manuscript codices.

<margin>(76)</margin>
N. <note>The manuscript designated 'N' by the author</note> contains the phrases *one-and-a-half times twelve* <note>original: "ἡμιόλιος τοῦ ιβ"</note> and *in the sesquioctave ratio* <note>original: "ἐπογδόῳ λόγῳ" - a 9:8 musical ratio</note> and the number *21 1/3* <note>original: "κα γ'"</note> in section 24. In those matters which it shares in common with the manuscript Paris 3027, you can see now this one, now that one being more extensive; you might call them brothers. However, in the parts of Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a mathematician and music theorist</note> which he has excerpted here, it offers us the best readings: *at that time they say* <note>original: "τηνικαῦτά φασιν"</note> on page 266, *of the parhypate* <note>original: "παρυπάτης" - the second lowest note in a Greek tetrachord</note>, *they take*, *and of the remaining*, *and from this 512*, pages 267 following, and *we find* page 271.

Another scribe inscribed the *Art of Cleonides* <note>An introductory treatise on harmonic theory</note> in large and beautiful letters. The initial letters, painted with great elegance, recall to the minds of readers the styles of Johannes Rhosus of Crete <note>A famous 15th-century Greek calligrapher</note> who produced the manuscript Venice 322; moreover, the manuscripts Barberinus II 86 and Vatican 221 seem to be written by the same hand. The title of the booklet is *Cleonides' Introduction, of which the beginning is...* (compare Riccardianus 36), and the words seem to be sought from Marcianus VI 3. For N agrees with M <note>Manuscript Marcianus VI 3</note> in Euclid page 149, lines 5 and 7 *proposition*, 12 *by a multiple*, 150, 6 *to the second*, 7 *multiple*, 151, 2 omitting four words, 152, 7 *only*, 158, 11 *being*, 161, 14 and 16 *which is*. In Cleonides 179, 3 *and*, 4 omitting *is*, 5 *and*, 7 *concerning the genera, concerning melody-making, concerning systems, concerning pitch*, 180, 19 *placing*, omitting *intervals*, 20 *intervals*, 181, 4 *lowness*. *Highness is the result produced through tension.* <note>original: "ὀξύτης δὲ τὸ δι᾽ ἐπιτάσεως γινόμενον ἀποτέλεσμα."</note> *For of both*, 182, 4 *some are in the diatonic*, 184, 15 omitting *lichanos hypaton* <note>The "index finger" note of the lowest tetrachord</note>, 185, 7–9 omitted, 186, 10 *hypate*, 187, 4 *and in which according to*, 7 *according to size*, 188, 1 *not so as to be either purified but to be roughened*, 189, 21 *double-step* (the first letter 'i' in an erasure), 190–191, 1 *one-and-a-half and*, 191, 8 he omits, 193, 12 *genera*, 199, 12 *of the conjunct quarter-tones*, 202, 12 *let us sing*, 205, 2 *melody-making* in the first hand of M. Even more significant is the fact that they agree in accents, such as having *to be stretched* <note>original: "τετᾶσθαι"</note> at 181, 8 and *per-fect* <note>original: "τέ-λεία"</note> marked with two accents at 200, 10. It also seemed to be of weightier importance that the words *are composed* and *composed* <note>original: "σύγγεινται" and "συγγείμενα"</note> appear in N (149, 1 and 8), since the form of the letter 'k' written in M is easily confused with 'g'. Moved by these facts, I have determined that a close relationship exists between this N and M. However, it is clear that N was not transcribed from V <note>The Vatican manuscript</note>, because it contains more than that one at 191, 1. 8; 192, 4; 195, 6; 203; 204 etc. Indeed, regarding those things which differ between N and MV, the scribe was able to correct many things from his own wit, as if in the case of the sounds...

The author concludes that N is closely related to Manuscript M (Marcianus VI 3) due to shared errors and specific calligraphic styles resembling Johannes Rhosus.</summary>

# (76) The Neapolitan Manuscript. LIII

<margin>(76)</margin>
In the tables (Introduction, chapter 4) he supplied what was missing, or rather, he reduced the species of harmonies <note>original: "harmoniarum"</note> (chapter 9) and the thirteen tones (chapter 12) into a better order¹; for this reason, I also think it happened that the Neapolitan manuscript (N) agrees with the codices of Pappus <note>Pappus of Alexandria, a 4th-century mathematician who wrote on music theory</note> in not a few places.² Although in certain places he seems to use a good archetype <note>The original source from which a manuscript is copied</note>, as in the term *proportionality* <note>original: "ὁμοιολίαν"</note> (191, 1) and in that phrase *if concerning the axis* <note>original: "εἰ περὶ ἄξις"</note> (195, 6) and in those things which he teaches concerning *modulation* <note>original: "μετάβολα"</note> (page 205, 13). If you consider these things, you will ask whether this manuscript N used two sources. And he who has diligently examined the source of the section cannot deny that another source besides M lies beneath it. For not only does N have the letters intact in demonstrating the 6th proposition <note>original: "protasi"</note>, but it describes the lines of the diagrams much better than the second hand of M (M²); it notes the true numerical ratios for figure 15, and it restores figures 17 and 18, which were corrupted in M. You would call this man most skilled in mathematical matters, who corrected everything with such great skill. But he who actually wrote the codex N is not himself worthy of this praise, since he placed all the diagrams in the wrong places—for instance, figure 18 appears near the 8th proposition. (However, he who restored Euclid's diagrams by his own genius surely placed each one in its own proper place.) Since these things are so, if N flowed from M, another codex must have intervened between them, the maker of which must have been skilled in the mathematical art. Therefore, although a great similarity exists between this and M, the more I weigh the matter, the more my mind inclines to deny that it sprang from M, and rather to call it a distinct and indeed excellent source of the Euclidean books.

Moreover, two learned men, who ascribed the scholia <note>marginal explanatory notes</note> in the margins of the first part, also bestowed their labor on this second part. For example, on page 181, line 4, in the margin, the one who uses smaller letters added: *low pitch is the result produced by relaxation* <note>original: "βαρύτης μέν ἐστι τὸ δι᾽ ἀνέσεως γινόμενον ἀποτέλεσμα"</note>, and at 182, 1 *and the diesis* <note>original: "καὶ δίεσιν" - a small musical interval</note>, and the corrector supplied the sounds which had been omitted at 182, 13. Furthermore, at 186, 14...

---

1 To this I refer 192, 11 *and a tone*, 12 *in number*, 194, 21 *twice*, 198, 14 *of the*, 201, 10 *to each*. <note>These are specific textual corrections found in the manuscript</note>
2 Page 186, 11. 22. 187, 17. 190, 10 *by division* <note>original: "διαιρέσει"</note> and *quarter-tone* <note>original: "τεταρτημ."</note> and others. They differ, however, besides the title and the preface, in 181, 4–6; 185, 7; 187, 16; 188, 2. 13. 16; 189, 2. 10. 17. 20. 21; 191, 7.

The author notes the presence of two different scholarly hands in the marginalia who corrected musical definitions and ratios.</summary>

# LIV
## Concerning the manuscript codices.

<margin>N.</margin> Another person (who I suspect may have been Valla <note>Giorgio Valla (1447–1500), a prominent Italian humanist and collector of Greek manuscripts.</note>) raised the objection: 
<margin>(76)</margin> *the diatonic does not participate in the "pyknon"* <note>original: "διάτονον οὐ μετέχει πυκνοῦ" — the pyknon is a structural element of ancient Greek musical scales</note>; the same person noted at page 187, line 15 *dissonances* <note>original: "διάφωνα"</note> and a little later *what is a consonance?* <note>original: "τί συμφωνία;"</note>; around 190, 14 *in how many ways is the genus divided?* <note>original: "ποσαχῶς διαιρεῖται γένος"</note>; at 199, 18 *how many conjunctions?* <note>original: "πόσαι συναφαί"</note>; and at the end of the verse *modulation in how many ways?* <note>original: "μεταβολὴ ποσαχῶς"</note>. Even those diagrams on page 193 and others in what they call the "Doctrine of Pappus" <note>Pappus of Alexandria, a 4th-century mathematician</note> were inscribed later.

That this book belonged to Giorgio Valla, he says himself on the last leaf of this volume: *This book belongs to Giorgio Valla* <note>original: "Γεωργίου Βάλλα ἐστὶ τὸ βιβλίον"</note>. And just as many written books were sold to Alberto of Carpi¹ after Valla's death, so it was with this codex, in which we read the inscription: *of the most learned Prince Alberto Pio* <note>original: "τοῦ λογιωτάτου ἄρχοντος ἀλβέρτου πίου"</note>. Compare (73. 74) Modena. — Cyril's catalog II 341. — I collated this in Naples in 1891. If I required anything further later on, R. Heinze and Tschiedel most generously supplied it.

<margin>(77)</margin> **Naples III C 3** (formerly 261), paper, octavo. Late 15th century, written by various hands. It contains Porphyry’s introduction to the commentary on Ptolemy (— *of the subject at hand*), tables and passages excerpted from Nicomachus and Ptolemy, Planudes’ verses on Ptolemy’s geography, a table of sounds (Naples excerpts § 1–3), and a table copied from Ptolemy. From folio 5: Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*, the short treatises on music by Plutarch and Theon, Nicomachus’ *Manual* book I, Macrobius’ doctrine translated into Greek by Planudes, and the final chapters of Pseudo-Ptolemy with the refutation by Barlaam <note>Barlaam of Seminara, a 14th-century monk and scholar</note>.

Here he excerpted from Nicomachus’ *Harmonics* chapter 3: *it must be known that the names of the notes* <note>original: "ἰστέον ὅτι τὰ ὀνόματα τῶν φθόγγων"</note> and offers us a good reading at page 241, line 8: *differing from one another in magnitude, speed, and place* <note>original: "μεγέθει καὶ τάχει καὶ τόπῳ ἀλλήλων παρηλλαγμένους"</note>. However, he changes the positions of Mercury and Venus, agreeing with that man who suspects a writing error (Nicomachus 272, 4). He continues in chapter 5: *And Pythagoras was the very first* <note>original: "ὁ Πυθαγόρας δὲ πάμπρωτος"</note> and agrees with the Vatican manuscript at 245, 8 (*the*). The same hand adds (folio 2, back) a diagram of sounds and stars copied from Nicomachus chapter 3, and another diagram which, beginning from the *proslambanomenos* <note>the lowest note in the Greek Greater Perfect System</note>, establishes the nature of the 18 sounds in the Perfect System:

(76. 77) Neapolitan Manuscripts.

## Standing <note>original: ἑστώς (hestos)</note> Proslambanomenos, non-composite <note>original: ἄπυκνος (apyknos); refers to a note that does not belong to a dense cluster or "pyknon"</note> (77)
## Tone <note>original: τόνος (tonos)</note>
## Standing, Hypate Hypaton, low-density <note>original: βαρύπυκνος (barypyknos)</note>, etc.

Since, however, the scribe added the chromatic and enharmonic to the diatonic lichanos <note>the "index finger" note</note> on the same line, you have a system adapted to Nicomachus, chapter 12. <margin>X.</margin> After the verses of Planudes follows a table of numbers, which you have on page 412 in the various readings at line 10, and **The Beginning of Musical Ratios** <note>original: ἀρχὴ τῶν μουσικῶν λόγων</note> on page 411, where I have designated this manuscript as X. That which was painted in the margin, **"It must be known that there are three rhythms,"** <note>original: ἰστέον ὅτι τριῶν ὄντων τῶν ῥυθμῶν</note> I provided on page 423 (should one write **tones** <note>original: τόνων</note> there instead of **rhythms**?). Moreover, although the Neapolitan (2 and 3) and the Parisian manuscripts seem related and similar, I have not found a "father" or "son" relationship in those variations; I would rather call them brothers.^1 Another diagram follows, by which that order of sounds is demonstrated which is found in Ptolemy, Book II, chapter 11. Here also is inscribed **non-composite** <note>apyknos</note> or **low-density** <note>bary-</note> or **middle-density** <note>mesopyknos</note>, and **standing** <note>hestos; fixed</note> or **moving** <note>kinoumenos; mobile</note> is added.^2 Furthermore, at this place, **Hypodorian** is inscribed for the Hypate Meson, and **Hypophrygian** for the Parypate Meson; this is done all the way to the Nete Diezeugmenon, to which is given the **Hypermixolydian, which is also the Hyperphrygian**. This table was therefore established according to the example of Ptolemy (see Wallis page 73 folio = 137 in quarto), except that the principal sound of the Iastian and Aeolian tones, and others of that kind intruded into this order, is also noted.

Then, written in a better hand (folio 5), follow the books of Ptolemy; they seem to have been taken from a manuscript that was not far removed from manuscript U. I have transcribed nothing from the doctrine of Plutarch and Theon, except for the latter's end, **"with the first starting point"** <note>original: μετὰ τῆς πρώτης ἀφορμῆς</note> (which is the end of Nicomachus, Book I). There follows: **"From the commentary of the most wise and learned Maximus Planudes on the second book: The Dream of Scipio."** <note>original: τοῦ σοφωτάτου καὶ λογιωτάτου καὶ μαξίμου τοῦ Πλανούδου ἐκ τῆς ἐξηγήσεως τοῦ δευτέρ. βιβλ. τὸ ἐκ τοῦ Σκειπίωνος ὄνειρον</note> It begins: **Py-**

2 The mobile sounds in the earlier system (folio 2) are distinguished by no epithet; **standing** <note>hestos</note> is only inscribed for the fixed sounds.

# On manuscript codices.

<margin>(77)</margin> **Pythagoras was the first of all men** <note>original: "Χ. θαγόρας πρῶτος πάντων ἀνθρώπων"</note> (Macrobius, *Commentary on the Dream of Scipio* I 1, 8). It concludes: **the double octave contains twelve tones and is produced from a fourfold ratio** <note>original: "ἡ δὶς διὰ πασῶν περιέχει δώδεκα τόνους καὶ γίνεται ἐκ τετραπλασίου"</note> (Macrobius II 1, 25). — I believe the *Manual* of Nicomachus <note>A 2nd-century mathematician and music theorist</note> (folio 92) was transcribed from the Vatican manuscript 198. Indeed, they agree in reading <term>tè</term> with an accent at 237, 9; <term>phrontidi</term> <note>thought/care</note> at 12; <term>hypomeiōsei</term> <note>diminution</note> at 238, 4; <term>plērei t. l. syllogismō</term> <note>with a full syllogism</note> at 9; <term>rhaonos</term> <note>easier</note> at 13; <term>dy’ ephaskon</term> <note>they said two</note> at 19 (the Vatican reads <term>diephaskon</term>); <term>epi panti</term> <note>upon everything</note> at 23; <term>diērthrōmenois</term> <note>articulated</note> at 239, 1; and so on throughout. At 239, 7, both manuscripts correct <term>hyparchei</term> <note>exists</note> to <term>metechei</term> <note>participates in</note>; at 264, 34, both omit the note "nete diezeugmenon" <note>the highest note of the disjunct tetrachord in Greek music</note>.

Added in an even better hand, though in tiny letters (folio 101), are those chapters which Nicephorus Gregoras wrote to complete the art of Ptolemy (III 14—16), and the refutation by Barlaam, which Joannes Franz published in Berlin in 1840 (*Commentary on Greek Musicians*). — Since the works of Nicomachus I, Planudes, and Barlaam found here also appear in the Urbino manuscript 77, you would call these "relatives." — Cyril II 341. Studemund inspected this one; I examined it thoroughly in 1891, and if there was any doubt, Richard Heinze and Rabe inspected it again (see *Philologus* LII 1892, p. 28).

<margin>N.</margin>
<margin>(78)</margin> Naples III C 4 (formerly 262) paper, octavo. 15th century. Contains Bryennius, Bacchius, Pseudo-Bacchius, hymns, Aristides Quintilianus, Anonymous authors, and Ptolemy III 16.

And when I saw in the text of Bacchius that an example of the "Bacchic foot" <note>a metrical foot consisting of one short and two long syllables</note> appeared under the name **Theodore** (§ 101, 5), I thought I had found the archetype <note>the original source manuscript</note> of that Paris manuscript 2456, for which I traveled to Paris in 1890. That hope deceived me. They are indeed very similar to one another, in that both omit <term>tis</term> <note>someone</note> at 292, 3 and <term>estin</term> <note>is</note> at 293, 1; they both fill the gap at 295, 18; they have <term>bainētai</term> at 305, 4, <term>hēs anietai</term> at 306, 11, <term>oiōdēpotoūn</term> at 307, 2, <term>allēlous</term> (though N reads <term>allēla</term>) at 12, and they agree in the forms of the musical notes 2, 15, and 18. However, the number of places where they differ is hardly smaller. To summarize the matter immediately: the Paris manuscript omits the text from 300, 2 <term>tinēs outoi</term> <note>who are these</note> down to those words in line 5, as well as five words in line 10; whereas N omits 303, 12 <term>dōr.</term> <note>Dorian</note> — 15 <term>baryt.</term> <note>lowest pitch</note> and 307, 13 <term>diast.</term> <note>interval</note> to 15 <term>eidē</term> <note>species</note>. They are therefore "twins," neither being the child of the other. And regarding the hymns, this manuscript N is the best codex, as it alone contains the musical signs from II 13 onwards. It is known that Galilei <note>Vincenzo Galilei, father of Galileo and a music theorist</note> used this manuscript when he published the hymns...
