# LVII
## (77—79) The Neapolitan Manuscripts

I hope it is now clear and evident (pp. 454—56) that by this very manuscript, which formerly belonged to the Farnese family <margin>N.</margin> <note>The letter 'N' refers to the specific siglum or shorthand used by the author to identify the Neapolitan manuscript in his critical apparatus.</note>, and by that same book, men were led to falsely claim (78) that **Dionysius** was the poet of the hymns, and that **Bacchius** was the author of the technical treatise which begins with the words <term>To the Musical Art</term> <note>original: Τῇ μουσικῇ τέχνῃ</note>. Indeed, you have the title **Dionysius** depicted in Bellermann’s *Hymns*, Plate II, which, appearing at the end of the lessons on the ratios of sounds, is unrelated to the hymns themselves. This manuscript does not contain the entirety of the **Anonymous** authors' doctrines. It omits sections 85—104 and substitutes matters of no utility. For we shall not highly value that diagram in Bellermann’s section 83, nor was there a need to repeat (section 84) that final chapter which **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Gregoras, a 14th-century Byzantine scholar who wrote on music and astronomy.</note> composed to supplement **Ptolemy**. We have a companion to this manuscript "N" in the Vatican Urbino Codex 77.

In **Aristides** <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of an essential ancient Greek treatise on music.</note>, who compares the primary diagrams, one might at first glance claim this manuscript for the "U" family, because it displays the letter *alpha* and that cross omitted in the Venice manuscript, while omitting the *beta* which the Venice manuscript provides. However, whoever examines it more accurately will see that this manuscript agrees with Vatican 192 (and the Modena manuscript), in which those same elements are either missing or present. And from those six harmonies in Book I, chapter 9, it shines through most clearly that it acts with the Vatican manuscript and stands with the Venice manuscript rather than with "U." For it possesses the sign for the second flat of the Mixolydian mode <note>The author is discussing specific musical notation symbols used in ancient Greek theory.</note> which is omitted in "U"; it has the sixth flat of the Ionian; it agrees with the Venice manuscript in all things where the Vatican agrees; and it has the second flat of the Ionian in section 7. Therefore, it is a brother to this manuscript, or a son of a brother. Even the diagram of the Lydian mode, which Bellermann published in *Musical Scales* p. 77 (and Jahn in Plate I, Amsel in his plate), is common to this and Vatican 192. Nor was **A. Jahn** mistaken when he said on page XLIX that **Galilei's** manuscript <note>Vincenzo Galilei, father of Galileo, who was a prominent music theorist in the Renaissance.</note> ought to be investigated; I believe it has been found and should be compared. Bellermann also declares this to be a good manuscript in the place cited, page 77. — **Cyril** II 347. Bellermann, *Anonymous* 1, *Hymns* p. 9, with a specimen of the handwriting in Plates II and III. I compared the **Bacchius** text in Naples in 1891.

**Naples III C 5** (formerly 263), paper, octavo. 16th Century. Contains: (79) **Ptolemy**, **Porphyry**, **Aristides Quintilianus**, **Anonymous**, **Bacchius**.

This manuscript, written poorly and without any diligence, agrees in several places in **Ptolemy** with Wallis’s edition <note>John Wallis, the 17th-century English mathematician who edited Ptolemy's Harmonics.</note>. In **Aristides**, it belongs to the Venice family VI 10 and in many matters is most accurate...

# Page 58
## Concerning manuscript codices.

<margin>(79)</margin> This manuscript agrees with the Modena codex <note>original: "Mut." for Mutinensis</note>, for the parts of the text placed between the musical signs are the same in both<sup>1</sup>; from Chapter I, section 7, both omit a certain entire series of signs. Everything that Bellermann noted regarding this Naples manuscript in *Tonleitern* page 67 fits the Modena manuscript perfectly. Since the former appears to have been written in the 15th century and the latter in the 16th, one might suspect that the former is the parent and the latter the descendant. In the section on Bacchius <note>Bacchius Geron, a Greek author on music theory</note>, the passages I compared agree with Venice manuscript VI 10, yet they differ from Naples 1 and 4 and come closer to Venice Marciana VI 2. I examined this in Naples in 1891. See Cyril, volume II, page 248.

->At Oxford, in the Bodleian Library<sup>2</sup>, there exist:<-

<margin>(80)</margin> **Oxford Baroccianus 41.** Paper, small quarto, 15th century. Contains Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius, Nicomachus (Book II), and Porphyry. From the paper and the handwriting, Wallis <note>John Wallis (1616–1703), English mathematician and music theorist</note> conjectures that it was produced in the East; however, the order of the books reminds us of manuscripts M and U (note also that there are two books of Nicomachus). It is older than those Oxford manuscripts used by Meibom <note>Marcus Meibom, editor of 'Antiquae Musicae Auctores Septem' (1652)</note>, and was donated to the library by the Earl of Pembroke (marked M in the preface to Gaudentius). Wallis designated it as "D." Compare what is to be said regarding (86) Miscellaneous 87. See Coxe, *Catalogue* (1893) Volume I, page 59.

<margin>(81)</margin> **Oxford Baroccianus 124.** Paper, quarto, 15th century. Contains Ptolemy. Wallis designated this as "C." See Coxe, page 200.

<margin>(82)</margin> **Oxford Selden 20 (formerly 3363).** Paper, folio, early 16th century. Mutilated at the beginning. Concerning the calculation of solar motion. Contains Aristoxenus<sup>3</sup> and Alypius. The musical signs end in the Aeolian mode, as occurs in Vatican manuscript 191. Meibom calls it "most corrupt" in his preface to *Alypius*. See also the preface to *Aristoxenus* near the end. Coxe, page 596. Marquard, *Aristoxenus* page XXIV. Westphal-Garan, *Aristoxenus* (1893) pages XCI and 108.

**Oxford Bodleian Miscellaneous 36.** 16th century. A chain of commentary on the Song of Songs.
<margin>(83)</margin> Psellus’ *Psychogonia* <note>The "Generation of the Soul," a Neoplatonic commentary on Plato's Timaeus</note>. Coxe, page 641. (From "The indeed mentioned..." to "...the highest point" = Linder 1.) See also page 179.

# (79—93) Oxford Manuscripts

<margin>(84)</margin>
Oxford, Bodleian Library, Miscellaneous 85. Paper, folio size, 16th century. (Formerly the property of Savile and numbered 2946). (Contains Ptolemy’s *Hypotheses* and other astronomical works.) Aristides Quintilianus <note>A 3rd-century music theorist</note> was examined here by Langbaine. See Coxe, Volume I, page 670 and (regarding Langbaine’s notes) page 878.

<margin>(85)</margin>
Oxford, Bodleian Miscellaneous 86. Paper, folio size, 16th century (formerly Savile and 2947). Written by the same hand as (84) Bodleian Miscellaneous 85. Contains Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, Nicomachus, Gaudentius, Alypius, Bryennius, and Hero’s *Pneumatics*. (Compare with entry 86.)

<margin>(86)</margin>
Oxford, Bodleian Miscellaneous 87. Paper, folio size, 16th century. (Formerly 2948), a second copy of the volume just mentioned. Coxe Volume I, 671. However, Wallis states in his preface to Ptolemy: "I believe Savile, about 60 years ago, more or less, had both of these (85 and 86, which are Miscellaneous 86 and 87, previously numbered 164 and 165, but which he himself marks A and B) transcribed either from the same Vatican manuscript (or another in Italy), or, as I rather suspect, manuscript B was copied from there and then A was copied from B." One of these is the more recent Bodleian manuscript used by Meibom, which he says was given to the library by Savile (in his notes on Aristoxenus and Gaudentius). He proclaims the Oxford manuscripts to be much better than the Scaliger manuscript (in the preface to Nicomachus), which he proves by the readings "of the parts" <note>original: μορίων</note> at 273, 9 and "13 degrees and 14 first minutes" <note>original: μοίρας μὲν ιγ, λεπτὰ δὲ πρῶτα ιδ</note> at 22. To me, indeed, a weightier argument for its excellence seems to be that the perverse doctrine concerning the *diazeuxis* <term>diazeuxis: the separation or disjunction between two tetrachords in a musical scale</term> at 259, 15 is absent from the Bodleian manuscript.

<margin>(87)</margin>
Oxford, Bodleian Miscellaneous 88 (formerly 2950). Paper, folio size, 16th century. Porphyry. Coxe 672.

<margin>(88)</margin>
Oxford, Bodleian Miscellaneous 225. Paper, folio size, early 14th century. Porphyry, Aristides Quintilianus, and the geometry of Pediasimus (perhaps including the section published by Vincent in *Notices* 290, beginning "A sound is indeed..." <note>original: Φθόγγος μέν ἐστι</note>). Coxe 779.

->In other Oxford libraries there exist:<-

<margin>(89)</margin>
The Balliol College Oxford manuscript contained Alypius; it was examined by Langbaine.

<margin>(90)</margin>
The St. John’s College Oxford manuscript contains Ptolemy; it is marked 'J' by Wallis. It formerly belonged to Archbishop Laud, from which the London Gale manuscript (47) was transcribed.

<margin>(91)</margin>
The manuscript of Patrick Young <note>original: Patricii Junii</note> at Oxford provided Gaudentius to Meibom; it was examined by Selden.

<margin>(92)</margin>
The Magdalen College Oxford manuscript provided Aristides to Meibom; it was transcribed under the care of Selden.

<margin>(93)</margin>
The Magdalen Oxford manuscript provided Porphyry and Bryennius to Wallis.

<margin>(95)</margin> Parisinus 360 (Colbert 4477). Cotton paper <note>term: "bomb." (bombycinus); a type of paper made from silk or cotton used before wood-pulp paper became common</note>, octavo size. 14th Century. Fragments of a Sticherarion <note>a service book of the Eastern Orthodox Church containing hymns</note>, and the Anonymous Hagiopolites. These instructions were edited by Vincent, *Notices* XVI 1, 259. Omont, *Summary Inventory of the Manuscripts of the National Library* I p. 36.
<margin>(96)</margin> Parisinus 1817. Paper, folio size. 16th Century. Theon’s exposition on Plato, and the same work repeated by another hand. Psellus on Plato’s *Psychogonia* <note>The "generation of the soul" in the Timaeus</note>. Omont II 148. Psellus was edited from this source by Vincent, *Notices* XVI 2, 316.
<margin>(97—100)</margin> Parisinus 1818—21, all paper and from the 16th Century (number 1820 is 17th Century); they contain that same exposition by Theon. Omont, same place.
<margin>(101)</margin> Parisinus 2338. Paper, folio size. 16th Century. George Pachymeres on the four mathematical sciences <note>The Quadrivium: arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</note>. Edited by Vincent, *Notices* XVI 2, 363. Omont 243.
<margin>(102—3)</margin> Parisinus 2339—40. Paper, folio size. Pachymeres. Number 102 was written by Angelus Vergecius, 103 by Peter Vergecius. Omont, same place.
<margin>(104)</margin> Parisinus 2379 (formerly Royal Medici 2157). Paper, folio size. 15th Century. (By John of Otranto); contains Diophantus and others. Aristoxenus <note>original: "Arx."</note>. Ruelle, in his edition of Aristoxenus by Westphal (Abel 1893) XCVII, judges it to be closely related to the Selden codex. Omont II 249.
<margin>(105)</margin> Parisinus 2430. Paper, folio size. 16th Century. Hero's *Pneumatics*, Bryenniu<unclear>s</unclear> and others. Omont 260.
<margin>(106)</margin> Parisinus 2448 (Colbert 4037). Cotton paper, small folio. 14th Century. The Compendium <note>term: "Syntagma"</note> *Music according to the ancients*¹ <note>original: "Μουσικὴν οἱ παλαιοί"</note>, more complete than the version edited by Xylander (Basel 1556). The doctrines omitted there were edited by Vincent in the place cited above, 338—42; parts of these are found in Vatican manuscripts 192 and 698. The author of this compendium is said to be Psellus by Xylander and Vincent; however, Joseph Pinarus Rhacendyta, who wrote a synopsis of the rhetorical art (edited by Walz, *Rhetoricians* III 467—569), is also declared the author of this short work in (135) Parisinus 3031 and (192) Marcianus 529.

# (93—114) Oxford — Paris. LXI

This Rhacendyta <note>Joseph Rhacendyta, a 14th-century Byzantine scholar</note>, who is said to have flourished around the year 1300 (Hardt catalog I 458), could in no way have written that *compendium*, which already existed as a transcript in the Palatine manuscript 281 in the 11th century. Furthermore, the astronomical part (page 83, Kyliander edition) refers to the Byzantine year 6516, which for us is the year 1008. Since those four small books must therefore be traced back to the eleventh century, one ought to embrace and believe the evidence presented by the Laurentian manuscript 87, 10 (25^c). When Valentin Rose did this in *Hermes* II (Berlin 1867) page 465, he attributed the *quadrivium* <note>The four liberal arts: arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</note>—which he demonstrated was connected to the synopsis of Aristotle's *Organon*^1—to Gregory "among the monks" <note>original: ἐν μονοτρόποις (en monotropois)</note>. Therefore, that Rhacendyta adapted Gregory's doctrines for his own use. — Omitted 263.

<margin>(107)</margin>
Paris 2449 (Royal 2740). Paper, quarto. 16th century. Aristoxenus. In the judgment of Ruelle (Aristoxenus Westphal XCVIII), this seems to have been transcribed either from the Scaliger manuscript or from (115) Paris 2457. Omitted in the same place.

<margin>(108)</margin>
Paris 2450 (Medici-Royal 2164), parchment, small folio. 14th century. — Ptolemy, Theo on music and astronomy. Omitted 263.

<margin>(109)</margin>
Paris 2451 (Medici-Royal 2717), paper, small folio. 15th century. — Contains Ptolemy, Porphyry, Plutarch. Omitted 264.

<margin>(112)</margin>

This manuscript, both in the order of the books (up to Porphyry) and in individual words, depends on manuscript U. It contains at 179 <note>original: τῶν συγκειμένων ἔχον (of the things composed having)</note>, 180 <note>original: τέθειται, διαστάσεις, διαστήματα δὲ τά (it is placed, dimensions, and the intervals are these)</note>, etc.

# LXII
-> Concerning manuscript codices. <-

(114) ...which we saw in the Leipzig manuscript <note>original: "Lips."</note> and I do not wish to repeat. However, in the context of that *Introduction* <term>isagoge: a formal introductory treatise</term>, it seems very closely joined with the Munich manuscript 104 <note>original: "Mon. 104"</note>, since it contains the readings "they are" <note>original Greek: εἰσὶν</note> at 179, 6 (instead of "it is"), "against themselves" <note>καθ' αὑτῶν</note> at 181, 8, "desio" <note>δεσίω</note> at 189, 21, and "intervallic" <note>διαστηματικοῦ</note> at 206, 4. Yet the Munich manuscript 187, 16 is fuller than this one. In the *Division of the Canon* <term>canonis sectione: a mathematical treatise on musical intervals attributed to Euclid</term>, it repeats almost all¹ those corrections shown in manuscript U (it is unaware of only one), such that you would call it a more recent book. In the works of Gaudentius <note>A Greek music theorist, likely 4th century AD</note>, by those readings "this" <note>αὗ το</note> at 328, "lower/graver" <note>βαρυτέρας</note> ibid., "no longer" <note>μηκέτ'</note> at 329, 2, and "lower/graver" <note>βαρυτέρους</note> at 13, you may recognize the U family of manuscripts. Likewise, in that section of Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a mathematician and music theorist</note> regarding the terms "underground," "planets," "was found," "burden," and "teacher" <note>original Greek: ὑπογίου, πλανήτας, ηὑρέθ., φορτίον, διδασκάλου</note> (in this part it seems very similar to the Leiden Vossianus manuscript).

<margin>P.</margin> At some more recent time (as in the Ambrosian manuscript E 19), the teaching of Bacchius <note>Bacchius the Elder, author of a musical catechism</note> seems to have been added to these, which I have examined and included in the collection of variant readings. For that notable example "to Theodore" <note>θεοδώρῳ</note> at 316, 2 is known from Mersenne’s edition <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath</note>, although it perhaps arose from a conjecture rather than from an ancient archetype. Compare that "to be weighed down" <note>βαρυνθῆναι</note> which this manuscript has at 188, 2 instead of "to be roughened" <note>τραχυνθῆναι</note>. A companion of the readings in Bacchius (not the father/source) is the Naples manuscript III C 4. I collated <term>collated: compared manuscripts word-for-word to find differences</term> this in Paris in 1890. (Bacchius, Argument I 25ᵇ.) Omitted at 264.

(115) P. 2457 (Teller Rem. 2179, 3). Paper, small folio, written in the year 1537 by Angelo Vergetio <note>A famous Cretan scribe who worked for King Francis I of France</note>. Contains Aristides Quintilianus, Bryennius, Plutarch, Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Nicomachus, Ptolemy, and Porphyry (cf. 114 and U). Omitted in the same place.

(116) P. 2458 (Colbert 1830). Paper, small folio. 16th Century (1544), elegantly written in large letters by Pietro Vergetio. Contains Aristides Quintilianus, Anonymous authors, Bacchius, Dionysius (Pseudo-Bacchius), and hymns. (cf. Berlin 1555.)

The first diagram of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus</note> seems to have been drawn according to the model of the Venice manuscript VI 10; for the signs 2 (as Bellermann numbers them, 62), 4, 5, 6 (= ϙ <note>koppa</note>), 8—11 and especially 12 𐅄 agree; 2 V and 7 Υ differ slightly. However, there lies beneath this a difficulty and doubt, because here (as in Vatican 192) it has the beginning of the numbers as α ✱. Then a second hand, with less care and elegance but in red ink, added part of the letters, expressed...

# Page LXIII
## (114—118) Parisian Manuscripts

According to the Wolfenbüttel copy <note>original: "Guelferbytanum exemplar"</note> (which you can find in Bellermann’s tables) or U, where 6 represents Mercury ☿, 12 the Moon ☽, 2<sup>b</sup> Alpha α, 5<sup>b</sup> the Moon ☽, and 6<sup>b</sup> Sigma σ. Therefore, the diagram was first written based on V or a similar source, then corrections were begun (as seen in Parisinus 2532 and the Barberini manuscript) according to a secondary, inferior copy; however, this work was never completed (as seen in the Hamburg manuscript). The same occurred in the second diagram (the six harmonies). For there too, the corrector, not satisfied with the diagram that began with the uncial **R**, substituted other letters which smack of more recent usage, such as ϱ- (= U). Vincent, in *Notices* page 2, separated the anonymous doctrines into two parts; I have distinguished a third doctrine (§ 66—104) in *Philologus* XXX 418. Furthermore, you will believe that Bacchius <note>Bacchius the Elder, a writer on music theory</note> and the hymns were transcribed from the 1555 Berlin manuscript once you have read what I have compared regarding that codex <term>codex: a manuscript book</term>. Indeed, my mind inclines toward the view that this entire manuscript originated from there and should be traced back to the family of the ancient Venetian manuscript, even though that Alpha α and the symbol ※ stand in the way. I inspected this in 1890. (See Bellermann, Anonymous 1; Hymns 19; Bacchius, Strasbourg edition I 28; *Yearbooks* 1890, 686.) Omitted in 265.

### Parisinus 2459 (Colbert 2595). Paper, folio. 16th Century. Andreas Darmarios. (117)
Aristides Quintilianus, Ptolemy. Omitted in the same place.

### Parisinus 2460 (Tellier-Remensis 2179, 2). Paper, folio. 16th Century. (118)
Alypius, Gaudentius, Anonymous, Bacchius, Dionysius, Pseudo-Euclid, Euclid, Theon with Pappus, Aristoxenus, Nicomachus, Aristides Quintilianus, Bryennius Books I and II.

And indeed, I was easily able to determine from the gaps <note>lacunae</note> that the Bacchius text was drawn from Munich manuscript 215. However, because it does not repeat that point about the musical note <note>original: "phthongo"</note> at 307, 19 as manuscript O does, but omits it along with Parisinus 2532, it seems to be a "son" <note>a direct copy</note> of the latter. Moreover, it shares many common features with this family (Hamburg and Barberini). It attributes the *Introduction* <note>original: "isagogen"</note> to Euclid in the first instance (for it presents it twice, as is the custom of this class of manuscripts) and aligns with manuscript U. However, Cramer published the other context of that art, inscribed "From the works of Pappus," from this source in the *Parisian Anecdotes* I 47. For it transitions from Theon’s 12th chapter to this form of Aristoxenian doctrine. Those who have followed that transition and attribute the *Introduction* to Pappus (I have counted 12, 36, 42, 76, 151, 159, 173, 187), I see agreeing so closely in their readings that I do not hesitate to call them very tightly linked. And although you might perhaps call a portion of the readings "attractive" and corrected by the wit of a clever scribe (such as the sounds and...

# (126 — 129) Paris Manuscripts. LXV
### Musical writings edited by Jan, version e

464, 9: One will easily persuade oneself that this is the origin of the codex <note>A "codex" is a manuscript in book form rather than a scroll.</note>. (126) Moreover, I have long suspected (JA I 28<sup>b</sup>) that the teaching of Bacchius <note>Bacchius Geron, a 4th-century author of a musical primer</note> flowed from that same source; and when Ruelle, with his characteristic kindness and generosity, had diligently compared that doctrine, the opinion was proven correct. For they agree not only in those matters which I enumerated above (p. XIV f.) as belonging to the entire "V" family, but they also equally contain "from melodies" <note>original: ἐκ μελῶν</note> 292, 14; they omit the symbol ⊢ 293, 18; they contain "and" <note>original: καὶ</note> Ψ 294, 4, "and" —υ 6, "and" ⊏ Z 10; they omit the things which were to be interposed between "mixolydian" 303, 11 and 14, likewise 307, 16 and 19 between the two "middle" <note>original: μέσον</note> notes; they omit that "CC" 297, 14 etc. Since, indeed, Bacchius and the hymns came into this codex from the Munich manuscript <note>referred to as "Mon."</note>, it seems likely that everything was sought from there. However, since this same Paris manuscript omits in Bacchius 295, 2 "what are these?" <note>original: τίνα ταῦτα;</note>; in 298, 11 "and a semitone" <note>original: καὶ ἡμιτόνιον</note>; has in the margin of the same page at line 14 "two tones" <note>original: δύο τόνους</note>; omits "another" <note>original: ἕτερον</note> 306, 23; omits "how many" <note>original: πόσοι</note> 315, 2; has "melody" <note>original: μέλος</note> 305, 15 (where the Munich manuscript has "strength" <note>original: μένος</note>); and omits 307, 19 "but every sound etc." <note>original: πᾶς δὲ φθόγγος κτλ.</note> — the Hamburg, Scaliger, and Barberini manuscripts (no. 42, 48, 173, and perhaps also Paris 2460, which I number 114) must be considered its "offspring" <note>meaning they were copied from it</note>, because they contain and omit the same things. And all of these, as far as I know, have that first diagram of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of "On Music"</note> corrected in a similar way. — It omits II 279; Bellermann provides a visual specimen of the writing in the tables of hymns I and II.

# On the Manuscript Codices

<margin>(129)</margin> It seems to have originally had the word *from* <note>original: "ἀπὸ"</note>; now the letter *a* has been erased and changed into *u*, while in manuscript P it is written as *from* <note>original: "ἀπό"</note>. They agree in the phrase *and it is called iastian, of which the lower-pitched...* <note>original: "καὶ ἰάστιος καλεῖται ὧν ὁ βαρύτερος"</note> which is placed in the wrong location at 204, 5. Their consensus is the same regarding the work of Euclid, both in the forms of the diagrams and the wording. Compare *nor* <note>original: "οὐδὲ"</note> page 149, 4; *by the proposition* <note>original: "προθέσει"</note> 5; *by the multiple* <note>original: "πολλαπλασίονι"</note> 12; *the mixing of the* <note>original: "κρᾶσιν τοῦ"</note> 19. It is clear, therefore, that these are connected by nature and origin. And where I saw that in several places N had been corrected and P had received those corrected readings, I used to think that P was transcribed from N. 

However, since the letter *eta* <note>η</note> of figure 6a is missing in N but exists here, and likewise the letter *gamma* <note>γ</note> from figure 8, and since the lengths of the lines in this figure 8 are more carefully expressed in P, and the entirety of figure 9 is present in P but missing in N, we shall instead conclude that both were copied from the same archetype <term>archetype: the lost original manuscript from which others are copied</term>. Added to this is the fact that the placement of diagrams in P is better observed than in N <insert>1</insert>, as are the accents on the word *hypaton* <note>the "highest" strings in ancient Greek music theory</note>. For while at 184, 16–17 and 186, 10 N has *hypatôn* <note>original: "ὑπατῶν", with a circumflex accent</note>, P has *hypáton* <note>original: "ὑπάτων", with an acute accent</note>; since scribes, if changing things of their own accord, usually preferred the circumflex, P seems to have more accurately imitated what existed in the archetype. 

As for the fact that the "second hand" <note>a later corrector</note> of the Neapolitan manuscript <insert>2</insert> sometimes agrees with P, who cannot see that this could be attributed to various causes and that nothing certain can be proven by it? On the other hand, no one can establish that N was transcribed from P, unless one dates P to the 15th century. Whether this can be admitted, I cannot decide today; let others investigate. But if P is considered the chief of this family of manuscripts, we should no longer wonder why in the Neapolitan and Barberini manuscripts the *Division of the Canon* <note>a famous treatise on musical mathematics</note> is also attributed to Cleonides; for in P, this work lacks any title. The end of the booklet does not exist in P now; however, one might conclude that the entire treatise was once present from the words *and it is* <note>original: "καὶ ἔστι"</note>, which act as "catchwords" for those...

---

<insert>1</insert> Figure 10 is added to proposition 6 in N, but to 8 in P; figure 15 to 6 in N, but to 9 in P; figure 17 to 7 in N, but to 9 in P.

<insert>2</insert> On page 151, 8 N had written *I say the multiple* <note>original: "πολλαπλάσιός φημι"</note>, later this word was deleted; in the 6th demonstration the second hand of N adds the letter K, then corrects *upon* <note>original: "ἐπὶ"</note> to *since* <note>original: "ἐπεὶ"</note>; at 156, 11 it adds *therefore a is of c* <note>original: "ἄρα ἐστὶν ὁ α τοῦ γ"</note>; at 157, 8 it inserts *and of the c* <note>original: "τοῦ δὲ γ"</note>; in the tables of sounds at 185, 6 it supplies what was missing.

<summary>The author analyzes the relationship between Greek manuscripts N and P, arguing they both derive from a common lost ancestor (archetype) rather than one being a copy of the other. This is evidenced by specific diagram placements, accents on musical terms, and missing text in the Neapolitan codex.</summary>

**P. 3027** (*Baluz. reg. 3054, 2*). Paper, octavo. 16th–17th Century. (134)
Included are *Libanius's oration to Calippus* and others. From leaf 16—39 Omont
(III 95), following the author of the catalog, says there exists a *treatise by Aristides*
mutilated at the beginning. But see *Vincent, Notices* XVI, 2, p. 5. They are
excerpts rather from *Cleonides* and *Euclid*. Indeed, the *introduction*
is sampled as far as leaf 24 verso. From here begins (if there were silence) the section
of Euclid; I do not know to what end it is led. Then on leaf 31, line 5,
one reads: [*Music is the science of rhythm] and melody and of all instrumental*
*theory; a musician is one experienced in these things*
(*Vincent p. 5*). On leaf 31, line 20, there exist excerpts from Naples § 9—16 (= *Parisian*
*Fragments*, *Vincent* 244—48 with a figure), then *The relaxation of the voice*
*toward the deeper pitch* (*Vincent 234*), concerning the tension and relaxation
of the voice, concerning sound, interval, and the fourth <note>diatessaron</note>, parts of which are
found in Aristides Quintilianus Book I, chapters 5—7. There follow on leaf 33, line 9, excerpts from Naples
§ 20—22 (= *Vincent* 242. Westphal *Fragments and Teaching*
*Principles* [1861] 78—80) and on leaf 34 *The Impulse* <note>original: "Ὁρμασία"</note> = Naples excerpts § 27
(*Vincent 254*). Moreover, I have noted this end: leaf 39 verso *but*
*for certainly the destruction of one is the source of birth for others, and...*

# LXIX
### (134—145) Paris Manuscripts. LXIX

<margin>(143)</margin>
Furthermore, Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibom (1630–1711), a scholar famous for editing ancient Greek musical texts.</note> found insertions in the codices <note>A "codex" is an ancient manuscript in book form.</note> of Vulcanus and Possevinus within the words of Euclid near the end of chapter 4 (see the notes on Aristides Quintilianus page 216 and on Euclid page 54). Therefore, see whether there is any relationship between these (55. 156) and the Paris manuscript 449. On page 10, however, Vincent cites a scholium <note>A marginal note or explanatory comment made by an ancient copyist.</note>: *A diesis is the third part of a tone and the fourth part. For the tone is divided into a half, into a third, and a fourth. And the half is called a semitone . . . and no interval smaller than these is used in melody, even if many have been ignorant of this.* <note>original Greek: Δίεσίς ἐστι τὸ τρίτον τοῦ τόνου καὶ τὸ τέταρτον... This passage discusses the smallest intervals in ancient Greek music theory.</note> This codex seems to require more accurate investigation. Ruelle-Westph. CI. Om. suppl. 51.

# Page 71
## (145—149) Parisian Manuscripts

In the diagram of Aristides (Book I, 9), within the table of harmonies, the Vatican manuscript agrees (149<sup>a</sup>) with the Venice manuscript regarding the signs for the Dorian 7th C, the Ionian 6th <note>original: "ion. 6<sup>b</sup> <"</note> (where the common reading is $\epsilon$), and the Phrygian 3rd P and 9th $\sqcup$. The work of Aristides is concluded by a <term>scholium</term><note>A marginal note or explanatory comment made by an ancient scholar.</note> by some philosopher. Then, another hand from the same century inscribed several chapters from Theon’s *Exposition on Music*. These are chapters 35 and 36 (the division of the <term>canon</term><note>The "monochord" or mathematical rule used to determine musical intervals.</note> according to Thrasyllus), and chapters 33 and 34 (on analogies), which also exist as excerpts in manuscripts 16, 34, 65, and 165. From this Vatican manuscript, it appears that the following readings should be adopted:

*   Page 87, 9 (Hiller edition): "dividing in two" <note>original: "δίχα μὲν διαιροῦσαν"</note>
*   Line 11: "having a double motion toward the high pitch" <note>original: "διπλασίαν κίνησιν ἔχουσαν ἐπὶ τὸ ὀξύ"</note>
*   Line 12: "as much of the magnitude as you take away"
*   Line 16: omits the <term>proslambanomenos</term> and <term>mese</term> <note>The lowest note and the central note of the Greek musical system; these were likely remnants of a diagram.</note>
*   Page 88, 1: "in three ways"
*   Lines 6 and 7: the additions by Hiller are not recognized.
*   Line 8: "according to the [measure]"
*   Line 10: "and the..."
*   Line 19: "and the <term>nete</term> of the [highest] is..."
*   Page 89, 10: "for let it be the..."
*   Line 15: "it will have"
*   Line 23: "everything"
*   Line 24: omits "the ratio"
*   Page 90, 7: omits "the"
*   Lines 10 and 12: "the [number] which..."
*   Line 17: "the <term>hyperypate</term> is 9 to 8, and the [ratio] of the whole tone is in an <term>epogdoic</term> ratio" <note>An epogdoic ratio is 9:8, the mathematical basis for a whole tone.</note>
*   Line 19: < omits
*   Line 21: "the" instead of "to the"
*   Line 22: "the ratios of the motions are inversely proportional"

-> **On the Condensation of Intervals** <-

*   Chapter 36: "it is condensed"
*   Page 91, 10: "from the <term>nete</term>"
*   Line 12: "from the [highest] itself"
*   Line 23 (end): "this"
*   Line 25: "from it"
*   Page 92, 1: "lower than the <term>nete</term>"
*   Line 7: < omits
*   Line 8: "which is also chromatic... higher"
*   Line 12: "and the ratio into... of the middle [notes] will be the <term>diesis</term> ratio into the complement" <note>A diesis is a small interval, often a quarter-tone or slightly larger.</note>
*   Line 19: "it has happened" (the manuscript adds "ai"). "From this."
*   Page 93, 4: "for it [is] under them"

From the words "it holds thus" (93, 3), the text transitions to Chapter 33, **On Analogies** (page 85, 8). After "being exceeded" (line 9), it supplies "as 6, 9, 12," and "geometric [analogy]" as written by Bullialdus. In line 13 "as" and line 15 "for example" are omitted; after "12" it adds "9, 12, 18."

*   Line 17: "double indeed"
*   Line 19: "the [number] 9"
*   Line 20: "the [number] 8"
*   Line 22: "the [parts] indeed... toward the..."
*   Page 86, 6: "of the [ratio]"
*   Line 13: "one-and-a-half" <note>original: "ἡμιόλια" (sesquialter ratio, 3:2)</note>
*   Lines 21, 24: "they become"
*   Line 23: "thrice these"
*   Line 24: omits "thrice"
*   Page 87, 1: "and of these"

There follows a diagram, then excerpts from Bacchius which Ruelle published in *Archives III II 609*. It begins: "The six species of consonance in the perfect system." After these 11 items from Bacchius are enumerated, it continues: "that there are three modes of the voice" (Bacchius 44). From section 45, it correctly transcribes "standing" <note>original: "στάσις"</note>. From 46, "modes." After "Lydian," the excerptor forgot his plan and retained the word "by how much." Then it reads: "the Phrygian is lower." It correctly has "by a minor third" <note>original: "τριημιτονίῳ"</note> and regarding the Dorian, correctly "by a major third" <note>original: "διτόνῳ"</note>. Then "the Mixolydian by a fourth" and likewise regarding the Hypolydian tone. Regarding the Dorian, it is correct. After "the Mixolydian by four tones," it correctly says "the Hypodorian is lower than the Hypophrygian."

## Concerning manuscript codices.

...by a whole tone, the Hypolydian by a ditone <note>two whole tones</note>, the Dorian by a fourth, the Phrygian by a fifth, and the Lydian by four tones (etc.)—which the Escorial manuscript omitted. A diagram has been added:

->[Diagram of Greek Musical Modes]<-
<note>The diagram illustrates the intervals between seven Greek modes: Hypodorian, Hypophrygian, Hypolydian, Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, and Mixolydian. Intervals of whole tones and semitones are marked between them. Arcs indicate larger harmonic intervals: a "fourth" (diatessaron) spans from Hypodorian to Dorian and from Dorian to Mixolydian; a "fifth" (diapente) spans from Hypodorian to Phrygian.</note>

This is followed by excerpts from Bacchius (sections 48 and 49). Then (from section 11): "The notes indicate these six types of concords <term>symphoniai: musical intervals considered harmonious</term>:"

->The fourth is **Z and Φ¹**<-
->The fifth is **Z and CC**<-
->The octave is **Z and ˙λ²**<-
->The octave plus a fourth is **Z and ℧³**<-
->The octave plus a fifth is **Z and LK.⁴**<-

The scribe omits the final concord, likely because he had already slipped down to the final signs. There follows Bacchius section 12, in the version published by Ruelle. Also included are Ptolemy’s *Harmonics* up to Book III, chapter 14, "unobserved through the things that happened" (which is the ending found in Venice manuscript VI 10) and Plutarch’s *On Music*. # Page 73
### (149—155) Paris Manuscripts.

**Plutarch, Theon, Cleonides** <note>The text notes "sic," indicating the original scribe used this spelling</note>, **Euclid, Gaudentius, Alypius, Bacchius, Dionysius.** (150) (**Pseudo-Bacchius**). — From the readings that Franz noted, I see that in the *Introduction*, this manuscript agrees with the others that attribute the work to Cleonides. Studemund states it was written by the same hand (at the end of the Euclid section) and derived from the same archetype as (173) the Barberini manuscript. However, it contains the "Canon" divided into 13 sections, which the Barberini manuscript seems to omit. Leaving out the parts that the first Barberini hand already contained, it shows corrections at 185, 9 and 200, 4. It is decorated with the coat of arms of Pope Paul III. — Inspected by Bormann.

**Vatican 671.** Cotton paper, 14th century. Contains the *Introduction* of **Pseudo-Euclid** (151) inscribed with the name of **Pappus**. I doubt whether this is the one seen by Possevino, who claimed that booklet for Pappus (see below p. 171 and Montfaucon’s *Library of Libraries* II p. 11^b), or if he possessed the one marked 180 in the Rehdiger index (below p. LXXV in the notes). I believe those I named at no. 118 took their *Introduction* from this one, unless perhaps they used (187) **Venice Marciana 512**.

**Vatican 698.** [**Psellus** or **Gregoras**] A compendium on the mathematical (152) sciences, the readings of which Franz compiled against a text drawn from Vatican 111. However, this Vatican manuscript joins to Xylander’s <term>square</term> <note>original: "τετράγωνον"</note> p. 27, 3 and to the <term>proportional</term> <note>original: "ἀνάλογον"</note> 28, 25 certain other things; likewise, after 29, 23, it inserts what you read in Vincent p. 340: <gloss>“The intermediate things, therefore...”</gloss> <note>original: "τὰ μὲν οὖν ἔμμεσα"</note>. It also contains the passage from Vincent 342: <gloss>“It must be known that regarding the tension...”</gloss> <note>original: "ἰστέον δὲ ὡς ἐπὶ μὲν τῆς τάσεως"</note> here after 30, 23 of Xylander. Finally, the end of that compendium is followed by a longer exposition: <gloss>“It must be known that the diesis <note>a small melodic interval</note> has a ratio of 31:32”</gloss> <note>original: "ἰστέον δὲ ὡς ἡ δίεσις λόγον ἔχει ἐπιτριακοστοδεύτερον"</note> concerning the ratios of intervals (96 : 108 : 121 : 128). From there it passes to geometry. Compare our entry 149^a.

**Vatican 1013.** Cotton paper, 13th century. Folio. Plutarch. [Amatus-Studemund 153]. (153)

**Vatican 1033.** Paper, octavo, 16th century. Under the title **Euclid’s Harmonic Introduction**, it contains that scholarly note: <gloss>“Power is the arrangement of notes...”</gloss> <note>original: "Δύναμις δὲ τάξις φθόγγων"</note> (p. 207, 10) as far as the word <gloss>“treatise.”</gloss> <note>original: "πραγματείας"</note> Euclid’s *Division of the Canon* follows. This scribe surely copied the title and the parts intruded into the Venice manuscript from either (2) **M** or (3) **V** folio 295.

**Vatican 1086.** **Bryennius**. (154^b)

**Vatican 1144.** [**Psellus** or **Gregoras**] A synopsis of the four (155) mathematical sciences <note>The Quadrivium: arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</note>. See also 106, 165, 189, etc.

# On manuscript codices.

<margin>(156)</margin> Vatican Library, MS 1341. Cotton paper <note>original: "bomb." for bombycinus</note>, octavo. 14th–15th Century. Since this codex is said by Nolhac in *The Library of Fulvio Orsini* [Paris 1887] pp. 125 and 337 to be that of Orsini no. 34 and to contain Aristotle’s *Topics*, Aphthonius *on music*(?), and various works by Nicetas of Heraclea—doubting whether Studemund had erred in the codex number, I inquired of Tschiedel whether this volume contained those things which Studemund found. Tschiedel affirmed that it did, and proved another point I had suspected. For that codex belonged to Fulvio Orsini <note>A famous 16th-century Italian humanist and collector</note>, from which Possevino, in his *Selected Library*, translated Euclid’s *Introduction* into Latin and expanded upon it¹ (see below, p. 175). Now, this MS 1341 shows that Possevino used it, because (folios 177ʳ to 179, 9) it has the marginal heading <gloss>concerning the sound and the rest</gloss> <note>original: "περὶ φθόγγου καὶ τῶν λοιπῶν"</note> and at 185, 15 it inserts <insert>the proslambanomenos <term>proslambanomenos: the lowest note in the Greek musical system</term> has therefore been mentioned</insert> <note>original: "προσλαμβανόμενος μὲν οὖν εἴρηται"</note>. While I see that it acts as "M" <note>a specific manuscript family designation</note> in 179, 8, even omitting <gloss>of the tone</gloss> <note>original: "τόνου"</note> along with that other manuscript at 206, 6 <gloss>of the parts</gloss> <note>original: "μερῶν"</note> and so forth, it often differs in small details. It is difficult to judge its nature and worth, because Studemund, thinking it of no value, was satisfied with comparing only the beginning and the end. However, it seems to me to be of the best quality, because at 207, 7 it brings the *Introduction* to an end (cf. chapter 56). Indeed, do not place much value on those additions by Possevino; for since the form <term>paramesos: the note next to the middle note</term> <note>original: "παράμεσος"</note> is read there—a form foreign to the followers of Aristoxenus—and <gloss>unisons</gloss> <note>original: "ὁμόφωνα"</note> (which Aristides Quintilianus says regarding sounds) is perversely applied to intervals, you can understand they are worthless. The Vulcanian codex (55) exhibits the same, and perhaps the Parisian (149) as well; I regret very much that I do not know by what method they end the *Introduction* <note>original: "exagogen," likely a slip for "isagogen"</note>.

---

¹ Possevino expands the text at p. 185, 15 from Aristides Quintilianus p. 10, 6; at p. 187, 2 he adds what Bryennius has concerning the "dense" <term>spisso: the pyknon or 'thickness' of a tetrachord</term> at 384, 33, mixed from Aristides Quintilianus 12, 2 and Aristoxenus 24, 11. In the same place he distinguishes consonant, dissonant, and equal pairs with Aristides Quintilianus 12, 18—13, 7. At p. 187, 18 he adds <gloss>trihemitone</gloss> <note>original: "τριημιτόνιον," an interval of three semitones</note> and the things I have given in the variant readings. At p. 189, 6 he expands from Aristides Quintilianus 13, 30. At p. 190, 11 from Aristides Quintilianus 14, 32. At p. 207, 7 he has: <gloss>Melody-making is the creative power of a song</gloss> <note>original: "ἡ μελοποιΐα ἐστὶ δύναμις κατασκευαστικὴ μέλους"</note> (with Aristides Quintilianus 28, 29), <gloss>and a song is a weaving together of sounds dissimilar in highness and lowness</gloss> <note>original: "μέλος δὲ πλοκὴ φθόγγων ἀνομοίων ὀξύτητι καὶ βαρύτητι"</note> (Aristides Quintilianus 28, 27), and continues with Aristides Quintilianus 28, 30. Instead of his 29, 26, however, he writes: <gloss>Petteia <term>petteia: the art of choosing which sounds to use in a composition</term> is the striking often produced by a single tone</gloss> <note>original: "πεττεία δὲ ἡ ὑφ’ ἑνὸς τόνου πολλάκις γινομένη πλῆξις"</note> (from the *Introduction* itself 207, 4 with Bryennius 503, 4) <gloss>and by which we know</gloss>. After <gloss>representative</gloss> <note>original: "παραστατική"</note> 29, 32 he continues (with Bacchius 302, 22 and Bryennius 503, 9): <gloss>Extension is the dwelling upon a single utterance for a very long time, whenever... they are sung.</gloss> <note>original: "τονὴ δὲ ἔστιν ἡ ἐπὶ πλεῖστον χρόνον μονὴ κατὰ μίαν γινομένη προφορὰν, ὅταν — μελῳδῶνται"</note>. It ends at 30, 25 with <gloss>it was corrected</gloss> <note>original: "διωρθοῦτο"</note>.

# Page 78
## Concerning manuscript codices.

Scribe B wrote out the final leaves. However, the volume seems to have been assembled from various archetypes <note>The original source-manuscripts from which a copy is made</note>. Indeed, Marquard (pages 25–28) believes that the text of Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and the most important ancient writer on music theory</note> was sought from two or more sources, so that the principles or "beginnings" <note>original: "archai" / πρὸ τῶν ἁρμ. στοιχ.</note> agree with the Riccardian and U manuscripts and those parts which were later inserted into M, while the elements themselves were drawn from Vatican manuscript 191. — The doctrine of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer and music theorist</note> is adorned with very many scholia <note>Ancient or medieval marginal notes or explanations</note>, a part of which is written between the lines themselves (<gloss>Definition of harmonics.</gloss> then above <term>power: habit</term>, above <term>high pitch: they are accustomed to call the tension of the highest and lowest notes in music</term>, afterwards <gloss>definition of sound.</gloss> above <term>struck: not coldness or heat, but clearly a vocal striking</term>), and a part in the margin, such as: <margin>hearing, matter, passion</margin>
->reason, form, cause.<-
Another scholion written in the middle of the first page, right under the doctrine itself, begins: <insert>And the present book was corrected, completed, and interpreted by the philosopher Nicephorus Gregoras <note>A 14th-century Byzantine scholar and historian</note>. For the long passage of time, employing the hands of clumsy scribes in the succession of this book, has transferred some parts from certainty into error, and by ignorantly cutting others, has caused them to disappear.</insert> Gregoras therefore promises that he will restore the false and lost parts. Finally, on the third part of the page, interpretations are inscribed: <gloss>He spoke of those things in sounds and not in voices, etc.</gloss> and regarding <term>harmony: That the sense of hearing is not the judge of harmony, but of that which has been harmonized.</term> A most full treasury of scholia also seems to have been gathered here (compare Bb. II 94 and Munich manuscript 200). — Studemund thought this manuscript had little value for emending <note>Correcting or improving a text</note> the words of Plutarch (Amsel 155), even though the copyist had consulted several codices. — From chapter 12 of Theon, he transitions to the introduction <note>original: "isagogen"</note>, which he attributes to Pappus (see page 171 and chapter 118). This part, not examined by Studemund, was transcribed with the greatest diligence by Franz; and since you could apply the readings of the related Parisian manuscript from Cramer’s anecdotes, while the version from Hamburg noted by Kiessling seemed inferior to me<sup>1</sup>, I sought a specimen of this family from the Barberini manuscript. Indeed, one might consider this the father of this class...

1 The Hamburg manuscript omits 183, 9–11.

# (173) Paris Manuscript. 79

The Neapolitan manuscript 2, whose marginal notes are contained within manuscript **B**.
<margin>(173)</margin>
In the main text at 184, 6 and 186, 16. (However, after comparing the works of Euclid <note>The famous Greek mathematician, to whom some musical treatises were attributed.</note> and Gaudentius <note>A Greek music theorist, likely 2nd or 3rd century AD.</note>, this is not confirmed; one should perhaps consider Vatican manuscript 671 or Marcian manuscript 512 instead.) Since the Paris manuscript contains more text at 194, 4 (the other omits the phrase "on the highest of the middle" <note>original: ἐπὶ ὑπ. μεσ.</note>), it cannot be considered its direct descendant. — We come now to the second recension <note>A specific version of a literary text based on critical revision.</note> of the *Introduction* <note>original: isagogae</note>, which this manuscript attributes to Cleonides <note>A follower of Aristoxenus who wrote the "Introduction to Harmonics."</note> along with the *Division of the Canon*. Because manuscript B inscribed this name, and because it has the title *On Sounds* <note>original: περὶ φθόγγων</note> (which manuscript N has written in the margin in red ink, but is omitted in the Paris manuscript), and because it inscribed the heading *Cleonides’ Introduction* on every page until the end of the section, you could bet any amount that these were taken from Valla’s Neapolitan manuscript. This is further supported by a great number of identical readings: *on the system*, *on the pitch* <note>original: π. συστήματος, π. τόνου</note> kept in the correct order; "in genus" <note>original: γένει</note> at 182, 4; the omission of 185, 7–9; "to be roughened" <note>original: τραχυνθῆναι</note> at 188, 2; they deviate together at "of the color" <note>original: χρώματος</note> 189, 16; "to the double" <note>original: δισαίω</note> 21; and the phrase "and called Ionian, of which the lowest..." <note>original: καὶ ἰάστιος καλ. ὧν ὁ βαρτ.</note> inserted in the wrong place at 204, 5. Similarly, in the *Division of the Canon*, they share "not even" <note>original: οὐδὶ</note> 148, "to happen" <note>original: τυγχάνειν</note> 149, 4, "purpose" <note>original: προθέσει</note> 5, and "the second" <note>original: τὸ β</note> 150, 6. And — what is more significant — they agree in those excellent diagrams which I have included in the text. The fact that manuscript B, along with Paris 2535, moved into the main text a line that manuscript N has in the margin ("lowness is..." <note>original: βαρύτης μέν ἐστι</note> placed before "highness" <note>original: ὀξύτης</note>), one might call the strongest proof of their family relationship. Nevertheless, I doubt whether we can say B is a direct descendant of the Neapolitan. For the evidence I cited regarding Paris 2535—concerning errors in figures 6 and 8 and the omission of figure 9—argues too clearly against the authority of N for me to dare call it the "father." Furthermore, since the placement and order of the diagrams are better preserved in B than in P, we are even less able to say this one was copied from N.¹ It remains for us to judge them as "brothers" or "cousins." And if anyone asks whether B was transcribed from Paris 2535, that too must be denied. The title of the section, *On Sounds*, stands in the way, as does the word "happens" <note>original: τυγχάνει</note> 149, 4 in...

---

1 Cf. also "one and a half" <note>original: ἡμιολίαν</note> in B 191, 1, which seems to have been written late in N (N¹ had "similarity" <note>original: ὁμοιολίαν</note>), and "if indeed the order" <note>original: εἴπερ ἡ τάξις</note> 195, 6 (N has "about the axis" <note>original: περὶ ἄξις</note>), and "of the highest strings" <note>original: ὑπάτων</note> 195, 12 (N has a circumflex accent). But if you were to think B is older and call it the "father" of the Neapolitan, know that figure 18 is missing in B but present in N.

# LXXVIII
->On manuscript codices.<-

<margin>(173) (36) Hearing is the matter or passive element; <note>original: ἀκοή ὕλη πάθος</note> Reason is the form or cause. <note>original: λόγος εἶδος αἴτιον</note></margin>
B. <note>Referring to a specific manuscript designated as 'B'</note> wrote out the final leaves. It seems, however, to have been composed from various archetypes <note>The original source texts from which copies are made</note>. Marquard (pages 25–28) believes that the text of **Aristoxenus** <note>A Greek philosopher and the most important ancient writer on music theory</note> was sought from two or more sources, so that the principles or *archai* (the "preliminaries to the elements of harmony") might agree with the Riccardian and U manuscripts and those parts later inserted into M, while the elements themselves were drawn from Vatican manuscript 191. — The doctrine of **Ptolemy** is adorned with very many *scholia* <note>Ancient or medieval marginal notes or comments on a text</note>, a portion of which is written between the lines themselves (such as "Definition of harmonics"; then above *power*: "habit"; above *high pitch*: "they are accustomed to call the tension of the highest and lowest sounds in music"; and later, "Definition of sound." Above *struck*: "not coldness or heat, but clearly a vocal striking"). Another part is in the margin, such as another note written in the middle of the first page under the doctrine itself, which begins: <insert>And the philosopher **Nicephorus Gregoras** <note>A 14th-century Byzantine scholar and historian</note> corrected, completed, and interpreted the present book. For the long passage of time, employing the hands of careless scribes in the succession of this book, transferred some things from certainty into error, and ignorantly cut other things out and made them disappear.</insert> Thus, Gregoras promises that he will restore the false and lost parts. Finally, on the third part of the page, interpretations are inscribed: "He spoke of those things in sounds and not in voices," etc., and regarding *harmonies*: "That sense-perception is not the judge of harmony, but of that which has been harmonized." A very full treasury of notes also seems to have been gathered here (compare Bb. II 94 and Mon. 200). — Studemund thought this manuscript was of little value for emending the words of **Plutarch** (Amsel 155), even though the scribe had consulted several codices. — From **Theon** chapter 12, it transitions to the *Isagoge* <note>An introductory text or treatise</note>, which he attributes to **Pappus** (see page 171 and chapter 118). Franz transcribed this part, which had not been examined by Studemund, with the greatest diligence; and since you could use the readings of the related Parisian manuscript from Cramer’s *Anecdota*, while the version from Hamburg noted by Kiessling seemed inferior to me^1, I sought a specimen of this family from the Barberini manuscript. Indeed, one might consider the father of this lineage to be—

1 H <note>The Hamburg manuscript</note> omits 183, 9–11.

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Greek musical treatises, specifically focusing on the manuscript traditions of Cleonides and the "Division of the Canon." It compares the Parisinus (P) and Neapolitanus (N) codices with the Barberini (B) manuscript.</meta>

# (173) Parisinus. 79

The Neapolitan manuscript 2, which contains marginal notes that Manuscript B includes in its main text at 184, 6 and 186, 16. (However, after comparing Euclid <note>The author refers to the "Division of the Canon" often attributed to Euclid</note> and Gaudentius <note>A Greek music theorist, likely 2nd or 3rd century AD</note>, this is not confirmed; one might rather consider Vatican 671 or Marcianus 512.) Since the Parisinus contains more text at 194, 4 (the other omits <note>original: "ἐπὶ ὑπ. μεσ."</note> *on the sub-principal of the middle strings*), it cannot be considered its "son" <note>In manuscript studies, a "son" is a direct copy of a "parent" manuscript</note>. — We come now to the second recension of the *Introduction* <note>original: "isagogae"</note>, which this manuscript attributes to Cleonides <note>A follower of Aristoxenus who wrote the "Harmonic Introduction"</note> along with the *Division of the Canon*. Because Manuscript B inscribed this name, and because it has the title *On Sounds* <note>original: "περὶ φθόγγων"</note> (which Manuscript N has written in the margin in red ink <margin>(173)</margin> — though it is omitted in the Parisinus), and because it inscribed *Cleonides' Introduction* <note>original: "Κλεονείδου εἰσαγωγή"</note> on every page until the end of the section, you could bet any stake that these were taken from Valla’s Neapolitan manuscript. This is supported by a great number of identical readings: *on the system*, *on the tone* preserved in the correct order, *in the genus* at 182, 4, the omission at 185, 7–9, *to be roughened* at 188, 2; they deviate at *of the color* 189, 16, *two-tone* 21, and the phrase *and called Ionian of which the lowest...* inserted in the wrong place at 204, 5. Similarly, in the *Division of the Canon*, they share *neither* 148, *to happen* 149, 4, *prefix* 5, and *the second* 150, 6. And — what is more significant — they agree in those excellent diagrams which I have included in the text. Furthermore, the fact that Manuscript B, along with Parisinus 2535, moved into the main line of text what Manuscript N has in the margin (the phrase *low pitch is* <note>original: "βαρύτης μέν ἐστι"</note> placed before *high pitch* <note>original: "ὀξύτης"</note>), you might call the strongest proof of family and close relationship.

However, I doubt whether we can say Manuscript B is a "son" of the Neapolitanus. For the points I raised regarding Parisinus 2535 concerning the errors in figures 6 and 8 and the omission of figure 9 argue more clearly against the authority of N than I would dare to call it the "father." And since the placement and order of the diagrams are better preserved in this Manuscript B than in P, we are even less able to derive this one from N.1 It remains for us to judge them as "brothers" or "cousins." If anyone asks whether B was transcribed from Parisinus 2535, this too must be denied. The title of the section *On Sounds* stands in the way, as does the word *to happen* at 149, 4 in—

---

1 Cf. also *one and a half* <note>original: "ἡμιολίαν"</note> in B 191, 1, which seems to have been written that way late in N (the first hand of N wrote *similar* <note>original: "ὁμοιολίαν"</note>), and *if indeed the order* 195, 6 (*about the axis* in N), and *of the sub-principals* 195, 12 (N uses a circumflex accent). But if you were to think B is older and call it the "father" of the Neapolitanus, know that figure 18 is missing in B but present in N.

# LXXX
-> Concerning the manuscript codices. <-

B. P <note>Referring to specific manuscript sigla, likely the Barberini and Parisinus codices</note>, omits that *one-and-a-half* <note>original: "ἡμιόλιος"</note> but the *d z* <note>musical notation signs</note> 154, 18. Not even the Parisinus seems to have originated from B (173); for B omits *in* <note>original: "ἐν"</note> 149, 22, and has *let it become* <note>original: "ἐγενήσθω"</note> 150, 5.

The text of Cleonides <note>Author of "Introduction to Harmonics," 2nd c. CE</note> was later corrected according to the authority of Vatican 191 or a very similar copy. For everything omitted there, you see here deleted in red color; those things read differently there are changed here in the same color. For example, 183, 26 *parhypate meson* <note>The "next-to-lowest" note of the middle tetrachord</note> omitted in the first hand of B is written in the margin; 184, 1 *enharmonic* added in the text, likewise lines 5 and 9; then (to say nothing of the most intricate passage 184, 15) 185, 7 *enharmonic*; 8 *paranete diezeugmenon chromatic*, *paranete diezeugmenon diatonic* <note>Technical names for specific notes in the Greek tonal system</note> marked with a red underline; 185, 18 *as many as* omitted, corrected to *they are*; 25 *of these* added in the margin; 186, 11 in place of *third*: *of the*. Even the *Division of the Canon* <note>A mathematical treatise on musical intervals attributed to Euclid</note> seems to have been emended according to the Vatican authority in these places: 149, 19; 151, 9; 152, 1; 156, 7.

In the *Art* of Aristides <note>Aristides Quintilianus, author of "On Music"</note>, since the first diagram preserved two rows of signs well but had the third row confused and disturbed, you might think these were transcribed from Venice VI 10¹, and since B substitutes another in place of this diagram expressed according to the Venice 322 copy²: you will be reminded of Parisinus 2532, which joins two forms of the diagram in the same manner. However, the context of the words which we know from Amsterdam-Studemund p. 128 recedes far from the Venice manuscript, as:

| 2, 2 | *of the art and that*, | *and that of the art* B, |

19, 34 after *of a semitone* V errs until 20, 1; B has everything.

# Page 81
-> (173—175) Rome — Turin <-

The earlier readings and corrected words agree with the Hamburg manuscript.¹ — <margin>(173)</margin> Likewise, in the work of Bacchius <note>Bacchius the Elder, a 4th-century author of a musical primer</note>, the Barberini and Hamburg manuscripts always harmonize; the archetype is Parisinus 2532. Leo Allatius collated Alypius <note>Author of a Greek treatise on musical notation</note> for the use of Meibom. Studemund also examined a portion (up to page 14), yet did not find many or significant differences. In Gaudentius <note>Author of the "Harmonic Introduction"</note>, since the Barberini manuscript contains those three diagrams, it shows itself again to be closely related to the manuscripts Naples 2 and Hamburg 36. For they agree in the reading "the" (omitting "again") at 328, 19; "tension" at 329, 8; "and he says" at 330, 3; "diesis" <term>diesis: a small musical interval, often a quarter-tone</term> at 331, 11; and they coincide in the musical notes and in the diagrams, so that you might seem to have proven for certain that this was transcribed from Valla’s manuscript. But here too, two passages occur that hinder this conclusion; for at 347, 7, a gap is visible in the Naples manuscript which is filled in the Barberini, and at 353, 21 the Naples manuscript omits "drawn down" <note>original: "καθειλυσμένον"</note> which is written in the Barberini! And one might easily think the very recent Hamburg manuscript was transcribed from the Barberini, but even this cannot be conceded. For its earlier readings sometimes shrink away from this example, while the things corrected there match the Barberini in almost every instance. —

Since these things are so, it appears that this manuscript made use of many sources, of which we know scarcely one or two. — It formerly belonged to Marcello of Santa Croce, whose coat of arms it contains. — I inspected it myself; Studemund collated the greater part.

**Barberini II 94.** Paper, folio. Contains Ptolemy and other works. <margin>(174)</margin>

In the Roman College <note>The Collegio Romano, a Jesuit educational institution in Rome</note> there formerly existed a manuscript from the year 1400. It contained Aristides Quintilianus, Ptolemy, Porphyry, Bryennius, Plutarch, Euclid, Aristoxenus, Alypius, Gaudentius, and Nicomachus II. It is not found there now; I searched for it in vain in the Victor Emmanuel Library.

Do not look for the Farnese manuscript in Rome with Jahn (number 49); see above at number 78 and below on page 456.

In Turin <note>original: "Taurinorum Augustae"</note> in manuscript 103 of the Athenaeum library <margin>(175)</margin> the first and second books of Bryennius are said to exist. (Catalogue page 193. Fabricius, *Bibliotheca Graeca* III 635, Harles edition).

# Page 83

(176—184) Trajan — Venice LXXXIII

On the works of Ptolemy and Porphyry in books I and II, the following was inscribed after the index in the hand of Bessarion himself$^1$: <note>original Greek: Βιβλίον ἄριστον καὶ δυσεύρετον, κτῆμα Βησσαρίωνος καρδηναλέως Νικαίας, ἐπισκόπου Σαβίνων</note> "An excellent and rare book, the property of Bessarion, Cardinal of Nicaea, Bishop of Sabina." Since this distinguished man was named Bishop of Sabina in the year 1449$^2$, but was afterwards called Bishop of Tusculum—a title he preferred to declare even in the inscriptions of his books$^1$—one might suspect the codex <note>A "codex" is a bound manuscript book.</note> was completed in that very year. It is clearly evident that the codex was not written after the year 1472, the year in which Bessarion died. It was written, however, by Joannes Rhosus, a priest from Crete, who is said to have transcribed a great many books for the use of both Bessarion and the Medici family.$^3$

And in the first diagram of Aristides (Book I, chapter 7, page 15 in Meibom's edition), the manuscript **U** begins with the letter alpha and that cross which many of the other family of manuscripts have omitted; and having omitted that **B** which opens the second part in the Venice manuscript (VI 10), it has well preserved the order of the final lines. Indeed, it extends the fourth and seventh lines (which contain the numbers) further than is proper, so that the numbers are separated from their signs. You can see this form designated from the Wolfenbüttel codex in Bellermann’s *Musical Scales*, plate 5. For sign 5$^b$ they have **Ↄ** (the Venice manuscript has **Π**, the Vatican has **⋂**), sign 6$^b$ is **σ**, 24$^b$ is **γ** or something similar (the Venice manuscript has **λ**), the signs for 30 are **ε** and **϶**, and even if the middle stroke is prolonged, it never becomes <unclear>⋲</unclear>; after 34$^a$ and $^b$ no spaces are visible. Regarding the words mixed into the diagram, see (11) the Bologna manuscript. However, regarding the second diagram or the harmonies (which Aristides records in...

# LXXXIV

## On manuscript codices.

<margin>(184)</margin>
(U. I 9 p. 22 M) The order in manuscript U is disturbed. It begins in such a way that in the first line one reads <note>original: "α λυδιστί, β δωριστί"</note> **a) Lydian mode, b) Dorian mode**, followed by a very long series of 24 or 27 musical signs, just as you see described from the Escorial manuscript in Bellermann’s fifth table. <note>Friedrich Bellermann (1795–1874) was a key scholar of ancient Greek music notation.</note> The first signs are these: ρ⁻ γ; then there are curved lines in the 7th Dorian note (C instead of E), the 7th Lydian, the 5th Dorian (C instead of <), likewise the 5th Phrygian; in the 1st Mixolydian all of these have the lowercase form ς, and they omit the 2nd Mixolydian. However, what is most quickly discovered is that these manuscripts provide the letter E as the last sign of the Ionian mode (whereas the others correctly show <). Therefore, anyone who examines Bellermann on page 67 finds that many things agree in manuscripts U, Esc., Guelf., Lips., and Vind., which differ from the Neapolitan (and Venice VI 10) manuscripts. Furthermore, manuscript U has a marginal note on page 6 M <note>original: "τέχνην αὐτὴν καλεῖ"</note> <gloss>he calls it the art itself</gloss>, and the same is found in the Leipzig (44), Hamburg (41), and Munich (65 and 71) manuscripts, while others have it within the main text itself (38, 84, 92, 114).

The scribe Rhosus <note>Joannes Rhosus, a famous 15th-century Greek calligrapher.</note> sought the *Division of the Canon* <note>A mathematical treatise on musical intervals attributed to Euclid.</note> from the Marcianus manuscript VI 3. Just as you might suspect this from the title attributed to Zosimus, you can also see it from the forms of the words¹ and the diagrams. For since we see all the diagrams—both those inscribed by the first hand within the text and those drawn in the margin of M by a second scribe—collected and repeated in U, you will easily concede that a close relationship exists between them. Moreover, U has that figure marked ηδβ (from the second hand of M) at the first proposition, and a little later another which you see in the variant readings at 151, 1 (from the first hand of M); to demonstrate the third proposition, U contains both a diagram from the second hand of M split into two parts and those traces of the figure from the first hand of M (152, 3. 7).

Rhosus also copied Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a Pythagorean mathematician and music theorist.</note> from this same source. For they agree in the title <note>original: "Πυθαγορικοῦ, ἁρμονικῆς, ἐγχειρίδιον"</note> **Pythagorean Manual of Harmonics**, in the word **planets** <note>original: "πλανήτας"</note> at 236, 7, they omit the article **the** <note>original: "τὴν"</note> at 31, and they both have **beside** <note>original: "παρὰ"</note> at 237, 8, **burden** <note>original: "φορτίον"</note> at 12, **it is to me** <note>original: "ἔστί μοι"</note> at 16, **connected** <note>original: "συναφὴς"</note> at 244, 14, **casting out** <note>original: "ἐκβαλών"</note> at 246, 20, **diagonal** <note>original: "διαγωνίου"</note> (fourth hand of M) at 22, **separating** <note>original: "διιστῶσαν"</note> at 260, 1 and—what seems a great matter—each presents that book which Meibomius numbers as Book II. In M, indeed, that **Manual of Nicomachus...** seems rather to be a subscription to the preceding sections;

***

¹ **To be said** <note>original: "Λέγεσθαι"</note> 149, 9; **dissonant** <note>original: "διαφόνους"</note> 18, 20; **multiple** <note>original: "πολλαπλάσιον"</note> 150, 8; **multiple or superparticular** <note>original: "πολλαπλάσιος ἢ ἐπιμόριος"</note> 154, 4; **the half** <note>original: "τὸν ἥμισυν"</note> 155, 15.

# (184) Venetus. LXXXV

Because Rhosus <note>Joannes Rhosus, a famous 15th-century Greek scribe</note> mistakenly took this for the title of that part *u.* which follows, I believe he was the author of the error.¹ Add to this that (184) 249, 11 in Meibom's edition <note>original: "M"; referring to Marcus Meibom's 1652 collection of ancient music theorists</note> contains several readings from a correction, while the manuscript U joins them all together.² We shall therefore suppose that the doctrines of Euclid and Nicomachus were transcribed from the Marcian manuscript VI 3; nor does it seem that any other conclusion should be reached regarding the *Art* of Aristoxenus, which Marquard (p. XXV) found to be in agreement with the Marcian manuscript as emended by the 3rd or 4th hand. 

Now, you might perhaps think that the *Introduction* <note>original: "isagogen"</note>, whose author is uncertain, flowed from the same source. But in this little book, manuscripts U and M differ widely from one another, and U follows that part of the Vatican 191 which I have designated as W. For it agrees in the phrase *having the order of things composed* <note>original Greek: "τάξιν τῶν συγκειμένων ἔχον"</note> on page 179; it agrees in the arrangement of the material and the plural *of the systems of tones* <note>original Greek: "συστημάτων τόνων"</note>; it agrees at 180, 19 with the word *is placed* <note>original Greek: "τιθεῖται"</note> and whatever I have noted as WL; almost everything I noted on page 181 agrees, including that strange phrase *by themselves* <note>original Greek: "καθ᾿ αὑτῶν"</note>; what I noted on pages 183 and 185, 25 agree; nor do they differ regarding that fifth and sixth consonance, which some fool <note>The author is criticizing a previous scribe's musical theory</note> thought he had discovered in the "disjunct system" <note>original: "synemmeno systemate"; a technical term in Greek music theory regarding tetrachords</note> at 194, 20. 

It appears, therefore, that Rhosus took these things from a certain source very close to that W. Whether he had W itself before his eyes, I strongly doubt, for there are points of difference³; nor are the compared passages sufficient for the matter to be decided with certainty. — Regarding Gaudentius, although all the manuscripts are very closely related to each other, U nevertheless approaches the Vatican 192 more closely than the Neapolitan manuscript. Diagrams are absent from the latter, and while N has *the* <note>original Greek: "τὸ"</note> at 328, 19, in V and U you find *again the* <note>original Greek: "αὖ τὸ"</note>; at 239, 8 these have *position* <note>original Greek: "στάσις"</note>, at 12 *is* <note>original Greek: "ἐστιν"</note>, at 330, 3 *it might be said* <note>original Greek: "λέγοιτ᾽"</note>, at 11 *concerning systems* <note>original Greek: "π. συστημάτων"</note>, at 342, 9 N has *remainder* <note>original Greek: "λεῖμμα"</note> while VU have *limma* <note>original Greek: "λίμμα"; a technical term for a small musical interval</note>, at 345, 4 VU have *themselves* <note>original Greek: "αὖτοὶ"</note> while N has *these* <note>original Greek: "οὗτοι"</note>, and at 351, 1 you have the same form of abbreviation for—

---

1 The Bern manuscript indeed fell into the same error when it added to the title *Handbook. Book 2.* <note>original Greek: "ἐγχειρίδιον. βιβλίον β."</note> However, the archetype of this seems to have originally had only the true words *By the same Nicomachus* <note>original Greek: "τοῦ αὑτοῦ Νικομάχου"</note>.

2 Also in other places, U recognizes almost all the emendations of the old Marcian manuscript, such as at 237, 16 and 244, 3 (though not the word *the* <note>original Greek: "τὴν"</note> at 236, 11).

3 They differ at *is* <note>original Greek: "ἐστίν"</note> 179, 6 (U has *are* <note>original Greek: "εἰσίν"</note>), *differing* <note>original Greek: "διαφέρον"</note> 181, 3 which U seems to omit, *tones* <note>original Greek: "τόνοι"</note> 195, 20 (U has *tonal* <note>original Greek: "τονιαῖα"</note>), and at 196, 5 U has *secondly, that of which the middle is the tone (corrected to: of the tones) the high pitch. Thirdly, that of which the first is toward the high pitch of the tones.* <note>original Greek: "δεύτερον δὲ οὗ μέσον τὸν τόνον (corr. τῶν τόνων) τὸ ὀξύ. τρίτον οὗ πρῶτον ἐπὶ τὸ ὀξὺ τῶν τόνων."</note>

# Page 86
## Concerning the handwritten codices.

<margin>(184)</margin> <note>The author is comparing specific Greek word variants across different manuscripts.</note> The word *barytaten* <note>original: "βαρυτάτην" - meaning "the lowest" or "deepest" pitch</note> is found in manuscripts V and U; *megalou* <note>original: "μεγάλου" - "great"</note> in V and U; *tetartou* <note>original: "τετάρτου" - "fourth"</note> in manuscript N. Even the forms of the accents are the same in V and U; for instance, in the particle *te* <note>original: "τέ"</note> they leave the tone as it is (328, 21), or they transfer it to the preceding word (24). They write *hypatōn* <note>original: "ὑπατῶν" - referring to the "hypate" or lowest string/note</note> at 332, 11 and 336, 9 (for both the *hypate* and *parhypate* positions) and 352, 6, but *hypatōn* <note>original: "ὑπάτων"</note> at 332, 14-15 and 336, 9 (for the *lichanos* position) and often elsewhere, whereas manuscript N almost always writes it with a circumflex accent. It is indeed strange that manuscript V does not have musical notation from page 353, 10 onwards, while U contains it until the end. But Cardinal Bessarion <note>Johannes Bessarion (1403–1472), a Greek scholar and Catholic cardinal who preserved many ancient texts</note>, a man most skilled in this art and learning, could easily have supplied the missing signs from another codex, perhaps even from the tables of Alypius <note>A Greek writer on music known for his tables of musical notation</note>. I believe, therefore, that he transcribed those parts of Gaudentius <note>A Greek music theorist</note> from V 192, and then added what seemed to be missing from another book. Furthermore, at page 351, 10, where a second hand had supplied the word *echei* <note>original: "ἔχει" - "it has"</note> in V, the scribe Rhosus <note>Johannes Rhosus, a famous 15th-century Greek calligrapher</note> received it into the main text.

In the books of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the astronomer and music theorist</note>, this manuscript has diagrams that differ from the figures in other copies. At Chapter 1, he paints the following:

->**[Diagram of Harmonic Ratios]**<-
| | | |
| :--- | :---: | ---: |
| **Approximate** <note>original: "σύνεγγυς"</note> | | **Accurate** <note>original: "ἀκριβές"</note> |
| | **Ratio / Reason** <note>original: "λόγος" (Logos). The diagram shows the word split into "Lo-" and "-gos" along diagonal lines.</note> | |
| **Number** <note>original: "ἀριθμός"</note> | | **Sensation** <note>original: "αἴσθησις"</note> |
| **Inventive** <note>original: "εὑρετικόν"</note> | | **Receptive** <note>original: "παραδεκτικόν"</note> |

At Chapter 2, he arranges the four families of musical precepts in a certain schema; at Chapter 3, he distinguishes the types of percussion, and other things of this sort. He also adds the supplements that Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Gregoras, a Byzantine scholar</note> provided for Book III. He presents Porphyry <note>A philosopher who wrote a commentary on Ptolemy's Harmonics</note> for Books I and II.

Bessarion wished other learned men to share in the use of this codex, which he valued so highly. This is apparent from the words inscribed in the Munich manuscript 104 <note>original: "Monacensi 104"</note>, and it is also clear from the readings¹ of codices 7, 11, 14, 17, 20, and 40.

---

1 There are also places where this is apparent from the shape of the letters. For instance, where the word *enestai* <note>original: "ἐνέσται" - "it will be in"</note> in Nicephorus 249, 6 is written with a letter *n* <note>ν</note> larger than usual, the manuscripts in the Ambrosian, Munich, and the two Leipzig collections have *egestai* <note>original: "ἐγέσται"</note> or similar errors. The copies made from the Venice manuscript are also easily recognized in Euclid 155, 6, where Rhosus skipped from one line to the next, and in Nicephorus 249, 11, where this manuscript U has:
