<meta>This page continues the analysis of the manuscript tradition of ancient musical and mathematical texts, specifically focusing on the relationship between the Venice manuscript (U) and its various descendants or "children."</meta>

(184) ... LXXXVII
->The Venice Manuscript<-

41a. 43. 44. 50. 51. 58. 59. 61. 65. 72. 80. 84—86. U.
92. 104. 114. 115. 170. 193. 199 (cf. 174. 198. 206) (184)
which have drawn either all their contents or a part from U <note>The Venice manuscript, Marcianus App. cl. VI, 3</note>, so that the reading of this manuscript may rightly be called the "vulgate" <note>The common or standard version of a text</note>.^1 However, so that we may better understand the order and age of those "children" <note>The later copies derived from the parent manuscript</note>, we must observe what has been corrected in U. There are, in fact, emendations made in red ink <note>original: "minio"</note> according to the authority of the Vatican manuscript W or something closely related to it:

1. In Euclid page 152, 8: the letter *eta* (in *ēz*) <note>original: "η (in ηζ)"</note> was deleted.
2. 152, 9: at "the remaining unit" <note>original: "λοιπὸν μονάς"</note>, the note "and since" <note>original: "καὶ ἐπεὶ"</note> was written above.
3. 152, 10: the *eta* <note>original: "η"</note> was deleted from *dē* <note>original: "δη"</note>.
4. 152, 11: after *dz* <note>original: "δζ"</note> there was added in the margin <margin>written as: "and 14, 17, 19"</margin> <note>original: "γρ. καὶ ιδ, ιζ, ιθ"</note>.
5. 155, 16: after "the" <note>original: "οἱ"</note>, the letter *alpha* <note>original: "α"</note> was added above.
6. 165, 19: "middle" (singular) <note>original: "μέσον"</note> was corrected to "middles" (plural) <note>original: "μέσων"</note>.

Since the Leipzig manuscript <note>Lipsiensis</note> knows only one of these corrections (5), the Berlin manuscript three (2, 5, 6), the Bologna manuscript four (1, 3, 5, 6), the Munich manuscript five (1—5), and the Paris manuscript 2456 five (2—6), one might suspect that the first was transcribed in ancient times, and the others in more recent times. However, another matter corrected in ink in U, which only the Munich manuscript knows (namely, that the *delta* <note>original: "δ"</note> should be deleted from *dz* <note>original: "δζ"</note> at 152, 9), would suggest that this Munich manuscript, which was written in the year 1552, is the newest of all. And he who examined the emended passages of Gaudentius <note>An ancient Greek music theorist</note> says that the Ambrosian manuscript is older than the Bolognese. From a scholium <note>An ancient marginal commentary</note> of Aristides page 6, 12 M, one may conclude with Caesar of Marburg that the Leipzig manuscript is certainly to be preferred to the Wolfenbüttel and Oxford manuscripts (*Rhythmics* 38). Finally, all things considered (44), we shall consider the Leipzig manuscript the "best and greatest" <note>original: "optimum et maximum"</note> of the children;^2 similar to this one seem to be

"the *eta* becomes a system through five" <note>original: "τὸ η διὰ πέντε γίνεται σύστημα"</note> and there is added in the margin "concordance" <note>original: "συμφωνία"</note>. And 251, 8 "to be exceeded" <note>original: "ὑπερέχεσθαι"</note>, 252, 5 "they declared" <note>original: "ἀποφαίνοντο"</note>, 255, 14 "tongue diphthong" <note>original: "γλῶσσι δίφθογγος"</note>. The order of the teachings is the same in 11, 20, 114. cf. 115.

***

1 Bellermann, in *Scales* <note>original: "Tonleitern"</note> p. 61, saw that the Aristides texts of manuscripts 20, 40, 44, and 201 flowed from a single source. Caesar, in *Principles of Rhythmics* <note>original: "Grundzüge der Rhythmik"</note> 38, demonstrates that manuscripts 40, 65, 72, 84, and 92 are closest to the Leipzig manuscript (44).

2 Even at 164, 11 where U formerly had "the therefore the" <note>original: "ὁ ἄρα ὁ"</note>, this Leipzig manuscript preserves the ancient reading, while the others found the passage already corrected; nevertheless, the Leipzig manuscript did see the corrections in 194, 18 "for" <note>original: "γὰρ"</note> and 196, 5 "of the tones" <note>original: "τῶν τόνων"</note>.

u. Ambrosian (59) and Berlin (7), while Bologna (11) occupies roughly a middle position.
(184) The youngest in age seem to be Munich (65), written in the year 1552, and Paris (114).
(185) **Venice, Marciana Library 332.** Paper, quarto. 15th century. Barlaam the Logician's <note>Barlaam of Calabria, a 14th-century monk and mathematician</note> refutation of three chapters (15, 16, 17) from Book III of Ptolemy's *Harmonics*. Zanetti 152. <note>Zanetti refers to the 1740 catalog of Greek manuscripts in Venice</note>
(186) **Venice, Marciana Library 333.** Paper, octavo. 15th century. Letters of Bessarion <note>Cardinal Bessarion, a key figure in bringing Greek learning to Italy</note>. Also Pediasimus' *Supervisions* (beginning: "A sound is..." as in Vincent page 290). Zanetti 152.
(187) **Venice, Marciana Library 512.** Paper, octavo. 13th century. Maximus the Sophist on insoluble objections. Also Psellus' *The Generation of the Soul* (Vincent 316). Theon's *Exposition* (partial, compare Riccardian 41) with Pappus' *Introduction*, if we trust Vitelli in *Italian Studies* II 498. Zanetti 275.
(188) **Venice, Marciana Library 516.** Paper, small folio. 12th century. Ptolemy's *Geography*. Heron's *Pneumatics* <note>original: "spiritalia," referring to Heron of Alexandria's works on air pressure and engines</note>. Zanetti 278.
(189) **Venice, Marciana Library 521.** Paper, quarto. 14th century. Plutarch's *Opinions of the Philosophers*, and others. (Gregory's) compendia on arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy. Zanetti 279.
(190) **Venice, Marciana Library 524.** Paper, quarto. 14th century. Prodromus' *Drama of the Mice* <note>The "Galeomyomachia," a parody of Greek tragedy</note>. Other works in which there is much by Psellus. Includes the letter "Whether as a flute-player" and those items which I numbered 2–4, 9, and 10 in the notes on page LXXXII regarding astronomy (beginning: "Let it be declared by me.") Zanetti 282.
(191) **Venice, Marciana Library 529.** Paper, quarto. 14th century. Pollux's *Onomasticon* <note>A thesaurus of Greek terms</note>, and others. Joseph Rhacendyta's *On the Contemplative Life* (beginning: "To me then"). 140 iambic verses, which briefly indicate what is taught in the following arts (beginning: "Seeing the spectator, a cheapened book.") Edited by Walz, *Rhetoric* III 473. (Ending: "...knowing... and also the Master who created these things. Remember, O friend, Joseph Rhacendyta, who composed this with the greatest labor.") There follow compendia of the arts of rhetoric, logic, and physics; excerpts from Aristotle's *On the Soul* and other books; then compendia of the four arts <term>quadrivium: arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy</term> (beginning: "As I am about to [speak] concerning the four..." as in Xylander page 1), on virtue, and others. "Not all the aforementioned works bear Rhacendyta's name, yet it is gathered from the iambic verses above that he is the author," Zanetti 236. Against whom, see entry (106) Paris 2448. Compare also 25c, 135, 192, 202.

# On manuscript codices. — Supplements.

(204) Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar</note> on the four mathematical sciences. Manuscript number 96.
(205) Vienna 220. Paper, quarto, ancient. Aristotle's *On the Soul*, and others; Ptolemy. Manuscript number 118.
(206) Vienna 268. Paper, quarto, moderately old. Nicomachus books I and II, Gaudentius. Manuscript number 173. —

Add that a copy of Psellus is said to exist in the Ambrosian Library in Milan. See Montfaucon, *Library of Manuscripts*, volume I, page 502 A., and Fabricius, *Greek Library*, volume III, page 96.

The notebooks of Langbaine <note>Gerard Langbaine, 17th-century provost and scholar</note> regarding Aristides Quintilianus (from miscellany 85), Euclid, and Porphyry (Magdalen College codex 13). See Coxe, *Bodleian Catalog*, page 878, number 3.

From the collection of Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibom, editor of ancient musical texts</note>, there exist at Leiden <note>original: "Lugduni Batavorum"</note>, besides codex (54) 133: excerpts from Aristides Quintilianus (Leiden 129); Alypius, Aristoxenus, and Nicomachus books I and II written in Meibomius's own hand (ibid. 130); Bryennius, which Selden arranged to be transcribed from the Oxford library and Langbaine compared with the Magdalen codex (ibid. 131); and a copy of the same book (132); Porphyry with fragments of scholia <note>ancient or medieval marginal commentaries</note>, which Meibomius seems to have transcribed from that Leiden codex 133 (134. Geel Catalog 38—40). At Leiden (111) there also exist parts of Aristides Quintilianus excerpted from the Laurentian codex; these formerly belonged to Gevartius (Geel 32).

Furthermore, at Utrecht are preserved the "Notes and musical characters according to the diatonic genus" written by Meibomius. 7 tables. Omont, *Catalog of Manuscripts of the Netherlands*, page 24, number 10 (45). — Regarding the notebooks of Meibomius, see page XXXVII, note 1.

### Supplements.

To item 11. The Bologna manuscripts are described by Olivieri in *Italian Studies in Philology*, volume III, page 396. Also, Bologna 2048 contains in part III Hero's *On Spirals* and similar matters.

To item 12. 16th Century. Bologna 2432, written by two scribes (folios 1—10 and 11 following), contains Alcinous's *Introduction to Plato*, Theon from the word "destructive" (page 48, line 1) to "breath" (page 57, line 5). Pappus's *Introduction*, Aristoxenus, and the Pseudo-Ptolemaic *Music* (= the Naples excerpt "The beginning of musical ratios" — "it contains it and the [ratio]" page 269, line 1). In section 19 it has a scholion: "the tone is divided into 12, and the fourth into 30." <note>original Greek: ὁ τόνος εἰς ιβ διαιρεῖται, τὸ δὲ διὰ τεσσάρων εἰς τριάκοντα</note>. It has "there are three" at 417, 12 and "upon these is the [ratio]" at line 18. Page 420, line 7 "they are / it is." Line 19 "it is reduced."

# Addenda. XCI

"These are the numbers from which..." <note>original: "Εἰσὶν οἱ ἀριθμοὶ, ἐξ ὧν"</note> everything appears as it does in (76) Naples 2, but it omits the *hormasian* <note>likely a reference to a specific musical or rhythmic notation system</note>. There follows Barlaam’s preface to the art of logistics <note>original: "πολλῶν μὲν" — Barlaam of Calabria was a 14th-century monk and mathematician</note> and a fragment of the art as found in N. — Next follows Gemistus <note>Gemistus Pletho, a Neoplatonist philosopher</note> on the dispute between Aristotle and Plato, and Alypius (at 406, 12), which are doctrines sought from elsewhere. However, you can see that Cleonides’ *Introduction* and his *Canon* were drawn from that Naples manuscript (N) based on the inscribed name.

->At Cambridge, the following exist in the University Library:<-

**Cambridge 1463 (Gg II 33).** Paper, quarto. 15th or 16th century. (13^b) *Mathematics. Hero’s Pneumatics.* — It belonged to Giorgio Valla and Alberto Pio, as did manuscript 73. — Catalog of manuscripts vol. III (Cambridge 1858) p. 58.

**Cambridge 1464 (Gg II 34).** Paper, small folio. 16th or 15th century. (13^c) *Cleonides (so written), Euclid’s title "On Sounds"* <note>original: "π. φθόγγων"</note> p. 148 as in N. — Catalog p. 63.

**Cambridge 2048 (Kk V 6).** Paper, folio. 16th century. Aristides Quintilianus is present. (13^d) *J. B. Hautin’s excerpts from Bryennius and Aristides Quintilianus.* — Catalog p. 676.

**Cambridge 2068 (Kk V 26).** Paper, small folio. 15th or 16th century. (13^e) *Aristoxenus, whose readings are not far removed from the Selden manuscript (82).* Catalog p. 694.

**Cambridge 2070 (Kk V 28).** Paper, small folio. 15th century. *Nicomachus.* (13^f) *Two books on Arithmetic and Harmonics.* Catalog p. 697.

**Laurentian 58, 20.** Cotton paper <term>bomb. (bombycinus): a type of paper made from silk or cotton common in the Byzantine era</term>, quarto. 15th century. *Joseph Rhacendytes’* (25^b) *compendium of rhetoric; compendium of logic by Gregory (Nicephorus the monk, surnamed Blemmydes, wrote a summary in almost the same words); and other works.* "A concise collection of the sciences" <note>original: "Ἄθροισις εὐσύνοπτος τῶν μαθημάτων"</note>. *Arithmetic, music* ("The ancients [defined] music..." <note>original: "Μουσικὴν οἱ παλαιοί"</note> — "as a cube and a pyramid"), *geometry, astronomy.* "Gregory wrote these things" <note>original: "Γρηγόριος τὰ δ’ ἔγραψε"</note>. Bandini vol. II p. 459.

**Laurentian 87, 10.** Paper, quarto. 14th century. *Aristotle’s Physics, Synopsis* (25^c) *of the four mathematical sciences* <term>the Quadrivium: arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</term>. Bandini vol. III p. 389. See also notes at 106, 165, 191.

# Table of manuscript codices.

->I have written 15th-century codices with simple numbers, and distinguished<-
->older ones with **bold** type. I have either omitted books from the 16th and 17th<-
->centuries or labeled them as "recent."<-

Alypius <note>Author of an "Introduction to Music," essential for understanding ancient Greek musical notation.</note> **2. 3.** 44. 80. 143. 184.
Anonymous **1. 2.** 69. 73. 75. 78. 126.
Aristides <note>Likely Aristides Quintilianus, author of a major three-volume work "On Music."</note> **1.** 40. 44. 45. 69. 73. 75. 78. 126. **149^a.** 184. 199.
Aristoxenus, *Harmonics* <note>Aristoxenus was a pupil of Aristotle and a foundational music theorist.</note> **2. 3.** 43. 44. 80. 143. 184. *Rhythmics* **2. 3.** (recent 159.)
Bacchius **1. 2.** 69. 73. 75. 78. 126. 138. **182.**
Barlaam 77. 185. (recent 159.)
Bryennius <note>Manuel Bryennius, the last great Byzantine music theorist.</note> 4. 22. 26. 41. 44. 45. 73. 78. 119. **130. 138. 182. 183. 201.**
Cleonides 76. (recent 12. 13^c. 36. 129. 150. 173.)
Dionysius **1.** 73. 75. 78. 126.
Euclid <note>The famous mathematician; several musical treatises were historically attributed to him.</note> **2. 3** twice. 44. **56.** 76. 80. 171^b. 184.
Pseudo-Euclid <note>Works once thought to be by Euclid but now attributed to others, such as Cleonides.</note> **2. 3** twice. 44. **56.** 143. 156. 171^b. 184.
Neapolitan excerpts 76. 143. parts in 77. 134. (recent 12. 36. parts in 76.)
Gaudentius **3.** 44. 76. 80. 143. 184.
Gregory or Psellus <note>Referring to Michael Psellus, a Byzantine polymath.</note> 25^a.b.c. 74^a. **106. 123. 135. 140. 145. 149^a.** 152. 155. **165. 189. 191. 192. 204.** (recent 16. 207.)
Hagiopolites <note>A significant Byzantine treatise on church music theory.</note> **95.**
Hero, *Pneumatics* <note>Hero of Alexandria, known for his mechanical inventions and hydraulic organs.</note> 188. (recent 11. 13^b. 41^c.d. 85. 105. 157^b. 161. 170. 178.)
Hormasia <note>A term related to the "setting in motion" or arrangement of musical intervals.</note> **149^a? 165.** (recent 16. 65. 134. 171^a.)
Mesomedes <note>A 2nd-century lyric poet and composer; these manuscripts likely contain his hymns.</note> **1.** 69. 73. 75. 78. 126. **182.** (recent 10^a. 50. 116.)
Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, author of a manual on harmonics.</note> Book I: 4. 77. 124. **182.** Books I and II: **2.** 13^b. 43. 44. 138. 184. parts in 77. 164.
Pachymeres <note>George Pachymeres, a Byzantine historian and philosopher.</note> (recent 101—3. 136.)
Pappus, *Introduction* 76. **151. 187.** (recent 12. 36. 42. 118. 159. 173.) see the Rehdiger index page 75, note number 180.
Pappus, *Commentary on Ptolemy* 143. 147^a.
Pediasimus 133. 186. **194.** (recent 88? 158^b. 197.)
Pseudo-Plutarch <note>Refers to the treatise "On Music" formerly attributed to Plutarch.</note> **1.** 26. 27. 28. 29. 30. 69. 75. 77. 109. 146. **148^a. 149^a.** 153. 180. 184.

<summary>This page provides a comprehensive index of ancient and medieval authors whose works on music and mathematics are preserved in a specific collection of manuscripts. It uses a typographic system to distinguish between older (pre-15th century) and more recent copies.</summary>
